Scars in random waves and the 𝐅𝐆𝐅 ⁡ ( 𝟏 𝟐 ) {\bf FGF(\frac{1}{2})} universality class
Louis Gass, Giovanni Peccati, Michele Stecconi

Abstract
. We study the large-domain asymptotics of geometric observables in Berry’s random wave model on ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . We show that, in sharp contrast with the behavior of stationary random fields with absolutely continuous spectral measures, any observable whose fluctuations are asymptotically fully correlated with its second Wiener chaos projection — under suitable non-degeneracy assumptions — belongs to a common universality class governed by a fractional Gaussian field with Hurst index H = 1 − d 2 H=\frac{1-d}{2} . This class also includes the classical stationary Poisson line process in ℝ d \mathbb{R}^{d} . Our findings show that suitable raw observables of Berry’s random wave (such as critical point counts or non-nodal level set volumes) have large-domain fluctuations that become arbitrarily close — in the sense of random tempered distributions — to those generated by a (possibly noisy) Poisson line process. This probabilistic approximation provides evidence that the large-scale filamentary patterns observed in numerical simulations of random waves—often referred to as “scars” or “scarlets” following the numerical investigations of Heller, O’Connor and Gehlen (1987)—may admit a natural probabilistic interpretation. In the second part of our work, we characterize the scaling limit — in a distributional sense — of suitable quadratic transformations of the Radon–Fourier coefficients associated with a large class of stationary fields. We show that random waves are characterized by the property that such a scaling limit is a generalized random field obtained by composing white noise on the affine Grassmannian of lines with a dimension-dependent deterministic operator. As an application of our main results, we derive explicit conditions ensuring that quadratic functionals of pullback monochromatic waves on compact Riemannian manifolds exhibit distributional limits in the fractional Gaussian universality class described above. Keywords : Random fields; Random waves; scars; Poisson line processes; fractional Gaussian fields; Wiener chaos; stochastic geometry; Radon transform AMS Classification : MSC 2020: Primary 60G60, 60F05; Secondary 60D05, 44A12, 60G57.



1.
Introduction

For
d\geq 2
,
Berry’s random wave
(BRW) model on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the unique (in law) centered, isotropic, unit-variance Gaussian field
B=\{B(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
satisfying almost surely the Helmholtz equation

(1.1)

\Delta B(x)+B(x)=0,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Equivalently, the covariance function of
B
is given by its spectral representation

(1.2)

\mathbb{E}\big[B(x)B(y)\big]=\int_{S^{d-1}}e^{i\langle x-y,\theta\rangle}\,\sigma(\differential\theta)=2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu+1)\frac{J_{\nu}(\|x-y\|)}{\|x-y\|^{\nu}},\qquad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\sigma
denotes the uniform probability measure on the unit sphere
S^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
J_{\nu}
is the Bessel function of the first kind of order
\nu=\frac{d}{2}-1
.

The model defined by (
1.1
)—(
1.2
) goes back at least to M.V. Berry’s seminal paper
[
14
]
, where it was proposed as a universal ansatz for the local high-energy behavior of Laplace eigenfunctions on manifolds with ergodic geodesic flow, supported by a detailed semiclassical argument. Commonly referred to as
Berry’s random wave conjecture
, the prediction that high-energy eigenfunctions are locally described by the model (
1.1
)—(
1.2
) remains one of the central open problems in quantum chaos and in the geometry of random fields; see
[
37
,
52
,
1
,
5
,
40
]
for recent advances on the matter. In the last two decades, the geometric properties of BRW and of related models — including
random spherical harmonics
and
arithmetic random waves
— have been studied extensively, often in connection with the
variance cancellation phenomena
first uncovered in
[
15
]
, and with outstanding open problems in differential geometry, like e.g.
Yau’s conjecture

[
97
,
60
]
. We refer to
[
96
]
for a recent survey of this line of research, and to
[
25
,
77
,
86
,
13
,
67
,
66
,
10
,
71
,
92
,
63
,
95
,
57
,
31
,
70
,
82
]
for a selection of representative contributions.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

(e)

(f)

Figure 1
.
First row ((a)—(c)): heatmap of the BRW field
B
over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
, for
R=100,200,400
. Second row ((d)—(f)): heatmaps over the same windows of the Bargmann-Fock field
F
(with covariance
\mathbb{E}[F(0)F(x)]\asymp e^{-\|x\|^{2}}
).

One striking empirical feature of the two-dimensional BRW model is that numerical simulations over large domains display characteristic quasi-linear filamentary patterns — typically described as
scars
or
scarlets
— that persist across scales and roughly correspond to chains of narrow high-level excursions of alternating sign. Such patterns are completely absent from simulations of other natural isotropic fields (such as the
Bargmann–Fock field
, see Figure
1
) and are clearly revealed by observables such as the square of Berry’s field (Figure
2
-(a)), critical point configurations (Figure
2
-(b)), non-zero level sets (Figure
2
-(c)), and non-zero excursion sets (Figure
2
-(d)). By contrast, they are essentially invisible at the level of nodal domains and nodal lines; see Figure
3
.

Remark 1.0.1
.

From a conceptual viewpoint, one may distinguish between two aspects of the phenomenon: the geometry of the filamentary structures themselves, and the oscillatory patterns carried by them. The first aspect leads to what one might informally call
white scars
, corresponding to observables
1
1

1

For the rest of the paper we use the expression “observable” to denote any distribution-valued functional of a given random function; see Section
2.1
.
that primarily encode the geometry of the filaments. The second aspect leads to observables that may be viewed as
coloured scars
, in the sense that they retain information about the oscillatory profile of the wave along the filaments.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 2
.
Several observables of BRW over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
: Figures (a) and (b) correspond to
R=400
and are associated with Fig.
1
-(c), whereas Figures (c) and (d) correspond to
R=200
and to Fig.
1
-(b).

The appearance of scars in simulations of the BRW model was first pointed out in the seminal paper of Gehlen, Heller and O’Connor
[
78
]
, where a mechanism for their formation was proposed, at a physics level of rigor, based on Gabor-type local filters
[
35
]
and the approximate invariance of their moduli under the Laplace propagator; see also
[
47
, Chapter 5]
and
[
48
, Chapter 23]
, as well as
[
18
]
for related experimental evidence. Following the work of Gehlen, Heller and O’Connor, the appearance of scars in simulations of the BRW model has remained a recurring yet elusive topic. Striking visualisations of filamentary patterns in random waves were featured, for instance, on the cover of the
Notices of the AMS
in January 2008
[
4
]
(see also the associated paper
[
9
]
), and were a central theme of a workshop at the American Institute of Mathematics in 2009 (see
[
3
]
for a particularly vivid report). A. Barnett’s webpage
[
8
]
provides a rich collection of numerical experiments illustrating these phenomena. More recently, Tacy
[
93
]
showed that, while coefficients of X-ray transforms associated with BRW do not exhibit anomalous concentration, the
L^{2}
norms of certain
semiclassical localizers
—closely related in spirit to the Gabor-type filters evoked above—do display concentration properties consistent with the presence of filamentary structures. On a different front, the work of Beliaev and Hegde
[
12
,
44
]
establishes that high maxima of a broad class of isotropic fields, including BRW, exhibit Poissonian scaling limits, suggesting that the mechanism underlying scars should instead be sought within the bulk of critical values.

To the best of our knowledge, however, no rigorous mathematical framework—let alone a formal definition of
scar
for random waves—capturing the visual patterns first observed in
[
78
]
has been attempted so far.

Figure 3
.
Nodal domains and nodal lines of BRW over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
, for
R=200
. They both correspond to the realization in Fig.
1
-(b).

The aim of this paper is to investigate the universal probabilistic mechanisms underlying the geometry and oscillatory structure of observables that empirically reveal the filamentary patterns exhibited by Berry’s random wave.

Our main findings may be summarized as follows.

(1)

In Sections
2.3
and
2.4
, we identify a
universality class
containing a broad family of observables associated with the BRW
B
, including the square of the field, suitably normalized critical point counts, level-set observables, and excursion-type observables revealing the filamentary structures visible in Figure
2
. As proved in Theorem
2.4.1
and Proposition
2.4.3
, the large-scale behavior of every observable in this class is governed by the
fractional Gaussian field
of index
\frac{1}{2}
(whose law is denoted by
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}
; see
[
59
]
, as well as Sections
2.2
and
3.4
below). More precisely, every member of the class satisfies both a law of large numbers under a classical volume-type normalization and a non-trivial fluctuation theorem under the normalization dictated by the self-similarity of the limiting field:

R^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\bar{X}(B(R\cdot))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2},

where
\bar{X}
denotes the centered observable, the convergence holds in the sense of random tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and the corresponding covariance structures converge as well. A common feature of all observables in the class is that, in the large-domain limit, their fluctuations are asymptotically dominated by their
second Wiener chaos projection
(see
[
74
, Chapter 2]
), which completely determines their limiting behavior. As discussed in Remark
2.4.2
, these findings are consistent with earlier second-order results for quadratic functionals of planar random waves
[
62
,
9
]
. Finally, the above results are applied in Section
2.4.3
to characterize the
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}
scaling limits of quadratic functionals of
pullback monochromatic random waves
on compact manifolds, as defined for instance in
[
25
,
27
,
98
]
.

(2)

As discussed in Section
2.3
, we show that a defining feature of the above universality class is that it also contains the random tempered distribution canonically generated by a
stationary Poisson process

\eta
on the
affine line Grassmannian

\mathbb{A}_{d}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Section
5.1
). In particular, if

(1.3)

Y=\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta

denotes the corresponding
Poisson line field
, that is, the distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
obtained by integrating against the affine lines in the support of
\eta
(with
\mathcal{R}^{*}
indicating the adjoint of the classical
X-ray transform
; see Definition
5.2.3
), then

R^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\bar{Y}(R\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2},

see Theorem
2.3.4
-
(a)
. We further establish in Theorem
2.3.4
-
(b)
that the above universality class is rich enough to permit a direct comparison between BRW observables and (possibly noisy) Poisson line process observables, in the sense that the finite-dimensional distributions of their large-domain fluctuations can be made arbitrarily close. As discussed in Remark
2.4.4
-(i), these results suggest that the large-scale filamentary patterns observed in numerical simulations of random waves may admit a natural probabilistic interpretation in terms of Poisson line processes, after averaging out the oscillatory profile carried by the wave (see also Remark
1.0.1
).

(3)

We will show that the phenomena described in Points
(1)
and
(2)
are by no means universal among stationary random fields. As demonstrated in Theorem
2.4.7
, for a broad class of stationary Gaussian fields whose spectral measure admits a density, the scaling limits of the corresponding observables are instead described by white noise. This sharp contrast between the two universality classes is consistent with the markedly different large-scale patterns observed in the simulations displayed in Figure
1
.

(4)

Let
f
be a smooth stationary Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, consider the random distribution generated by the Wick square of
f
through the relation

(1.4)

\varphi\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,:\!f(Rx)^{2}\!:\,\differential x,\quad R>0,

and notice that, when
f=B
, the observable in (
1.4
) (with
R=1
) is the most basic representative of the universality class described at Point
(1)
. A key result established in Section
5.7.3
is that the operator in (
1.4
) admits a natural representation as the restriction to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of a random distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
of the form

(1.5)

\Sigma_{R}(f):=\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}\!:,

where
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
is a suitable fractional Laplacian, and
:\!|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}\!:
is the formal Wick square of the distribution induced by the one-dimensional
Radon–Fourier coefficients
associated with
f
.
We call
\Sigma_{R}(f)
the
scar field
of
f
at scale
R
.

As demonstrated in Theorem
2.4.9
(and discussed in greater detail in Sections
5
and
6
), one of the main achievements of our work is a full characterization of the scaling limit of the distribution
\Sigma_{R}(f)
, depending on both the dimension
d\geq 2
and the nature of the spectral measure associated with the law of
f
. We will see that, in the special case
f=B
, the limiting object admits a description in terms of the composition of a white noise on the affine line Grassmannian
\mathbb{A}_{d}
with a dimension-dependent deterministic operator. In this sense, the limiting random distribution provides a natural probabilistic counterpart of the
coloured scar-like patterns
informally discussed in Remark
1.0.1
.

Remark 1.0.2
.

(i)

Our results reveal an interesting dimensional dichotomy. The scaling limits associated with white scars are universal across dimensions and always belong to the
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
universality class. By contrast, once frequency information is retained, the limiting objects become dimension-dependent; see also Remark
5.7.6
and
section

5.8
. We will see in Section
2.4.2
and Sections
5
–
6
that in dimension
d=2
, the limiting frequency distribution is concentrated at the two monochromatic frequencies
\pm 1
, whereas in dimensions
d\geq 3
it is described by a non-trivial density depending explicitly on the dimension. In this sense, white scars exhibit a universal geometric behaviour, whereas coloured scars retain a finer signature of the ambient dimension. We stress that the terminology of “white” and “coloured” scars is only intended to informally emphasize the distinction between geometric information and oscillatory information. The mathematical content of the paper is entirely formulated in terms of the universality classes and scaling limits discussed above.

(ii)

The filamentary patterns in random waves discussed above are sometimes compared with the phenomenon of
quantum scars
observed in eigenfunctions of classically chaotic systems (see e.g.
[
78
,
47
,
48
,
18
]
). Beginning with Heller’s seminal work
[
46
]
, the literature on quantum scars has largely developed around their relation to unstable periodic orbits; see, for instance,
[
55
,
69
,
85
]
. The present paper does not address the relation between this semiclassical notion of scar and the probabilistic phenomena uncovered by our findings.

(iii)

Approximately one week after the first version of the present paper was posted on the arXiv, the independent work of Beliaev and Hegde
[
11
]
appeared. Among other results, it establishes a particular case of the
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}
universality phenomenon described at Point
(1)
above. Some further findings from
[
11
]
, and their relation to the present work, are discussed in Remark
2.4.4
-(i).

From now on, every random object is defined on a common probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{E},\mathbb{P})
, with
\mathbb{E}
indicating an expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
.

The next section contains a discussion of the main results established
in our work, and implicitly serves as a plan for the rest of the paper

2.
Formal description of main results

2.1.
Some conventions

All objects discussed below are presented in more detail in Section
3
. For
d\geq 2
, we denote by
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the class of Schwartz functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whereas the class of tempered distributions (the topological dual of
\mathcal{S}
) will be written
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We endow
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
with the Borel
\sigma
-field
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})
generated by the weak-
\star
topology. Given
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
, we will use interchangeably the notations

(2.1)

X(\varphi)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\langle X,\varphi\rangle,

both denoting the canonical duality pairing between

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{S}
.

In this paper, we use the term
random distribution
to indicate a
\left(\mathcal{S}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})\right)
-valued random element. The existence of such objects is guaranteed by the Bochner-Minlos theorem (see
[
16
, Corollary 2.1]
, as well as
theorem

3.3.1
below). As anticipated, when a random distribution
X
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-field
\sigma(Z)
generated by a random field
Z=\{Z(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, we will say that
X
is an
observable
of
Z
. As usual, a sequence of random distributions
\{X_{n}:n\geq 1\}
is said to
converge in law
to a random distribution
X
, written
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
, if

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X_{n})]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}[\Phi(X)],\quad n\to\infty,

for every
\Phi:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and continuous. According e.g. to
[
16
, Corollary 2.4]
and to the classical results by Fernique
[
36
]
(see
theorem

3.3.3
below), one has that
X_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}X
if and only if
X_{n}(\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}X(\varphi)
for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
, where, here,
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}
indicates convergence in distribution of random variables. Given
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
, we write
\varphi_{R}(\cdot):=\varphi(\frac{\cdot}{R})
to indicate the mapping

(2.2)

x\mapsto\varphi_{R}(x):=\varphi\left(\frac{x}{R}\right),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Given
t>0
, we will write
\Pi_{t}
to denote a
homogeneous Poisson point process
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
t
(see Section
3.5
for definitions), that we identify with a random point measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and therefore with the random distribution
\Pi_{t}=\{\Pi_{t}(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}
, where
\Pi_{t}(\varphi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\Pi_{t}(\differential x)
(see Remark
3.5.3
). We will denote by
\Pi_{0}
the
trivial null measure
, with total mass equal to zero. Observe the following relation, that one can e.g. deduce from the general results of
[
81
, Section 7.3]
: for every
q\geq 1
, the
q
th
cumulant
of
\Pi_{t}(\varphi)
is given by

(2.3)

\kappa_{q}(\Pi_{t}(\varphi))=t\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)^{q}\differential x,\quad\mbox{and therefore}\quad\kappa_{q}\left(\Pi_{t}\left(\varphi_{R}\right)\right)=tR^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)^{q}\differential x.

Throughout the paper, and unless explicitly specified, the symbol
\|\cdot\|
denotes the Euclidean norm on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Given two random vectors
U,V
with values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the
convex distance
between the laws of
U
and
V
is defined as

(2.4)

d_{\rm conv}(U,V):=\sup_{C}\Big|\,\mathbb{P}[U\in C]-\mathbb{P}[V\in C]\,\Big|,

where the supremum is taken over all convex sets
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
. See, e.g.,
[
73
]
, and the references therein, for a recent discussion of the properties of
d_{\rm conv}
.

Given numerical sequences
a_{n},b_{n}
, we write (i)
a_{n}\simeq b_{n}
, and (ii)
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
to indicate, respectively, that (i)
b_{n}\neq 0
and
a_{n}/b_{n}\to 1
, and (ii) there exist constants
0<c<C
such that
c\,b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq C\,b_{n}
.

2.2.
The scaling limit: fractional Gaussian fields of index
\frac{1}{2}

For
d\geq 2
, the
Fractional Gaussian Field
of index
s=\frac{1}{2}
(in symbols,
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) is the unique (in law) centered Gaussian random distribution
h=\{h(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}
with covariance given by

(2.5)

\mathbb{E}[h(\varphi)h(\psi)]=C(d)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\varphi(x)\psi(y)}{\|x-y\|^{d-1}}\differential x\differential y,\quad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S},

with the constant
C(d)
defined as

(2.6)

C\!\left(d\right):=\frac{1}{2}\,\pi^{-(d+1)/2}\,\Gamma\!\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)=\frac{1}{\pi\,\Omega_{d-2}},

and where
\Omega_{d-2}
denotes the surface measure of the unit sphere
S^{d-2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. In what follows, we will write

h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

to indicate the fact that
h
is a centered random Gaussian distribution with covariance (
2.5
). As explained in full detail in Section
3.4
, a field
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a member of the large family of
fractional Gaussian fields
categorized in the influential survey
[
59
]
(containing both the white noise and the Gaussian Free Field as special cases — see also
[
28
]
); as such,
h
admits the distributional representation

h=(-\Delta)^{-1/4}\,\mathbb{W},

where
\mathbb{W}
indicates a standard white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\Delta
is the usual Laplacian. It is not difficult to deduce from (
2.5
) that, if
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
h
is a
self-similar random distribution
with negative
Hurst index

H=\frac{1-d}{2}
, from which one deduces the crucial relation

(2.7)

h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{=}}\left\{\frac{h\left(\varphi_{R}\right)}{R^{(d+1)/2}}:\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\right\},\quad R>0,

where we have adopted the notation (
2.2
), and the equality in law is in the sense of random distributions.

One fundamental fact exploited in the present paper (that, to the best of our knowledge, has been overlooked in the probabilistic literature so far) is that fractional Gaussian fields of index 1/2 can be naturally related to linear structures through the use of
Blaschke–Petkantschin formulae
, such as the ones proved in
[
65
, formula (27)]
(see also
[
54
, Proposition 3.2]
, or
[
68
, formula (2)]
), yielding that, for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}
,

(2.8)

C(d)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\varphi(x)\psi(y)}{\|x-y\|^{d-1}}\differential x\differential y=\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell)\mathcal{R}\psi(\ell)\,\mu(\differential\ell),

where
C(d)
is given in (
2.6
),
\mathbb{A}_{d}
indicates the
affine Grassmannian
of affine lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Section
5.1
),
\mu
is its
invariant measure
2
2

2

The invariant measure on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
is uniquely defined up to a multiplicative constant. In this paper, for every
d
, such a constant will be implicitly chosen in such a way that the identity (
2.8
) holds exactly. See e.g.
[
54
, Section 5.1]
for a discussion of alternative normalization choices.
and

(2.9)

\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell):=\int_{\ell}\varphi(x)\mathcal{H}^{1}(dx),\quad\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{d},

indicates the usual
X-ray transform
of
\varphi
. See Section
5.2
for definitions, as well as Proposition
5.4.1
for a discussion of the fact that (
2.9
) yields the following distributional representation: if
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
h=\mathcal{R}^{*}\widetilde{\mathbb{W}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathbb{W}}
indicates a white noise on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
with intensity measure
\mu
(see (
2.6
)), and
\mathcal{R}^{*}
is the
adjoint X-ray transform
operator; see also Figure
4
.

Figure 4
.
On the left, a simulation of the mapping
x\mapsto h(\varphi_{x})
,
x\in[-100,100]^{2}
, where
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and where
\varphi_{x}
is an approximation of the Dirac mass at
x
, compactly supported in the ball
B(x,\varrho)
, with
\varrho=0.07
. On the right, a simulation of the mapping
x\mapsto W(\varphi_{x})
,
x\in[-100,100]^{2}
, where
W
is a white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
\varphi_{x}
is the same Dirac mass approximation.

2.3.
The
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
universality class

Fractional Gaussian fields, including those of the
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
type, have appeared in recent years as scaling limits in a variety of frameworks, such as sandpile models
[
29
]
, local statistics of Riesz gases
[
83
]
, Boolean models with random radii
[
17
]
, and occupation functionals of branching processes
[
20
,
21
]
. In this paper, we instead focus on families of random distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying the following three properties: (a) their scaling limit under homothety is
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; (b) their scaling exponent is consistent with the self-similarity relation (
2.7
); and (c) they satisfy a law of large numbers under the usual volume-type normalization. We formalize this in the next definition through the notion of a
universality class
.

Definition 2.3.1

(The Universality Class)
.

Fix
d\geq 2
, and let
X=\{X(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}
be a random distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
X(\varphi)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P})
, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
. We say that
X

belongs to the universality class of

{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, written

X\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\},

if there exist
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta^{2}>0
such that

(a)

as
R\to\infty
,

(2.10)

\mathbb{E}[X(\varphi_{R})]=\alpha R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x+o(R^{(d+1)/2}),\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{S},

where we have adopted the notation (
2.2
), and the constants implicit in the
o(\cdot)
notation might depend on
\varphi
;

(b)

again as
R\to\infty
,

(2.11)

\widetilde{X}_{R}(\varphi):=\frac{X(\varphi_{R})-\alpha R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x}{\beta R^{(d+1)/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}h(\varphi),\quad\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{S},

where
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Note that the remainder term in
(a)
is chosen so as to be negligible at the fluctuation scale appearing in
(b)
and that, in most examples considered in this paper, the
o(R^{(d+1)/2})
term will actually be equal to zero. As a consequence of tightness and of the (already recalled) results from
[
36
,
16
]
, one verifies immediately that, if
X\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
, then

(2.12)

\frac{X(\varphi_{R})}{R^{d}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x,\quad R\to\infty,

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}}}{{\longrightarrow}}
indicates convergence in probability, and

(2.13)

\left\{\widetilde{X}_{R}(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad R\to\infty,

where we have used the notation (
2.11
), and the convergence is in the sense of random distributions. Also, one easily deduces from (
2.3
) that, if
X\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
and if
\Pi_{t}
is a homogeneous Poisson process with intensity
t>0
independent of
X
, then

X+\tau\Pi_{t}\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\},\quad\forall\tau\in\mathbb{R},

that is,
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
universality classes are
stable
with respect to the addition of an independent Poisson noise.
3
3

3

Plainly, the stability of
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
classes is still verified if the Poisson random measure
\Pi_{t}
is replaced by any independent random distribution
M(\cdot)
such that the quantity
M(\varphi_{R})
has expectation and variance that scale, respectively, as in (
2.10
) and as
o(R^{d+1})
. For instance, one can take
M(\varphi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,\differential x
.

Remark 2.3.2
.

We emphasize that Definition
2.3.1
does not require the covariance
structure of
\varphi\mapsto X(\varphi_{R})
to converge to that of
h
.
While such a covariance convergence is satisfied by all examples
considered in the present paper, we do not regard it as a defining
feature of a universality class, whose role is primarily to encode
common scaling limits. In fact, all random distributions

X\in{\bf UC}\{\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}
studied below satisfy

X(\varphi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
, and
moreover

(2.14)

\frac{1}{\beta^{2}R^{d+1}}{\bf Cov}\left(X(\varphi_{R}),X(\psi_{R})\right)\sim{\bf Cov}\left(\widetilde{X}_{R}(\varphi),\widetilde{X}_{R}(\psi)\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}[h(\varphi)h(\psi)],\quad R\to\infty,

where
{\bf Cov}
stands for the usual covariance between random
variables. Likewise, although Definition
2.3.1
also includes a first-order
condition describing the asymptotic behavior of the mean, the
terminology
{\bf UC}\{\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}
is motivated by the
fluctuation theory: the normalization
R^{-(d+1)/2}
appearing in
Definition
2.3.1
is dictated by the self-similarity properties of

\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and every member of the class exhibits fluctuations
governed by this limiting field. In this sense, the defining feature of
the class is the behaviour of its fluctuations rather than its
first-order asymptotics.

As already evoked in Section
1
, the universality classes introduced in Definition
2.3.1
contain some of the canonical observables associated with a family of classical geometric models, namely the class of
stationary Poisson line processes
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. To define such a model for
d\geq 2
(see Section
3.5
for a full discussion, as well as
[
51
, Chapter 4]
or
[
88
, Section 4.4]
for standard presentations of these objects), we consider as before the affine Grassmannian of lines
\mathbb{A}_{d}
endowed with its invariant measure
\mu
; then, a
stationary Poisson line process
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
t>0
is a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
with control measure given by
t\cdot\mu
. In what follows, we write
\eta_{t}\sim{\rm PLP}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to indicate that
\eta_{t}
is a point process on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
distributed as a stationary Poisson line process with intensity
t
. The reader is referred to Figure
5
for a visualization of a Poisson line process restricted to a sequence of growing domains.

Figure 5
.
One realization of a stationary Poisson line process with intensity
t=0.3
, over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
, for
R=100,200,400
.

Remark 2.3.3
.

It is important to notice that Poisson line processes are
locally finite
objects in the sense that, if
\eta_{t}\sim{\rm PLP}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then, with probability one, every bounded set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
intersects only finitely many lines in the support of
\eta_{t}
(this is a direct consequence of the fact that
\mu
is locally finite). In this respect, Poisson line processes of the kind considered in this paper are substantially different from the
scale-invariant Poisson stick soups
recently introduced in
[
94
]
, whose support is instead dense in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Given
\eta_{t}\sim{\rm PLP}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we introduce the associated
Poisson Line Field
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is defined as the random distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

(2.15)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t}=\{\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t}(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}

where

(2.16)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t}(\varphi):=\sum_{\ell\in\eta_{t}}\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell),

\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell)
is given in (
2.9
),
\mathcal{R}^{*}
is the adjoint of
\mathcal{R}
, and
\ell\in\eta_{t}
indicates that
\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
is in the support of
\eta_{t}
.

The next statement (that, to the best of our knowledge, is new) shows that
\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t}
belongs to the
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
universality class, and that the raw
4
4

4

That is, without centering and rescaling.
fluctuations of any element of this class can be made arbitrarily close, in the sense of the discrepancy of finite-dimensional marginals evaluated in the convex distance, to those of a random distribution of the form

\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t_{1}}\pm\Pi_{t_{2}},

for suitable
t_{1}>0
and
t_{2}\geq 0
, with
\Pi_{t_{2}}
an independent Poisson point process. See Section
7.1
for a proof.

Theorem 2.3.4
.

(a)

For every
d\geq 2
and every
t>0
, one has that

\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t}\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\},

with
\alpha=t\gamma
and
\beta^{2}=C(d)^{-1}t
, where
\gamma=\gamma(d)
is a constant uniquely depending on
d
, and
C(d)
has been defined in (
2.6
).

(b)

Let
Y\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
. Then, there exists a unique
5
5

5

Except for when
t_{2}=0
, in which case the choice of
\varepsilon
is immaterial.
triple

(t_{1},\varepsilon,t_{2})\in(0,\infty)\times\{-1,1\}\times[0,\infty)

such that, for all
m\geq 1
and all
\varphi^{(1)},...,\varphi^{(m)}\in\mathcal{S}
such that the covariance matrix of
(h(\varphi^{(1)}),...,h(\varphi^{(m)}))
is non-singular, one has that

(2.17)

d_{\rm conv}\left({\bf Y}_{R},{\bf L}_{R}\right)\longrightarrow 0,\quad R\to\infty,

where

\displaystyle{\bf Y}_{R}:=\left(Y(\varphi^{(1)}_{R}),\ldots,Y(\varphi^{(m)}_{R})\right),

\displaystyle{\bf L}_{R}:=\left(\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t_{1}}(\varphi^{(1)}_{R})+\varepsilon\,\Pi_{t_{2}}(\varphi_{R}^{(1)}),\ldots,\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t_{1}}(\varphi^{(m)}_{R})+\varepsilon\,\Pi_{t_{2}}(\varphi_{R}^{(m)})\right),

the random distributions
\mathcal{R}^{*}\eta_{t_{1}}
and
\Pi_{t_{2}}
are stochastically independent, and we have adopted the notation (
2.2
) and (
2.4
).

Remark 2.3.5
.

We stress that the families of probability laws on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
associated with the classes
\{{\bf Y}_{R}:R>0\}
and
\{{\bf L}_{R}:R>0\}
appearing in (
2.17
) are
not
tight. See Davydov and Rotar
[
30
]
for a general reference on the topic of measuring the proximity of non-tight families of probability distributions on metric spaces.

We are now in a position to state the main results of the paper.

2.4.
Main results

2.4.1.
Scaling limit of BRW observables, and comparison with other fields

Fix
d\geq 2
, and let
B=\{B(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
be the BRW model defined in (
1.1
)–(
1.2
). The next statement (proved in Section
4.2
) is one of the main achievements of our work. Recall that an
observable
of
B
is a
\sigma(B)
-measurable random distribution, where
\sigma(B)
is the
\sigma
-field generated by
B
.

Theorem 2.4.1
.

Let
X=\{X(\varphi):\varphi\in\mathcal{S}\}
be an observable of
B
such that, for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
,
X(\varphi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
and (
2.10
) is verified for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, assume that there exist real constants
\{c_{j}:j=0,1,...,k\}
such that
\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\neq 0
and

(2.18)

\displaystyle X(\varphi_{R})-\mathbb{E}[X(\varphi_{R})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{R}(x)\left(\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}-1\right)\differential x+T_{R},

where we used the notation (
2.2
) and

(2.19)

{\bf Var}(T_{R})=o(R^{d+1}),\,\,\mbox{as}\,\,R\to\infty.

Then,
X\in{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
, with
\beta=C(d)^{-1/2}\,K_{d}\,|\sum c_{j}|
, where
C(d)
is defined in (
2.6
), and

(2.20)

K_{d}:=\frac{2^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}},\quad d\geq 2.

We note that Theorem
2.4.1
yields that the random distribution generated by the density
x\mapsto B^{2}(x)
— corresponding to the choice
k=0
,
c_{0}=1
, and
T_{R}=0
, and illustrated in Figure
2
-(a) — belongs to the universality class

{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

The forthcoming Proposition
proposition

2.4.3
shows that the same conclusion holds for the three remaining observables displayed in Figure
2
. For a smooth function
f
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the following quantities:

–

X_{1}(f,u,\varphi):=\sum_{x\in{\bf CP}(u)}\varphi(x)
, where
{\bf CP}(u)
indicates the collection of all critical points
x
of
f
such that
f(x)>u
;

–

X_{2}(f,u,\varphi):=\sum_{x\in{\bf M}(u)}\varphi(x)
, where
{\bf M}(u)
indicates the collection of all local maxima
x
of
f
such that
f(x)>u
;

–

X_{3}(f,u,\varphi):=\int_{f^{-1}(u)}\,\varphi(x)\,\differential\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(x)
, where
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure;

–

X_{4}(f,u,\varphi):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\bf 1}_{\{f(x)>u\}}\,\varphi(x)\,\differential x
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

The fact that, after the appropriate normalization, the asymptotic variance of

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{R}(x)(B(x)^{2}-1)\,\differential x

coincides with the covariance functional of
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
can already be deduced from
[
9
, formula (16)]
(see also
[
32
, Section III.B]
). Moreover, a version of Theorem
2.4.1
in the case where
d=2
,
k=0
and
\varphi
runs over the class of indicators of bounded convex sets with non-vanishing Gauss curvature can be inferred from
[
62
, Example 4.10]
, a result that is partially based on the one-dimensional spectral CLTs proved in
[
61
]
.

Proposition 2.4.3
.

Let the above notation prevail. For
\ell=1,...,4
, there exist finite subsets
E_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}
such that
0\in E_{\ell}
and, for
u\in\mathbb{R}\setminus E_{\ell}
, the observable
X_{\ell}(B,u,\cdot)
is a member of the class
{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
. For these observables, the convergence relations (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) as well as the conclusion of Theorem
2.3.4
-
(b)
continue to hold if one replaces the test functions
\varphi,\varphi^{(i)}\in\mathcal{S}
with indicators of bounded convex sets having a
C^{\infty}
boundary with non-vanishing Gauss curvature; in this case, one has to interpret
h(\varphi)
and
(h(\varphi^{(1)}),....,h(\varphi^{(m)}))
as centered Gaussian elements whose variance/covariance structures are given by (
2.5
).

Remark 2.4.4
.

The combination of Theorem
2.4.1
, Proposition
2.4.3
and Theorem
2.3.4
-
(b)
shows that, in the large-domain limit, the fluctuations of the raw observables generated by Berry’s random wave and those generated by a homogeneous (possibly noisy) Poisson line process become asymptotically indistinguishable, both as random tempered distributions and, via their measure-valued extensions, when evaluated on indicators of smooth convex sets. An interesting question is whether asymptotic indistinguishability, in the above sense, fully accounts for the filamentary patterns observed in numerical simulations. The subtlety of this issue is illustrated by the recent work of Beliaev and Hegde
[
11
, Theorem 2.4 and Remark 2.6]
. They consider a stationary Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose spectral density satisfies

\rho(\xi)\asymp\frac{\mathbf{1}_{\{|\xi|<1\}}}{|\xi|},

and prove that the fluctuations of the excursion areas (at any level
u
) belong to the
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
universality class. Nevertheless, numerical simulations of this field do not exhibit the striking scar-like filamentary patterns observed for Berry’s random wave; see Figure
6
. It is plausible that Berry’s random wave and homogeneous Poisson line processes are asymptotically closer under a stronger mode of convergence, one that would allow for a formal statistical detection of scarred patterns.

Figure 6
.
The singular random field from
[
11
]
, as evoked in Remark
2.4.4
-(i), over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
, for
R=100,700
.

We stress that the conclusions of Proposition
2.4.3
hold at a
fixed
level
u
, suggesting, in particular, that one mechanism contributing to the formation of scarred patterns is already present in the bulk of critical points and maxima. Figure
7
provides an illustration of this fact.

Figure 7
.
Simulation of the local maxima of BRW above the threshold
u=2
over the window
[-R,R]^{2}
, for
R=1000,2500
.

Relation (
2.18
) implies that the random variable
X(\varphi_{R})
is asymptotically dominated by its projection onto the
second Wiener chaos
associated with
B
(see
[
74
, Chapter 2]
for definitions). In particular, the conclusion of Theorem
2.4.1

does not apply
to observables exhibiting a form of anti-symmetry or of
Berry’s cancellation
, such as those studied for instance in
[
15
,
95
,
71
,
82
,
70
,
92
,
63
,
64
,
57
,
41
]
, which are typically fully correlated with their projection onto the
fourth
Wiener chaos. This is consistent with the fact that, according for example to
[
72
,
82
,
70
]
, the scaling limit of the centered and rescaled nodal length associated with
B
(corresponding to
X_{3}(B,0,\cdot)
) is given by white noise (see
[
70
, Proposition 1.3]
for a precise statement), as well as with the empirical observation that scars are not detected by nodal patterns; see Figure
3
. A similar observation can be made for the point process of all critical points of BRW, formally corresponding to the functional
X_{1}(B,-\infty,\cdot)
(see
[
41
, Sec. 1.3]
); see also
[
38
, Section 2.6]
. Note that the above arguments straightforwardly justify the fact that, in Theorem
2.4.1
, one has that
0\in E_{1}\cap E_{3}\cap E_{4}
. The fact that, also,
0\in E_{2}
can be deduced from a standard homogeneity argument that is left to the reader.

It is instructive to reinterpret Theorem
2.4.1
(case
k=0
) in a high-energy regime, using the rescaled field

B_{R}(x):=B(Rx),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

which satisfies the equation

\Delta B_{R}(x)+R^{2}B_{R}(x)=0.

In this case, Theorem
2.4.1
implies the statements

(2.21)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}B_{R}^{2}(x)\,\varphi(x)\,dx\xrightarrow{\mathbb{P}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,dx,\qquad R\to\infty,

and

(2.22)

c\,R^{(d-1)/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big(B_{R}^{2}(x)-1\big)\,\varphi(x)\,dx\xrightarrow{\rm law}h(\varphi),

for some
c>0
and
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. These relations show that, although
B_{R}^{2}
equidistributes in the high-energy limit
R\to\infty
, the natural scale at which any large-scale filamentary behaviour may emerge is that of second-order fluctuations, namely
R^{-(d-1)/2}
.

The techniques developed in Section
4
will also allow us to determine a precise asymptotic relation for the variance of
X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})
, when
u,R
tend simultaneously to infinity. To state such a result, we define
X_{1}^{(2)}(B,u,\varphi_{R})
to be the projection on the second Wiener chaos of
X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})
(see Section
4.3.2
for more details). To simplify the discussion, we also introduce the shorthand notation

(2.23)

\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\varphi(x)\varphi(y)}{\|x-y\|^{d-1}}\differential x\differential y,

and observe that, according to (
2.5
) and using (
4.2
) one has that, if
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
, then
{\bf Var}(h(\varphi))=C(d)\,\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L}
.

Proposition 2.4.5
.

Let the above notation prevail, fix
d\geq 2
, and let
\varphi
be either an element of
\mathcal{S}
or a finite linear combination of indicators of bounded convex sets with a
C^{\infty}
boundary and non-vanishing Gauss curvature.
Assume that
u,R\to\infty
, in such a way that, for some positive constant
c
,

(2.24)

|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})]|\geq c\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x\right|.

Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(X_{1}^{(2)}(B,u,\varphi_{R})\right)+\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})],

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}}{16d^{d}\pi^{d+2}}\,R^{d+1}u^{2d+2}e^{-u^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}+\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}R^{d}u^{d-1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)^{2}\differential x.

Remark 2.4.6
.

By virtue of the forthcoming Lemma
4.4.2
, Condition
(
2.24
) is equivalent to

\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}R^{d}u^{d-1}e^{-u^{2}/2}\geq c.

Under this assumption, three asymptotic regimes emerge for the number
of critical points above a growing level
u
.

(i)
If
u
grows sufficiently slowly, namely

\frac{1}{u^{d+3}}e^{u^{2}/2}=o(R),

then the second Wiener chaos projection dominates. In this regime, one
can adapt the proof of Theorem
2.4.1
to show that the

\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}
scaling limit remains in force, namely

\sqrt{C(d)}\frac{X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})]}{\sqrt{H(R,u)}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}h(\varphi),\qquad h\sim\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
H(R,u):=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)^{2}}{16d^{d}\pi^{d+2}}\,R^{d+1}u^{2d+2}e^{-u^{2}}
.

(ii)
If
u
grows sufficiently fast, namely

R=o\!\left(\frac{1}{u^{d+3}}e^{u^{2}/2}\right),

then the variance is expected to exhibit the standard Poisson scaling.
In view of the estimates provided by Lemmas
4.4.5
,
4.4.6

and
4.4.7
, it is plausible that the techniques developed in

[
7
,
12
]
could be used to show that the point
process of critical points above level
u
becomes asymptotically
indistinguishable from a Poisson point process with intensity

\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}R^{d}u^{d-1}e^{-u^{2}/2}.

(iii)
In the intermediate regime

R\asymp\frac{1}{u^{d+3}}e^{u^{2}/2},

the two mechanisms appear to contribute at the same scale. This
suggests the conjecture that the limiting critical point measure should
exhibit a mixed behavior, combining a Poisson component and an
independent
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
component.

The forthcoming Theorem
2.4.7
demonstrates that, for stationary fields with integrable correlation function, the natural scaling limit of the observables studied in Proposition
2.4.3
is white noise. In particular, this result applies to stationary random fields whose spectral density is supported in an annulus. Figure
8
provides an
illustration of this phenomenon.

Figure 8
.
Heatmap on the window
[-R,R]^{2}
of a stationary Gaussian random field
A=\{A(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
with a smooth spectral measure supported on the annulus
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:1.9\leq\|x\|\leq 2.1\}
, for
R=100,200,400
.

Theorem 2.4.7
.

Let
d\geq 2
and
f
be a stationary Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
centered with unit-variance, such that
\nabla^{j}f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
0\leq j\leq 4
. Then for
\ell=1,\ldots,4

(i)

As
R\to\infty
and for fixed
u
, one has that, for some constants
C_{\ell},C^{\prime}_{\ell}>0
depending on
u
, the following asymptotic relations:

\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}(f,u,\varphi_{R})]\simeq C_{\ell}R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(X_{\ell}(f,u,\varphi_{R})\right)\simeq C^{\prime}_{\ell}R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x.

(ii)

As
R\to\infty
and for fixed
u
, the random tempered distribution

\varphi\mapsto\frac{X_{\ell}(f,u,\varphi)-\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}(f,u,\varphi)]}{\sqrt{C^{\prime}_{\ell}}R^{d/2}}

converges in law towards a standard white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(iii)

If
R,u\to\infty
and (
2.24
) is in order, then

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})\right)\simeq\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})]\simeq\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}R^{d}u^{d-1}\int\varphi^{2}(x)\differential x.

Remark 2.4.8
.

In the setting of Theorem
2.4.7
, that is, for
stationary Gaussian fields whose spectral measure satisfies the
non-degeneracy assumptions of that result, the limiting distribution
at fixed level
u
is Gaussian white noise. This stands in contrast
with the Berry random wave model, where Proposition

2.4.3
shows that the corresponding scaling limit is a
fractional Gaussian field
\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We further conjecture that the high-level regime exhibits the same
qualitative behaviour as in the monochromatic case. More precisely, in
view of the estimates provided by Lemmas
4.4.5
,
4.4.6
and

4.4.7
, it seems plausible that the techniques developed in

[
7
,
12
]
could be adapted to show that, as

u\to\infty
under Condition (
2.24
), the point process of
critical points above level
u
becomes asymptotically close in
distribution to a Poisson point process with intensity

\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}R^{d}u^{d-1}e^{-u^{2}/2}.

2.4.2.
Beyond white scars: scaling limits of random distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}

Fix
d\geq 2
. In what follows, we denote by
\mathcal{A}_{d}
the set of all pairs

(\ell,v)
such that
\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
and
v
belongs to the
one-dimensional linear subspace parallel to
\ell
; see Definition

5.1.1
. Equivalently, every element of
\mathcal{A}_{d}
can be
written in the form
(\ell,\eta u)
, where
\ell=r+\mathbb{R}u\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
,

u\in S^{d-1}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. As demonstrated in Section
5.2
, the space
\mathcal{A}_{d}
provides the natural domain of the
X-ray
Fourier transform
, as introduced in Definition
5.2.3
: a point
(\ell,\eta u)\in\mathcal{A}_{d}
records both the
geometric component of the transform, through the affine line
\ell
,
and its oscillatory
component, through the frequency parameter
\eta
along the direction
of
\ell
. We recall that the
X
-ray Fourier transform of a Schwartz function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as

(2.25)

\mathcal{A}_{d}\ni(\ell,\eta u)\mapsto\mathcal{X}\varphi(\ell,\eta u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi(r+tu)e^{it\eta}\differential t;

see (
5.14
).

Now let
f=\{f(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
be a centered stationary Gaussian field, and assume that the spectral measure
\omega(\differential\xi)
of
f
is

(A1)

either absolutely continuous with respect to the uniform probability measure on the sphere
S^{d-1}
, with a bounded and continuous density
\rho
(the case of BRW (
1.2
) corresponds to
\rho=1
), or

(A2)

or absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with a density
\rho
such that
\sqrt{\rho}
is a Schwartz function (for instance, the case of the Bargmann-Fock field in Figure
1
, corresponds to
\rho(x)\asymp e^{-c\|x\|^{2}}
).

As anticipated in Section
1
, thanks to Proposition
5.7.2
, the random tempered distribution
:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:
defined by (
1.4
) admits the a.s. representation

(2.26)

:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:\,\,=\int\differential\eta\,\,\Sigma_{R}(f),

where
\Sigma_{R}(f)
is the random element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
defined in (
1.5
), with

–

\mathcal{R}^{*}
the adjoint of the one-dimensional Radon transform
\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell):=\mathcal{X}\varphi(\ell,0)
, canonically extended to tempered distributions on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
(see Definition
5.2.3
);

–

\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
is a fractional Laplacian (introduced in Definition
2.3.1
), acting on tempered distributions on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
;

–

\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:
is a random tempered distribution on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
, obtained as the limit in probability of the (pointwise defined) random distribution
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f_{\epsilon}|^{2}}\!:
, where
f_{\epsilon}
is a suitable mollification of
f
(see Theorem
5.6.9
);

–

\int\differential\eta
indicates the restriction of
\Sigma_{R}(f)
to test functions of the form
\Phi(x,\eta)=\varphi(x)
(
(x,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
).

As discussed in Section
5.6
, the random distribution
\Sigma_{R}(f)
should be viewed as a
frequency-dependent version of the Wick square

:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:
. While the latter only retains geometric
information, the additional variable
\eta
records the oscillatory
content of the field along the direction of affine lines. Heuristically, the projection

\int\differential\eta
corresponds to forgetting this frequency information,
and relation (
2.26
) shows that
:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:
is formally recovered as
the frequency average of
\Sigma_{R}(f)
. The next statement corresponds to a condensed (and less general) version of Theorem
5.7.1
, which is one of the main achievements of our work.

Theorem 2.4.9
.

Let the above notation and assumptions prevail.

(1)

In the case
(A1)
, as
R\to\infty
, the renormalized distribution
R^{d-1}\Sigma_{R}(f)
converges in law to a random
distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
whose action on a test function

\Phi(x,\eta)
is obtained by first applying a deterministic
dimension-dependent operator
E_{d}
in the frequency variable
\eta
,
and then evaluating the resulting function against the random
distribution
\mathcal{R}^{*}(\rho\,\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}})
in the spatial variable

x
, where
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}
is a white noise on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
(with an intensity depending on
\rho
).

(2)

In the case
(A2)
, as
R\to\infty
, the renormalized distribution
R^{d}\Sigma_{R}(f)
converges in law to a Gaussian
random distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
whose action on a test function

\Phi(x,\eta)
is obtained by first applying a deterministic
dimension-dependent operator
F_{d}
to
\Phi
, where
F_{d}\Phi
is a
function of two variables
(s,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and then evaluating
the resulting function against a white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
with
intensity
\rho(z)^{2}\,\differential s\,\differential z
.

Remark 2.4.10
.

(i)

In the case
(A1)
, the dimension dependence of the limiting distribution is
entirely encoded by the operator
E_{d}
. In the framework of the present
section (see Remark
5.7.4
for a more precise statement),
E_{d}
acts on test functions

\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
according to

(E_{d}\Phi)(x)\approx\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}\Phi(x,\eta)\,(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}\,\differential\eta,&d\geq 3,\\[8.53581pt]
\Phi(x,1)+\Phi(x,-1),&d=2.\end{cases}

Thus, in dimension
d=2
, the limiting frequency distribution is
concentrated on the two monochromatic frequencies
\eta=\pm 1
,
whereas in dimensions
d\geq 3
it is spread over the interval

[-1,1]
according to the density

(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}
. As already discussed in Remark
1.0.2
-(i), no such
dimensional dependence appears in observable-based results such as
Theorem
2.4.1
. We refer the reader to Remark
5.7.6
and
section

5.8
for further interpretations and discussions of this result.

(ii)

We will see in Section
5.7.3
that Theorem
2.4.9
(as well as the more general Theorem
5.7.1
) allows one to recover Theorem
2.4.1
6
6

6

The case
T_{R}=0
is a direct corollary, see
section

5.7.3
, and the same arguments used in the proof of
theorem

2.4.1
allows one to deal with a general
T_{R}
.
, as well as a variation of Theorem
2.4.7
focusing on quadratic functionals of smooth Gaussian fields with an absolutely continuous density.

2.4.3.
Local
FGF_{\frac{1}{2}}
scaling limits for quadratic functionals of monochromatic random waves on manifolds.

The aim of this section is to show that, by combining the results of Section
2.4.1
with the coupling techniques initiated in
[
31
]
and the estimates from
[
56
]
, one can establish
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
-type fluctuations for quadratic functionals of
pullback monochromatic random waves
on Riemannian manifolds without conjugate points. We choose to focus exclusively on quadratic functionals (involving derivatives up to an arbitrary order
k\geq 1
), both to keep the notational complexity of our forthcoming Theorem
2.4.15
under control and in view of the central role these functionals play in Section
2.4.1
. Our approach is robust, and can be in principle used to infer
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
limits for more general geometric functionals, e.g. through the use of
mixed Kac-Rice formulae
similar to those applied in
[
31
, Section 3]
.

For
d\geq 2
, we denote by
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a
d
-dimensional smooth compact Riemannian manifold. We write
\Delta_{g}
to indicate the
Laplace-Beltrami operator
on
\mathcal{M}
, and use the symbol
\{f_{j}:j\in\mathbb{N}\}
to indicate an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathcal{M})
, composed of real eigenfunctions of
\Delta_{g}
such that

\Delta_{g}f_{j}+\lambda^{2}_{j}f_{j}=0,

where (without loss of generality) the eigenvalues are ordered in such a way that
0=\lambda_{0}<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\dots\uparrow\infty
. Following e.g.
[
25
,
27
,
98
]
, the
monochromatic random wave
on
\mathcal{M}
of parameter
\lambda
is defined as the real-valued random field

(2.27)

\Psi_{\lambda}(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\text{dim}(H_{\lambda})}}\sum_{\lambda_{j}\in[\lambda,\lambda+1]}a_{j}f_{j}(x),\quad x\in\mathcal{M},

where
\{a_{j}\}
is a collection of i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables, and

H_{\lambda}:=\bigoplus_{\lambda_{j}\in[\lambda,\lambda+1]}\text{Ker}(\Delta_{g}+\lambda_{j}^{2}\,\text{Id}),

with
Id
the identity operator. By construction, the field
\Psi_{\lambda}
is centered and Gaussian, and its covariance kernel is

(2.28)

K_{\lambda}(x,y):=\mathrm{Cov}(\Psi_{\lambda}(x),\Psi_{\lambda}(y))=\frac{1}{\text{dim}(H_{\lambda})}\sum_{\lambda_{j}\in[\lambda,\lambda+1]}f_{j}(x)f_{j}(y),\quad x,y\in\mathcal{M}.

Monochromatic “short window random waves” such as
\Psi_{\lambda}
were first introduced by Zelditch in
[
98
]
as approximate models of random Laplace eigenfunctions on manifolds that do not necessarily possess spectral multiplicities; see e.g.
[
25
,
82
,
31
,
87
,
26
,
23
,
27
,
24
]
for further references and details, as well as
[
38
, Section 2.2]
for a recent overview of this area.

Following
[
25
]
, we now fix
x\in\mathcal{M}
, and consider the tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\mathcal{M}
at
x
. We define the
pullback random wave
associated with
\Psi_{\lambda}
at the point
x
to be the Gaussian random field on
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
given by

\Psi_{\lambda}^{x}(u):=\Psi_{\lambda}\left(\exp_{x}\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right)\right),\qquad u\in T_{x}\mathcal{M},

where
\exp_{x}:T_{x}\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
is the usual
exponential map
at
x
. The centered Gaussian (Euclidean) field
\Psi_{\lambda}^{x}
is smooth with probability one, and its covariance kernel is trivially given by

K_{\lambda}^{x}(u,v)=K_{\lambda}\left(\exp_{x}\left(\frac{u}{\lambda}\right),\exp_{x}\left(\frac{v}{\lambda}\right)\right),\qquad u,v\in T_{x}\mathcal{M}.

Definition 2.4.11

(See Definition 1 in
[
25
]
)
.

The point
x\in\mathcal{M}
is said to be of
isotropic scaling
if, for every positive function
\lambda\mapsto r(\lambda)
such that
r(\lambda)=o(\lambda)
, one has that

(2.29)

\sup_{u,v\in{B}(0,r(\lambda))}\left|\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\left[K_{\lambda}^{x}(u,v)-(2\pi)^{d/2}\frac{J_{\nu}(\|u-v\|_{g_{x}})}{\|u-v\|^{\nu}_{g_{x}}}\right]\right|\to 0,\quad\lambda\to\infty,

where
\nu=(d-2)/2
as before,
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
are multi-indices describing partial derivatives with respect to
u
and
v
, respectively,
\|\cdot\|_{g_{x}}
is the norm on
T_{x}\mathcal{M}
induced by
g
, and
{B}(0,r(\lambda))
is the ball of radius
r(\lambda)
centered at the origin.

Remark 2.4.12
.

(a)

Note that
{B}(0,r_{\lambda})
corresponds to a shrinking ball of radius
\frac{r_{\lambda}}{\lambda}
on
\mathcal{M}
.

(b)

Sufficient conditions for a point
x_{0}
to be of isotropic scaling are discussed e.g. in
[
25
, Section 2.5]
, where it is shown that a sufficient condition for
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
to be of isotropic scaling is that the class

\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},x_{0}}:=\{\xi\in S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}:\exists t>0\mbox{ s.t. }\exp_{x_{0}}(t\xi)=x_{0}\}

has volume 0 in
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}
, with
S_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}
denoting the unit sphere in
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}
with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{g_{x_{0}}}
. For every compact smooth manifold
\mathcal{M}
and for every
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
, the property
|\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},x_{0}}|=0
is generic in the space of all Riemannian metrics
[
91
, Lemma 6.1]
. It is well-known that the condition
|\mathcal{L}_{x_{0},x_{0}}|=0
holds for every
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
whenever
\mathcal{M}
has no conjugate points (and, in particular, when
\mathcal{M}
is negatively curved).

(c)

The asymptotic relation (
2.29
) implies that the Gaussian field
\{\partial^{a}\Psi^{x_{0}}_{\lambda}(u):|a|\geq 0,\,u\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}\}
converges in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions to

\left\{({2\pi})^{d/4}\partial^{a}\Psi^{x_{0}}_{\infty}(u):|a|\geq 0,\,u\in T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}\right\},

where
\Psi^{x_{0}}_{\infty}
is the centered Gaussian field on
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}
with covariance

\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{\infty}^{x_{0}}(u)\Psi_{\infty}^{x_{0}}(v)]=\frac{J_{\nu}(\|u-v\|_{g_{x}})}{\|u-v\|^{\nu}_{g_{x}}}.

It is known that, with probability one,
\Psi_{\infty}^{x_{0}}
is an eigenfunction with eigenvalue 1 of the Laplace operator on
T_{x_{0}}\mathcal{M}
associated with the metric
g_{x_{0}}
.

(d)

In this section, we are only interested in second-order results holding for a fixed
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
of isotropic scaling. As a consequence we will always implicitly choose coordinates around
x_{0}
in such a way that
g_{x_{0}}={\rm Id}
, and we will write
\|\cdot\|_{g_{x_{0}}}=\|\cdot\|
. With this convention, the field
(2\pi)^{d/4}\Psi_{\infty}^{x_{0}}
at Point (c) can be identified with
\{\sigma(d)\cdot B(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, where
\sigma(d):=\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{d/2}}{2^{\nu}\Gamma(\nu,+1)}}
(with
\nu=\frac{d-2}{2}
), and
B
is Berry’s field, as defined in (
1.1
)–(
1.2
).

The following statement, taken from
[
56
]
, allows one to explicitly quantify the speed of convergence to zero of the supremum featured in (
2.29
).

Theorem 2.4.13

(Corollary 1.1 in
[
56
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},g)
be a smooth, compact, Riemannian manifold of dimension
d
without conjugate points. Then as
\lambda\to+\infty
, for any multi-indices
a,b\in\mathbb{N}^{d}

(2.30)

\sup_{u,v\in{B}(0,r(\lambda))}\left|\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\left[K_{\lambda}^{x}(u,v)-(2\pi)^{d/2}\frac{J_{\nu}(\|u-v\|_{g_{x}})}{\|u-v\|^{\nu}_{g_{x}}}\right]\right|=O\left(\frac{1}{\log\lambda}\right),

whenever
r_{\lambda}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{\log\lambda}}\right)
. The constant in the
O
-notation depends on the choice of
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
and
\{r_{\lambda}\}
, and on the order of differentiation.

Remark 2.4.14
.

According to Remark
2.4.12
-(a), the ball
{B}(0,r_{\lambda})
appearing in Theorem
2.4.13
corresponds to a shrinking ball of radius
O\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda\log\lambda}}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}
.

Keeler’s estimate (
2.30
) was combined in
[
31
, Theorem 1.6]
with coupling techniques for Gaussian random fields to show that, in dimension
d=2
, if the sequence
\{r_{\lambda}\}
grows at a rate significantly slower than
\lambda
(more precisely, as
r_{\lambda}=o(\log\lambda)^{1/25}
), then the
nodal length measure
associated with
\Psi^{x_{0}}_{\lambda}
at a point of isotropic scaling satisfies a large-domain CLT, with the same normalization and centering as in the case of the nodal length of planar Berry’s random wave model (see, e.g.,
[
71
,
15
]
). Combining
[
82
, Section 4.1]
and
[
70
, Proposition 1.3]
, one also infers that this convergence can be interpreted as convergence to white noise in the sense of random distributions.

The next statement, which constitutes the main result of this section, shows that in any dimension
d\geq 2
, and under growth conditions on
r_{\lambda}
comparable to those above, one can use the results established in Section
2.4.1
to derive large-domain
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
limits for quadratic functionals of pullback waves. The proof, which also relies on the Gaussian coupling techniques introduced in
[
31
]
, is deferred to Section
7.2
.

Theorem 2.4.15

(
{\rm FGF}_{1/2}
limits for pullback random waves)
.

For
d\geq 2
, let the above notation and framework prevail, and let
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
be a point of isotropic scaling. For
k\geq 1
, let
J=J(d;k)
denote the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{J}(d;k)
of all multi-indices
a
such that
|a|\leq k
. Let

Q:\mathbb{R}^{J}\to\mathbb{R}

be a polynomial of degree
\leq 2
such that the Berry’s random wave observable

\varphi\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q\big(\partial^{a}B(x):a\in\mathcal{J}\big)\,\varphi(x)\,dx,

is in the class
{\bf UC}\left\{{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta^{2}>0
(see Definition
2.3.1
). Then, if

(2.31)

{r_{\lambda}\to\infty\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{\lambda}^{12d+4k+4}(\log r_{\lambda})^{2}=o\big(\log\lambda\big)},\quad\lambda\to\infty,

one has that, for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}
,

\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}Q\big(\partial^{a}\tilde{\Psi}_{\lambda}^{x_{0}}(x):a\in\mathcal{J}\big)\,\varphi_{r_{\lambda}}(x)\,dx-\alpha\,r_{\lambda}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,dx}{\beta\,r_{\lambda}^{(d+1)/2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm law}}{{\longrightarrow}}h(\varphi),

where
h\sim{\rm FGF}_{1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
\tilde{\Psi}_{\lambda}^{x_{0}}:=\frac{{\Psi}_{\lambda}^{x_{0}}}{\sigma(d)}
, and the constant
\sigma(d)
has been defined in Remark
2.4.12
-(d).

Remark 2.4.16
.

The growth condition (
2.31
) is not expected to be optimal. In particular, as in
[
31
]
, the exponents are likely artefacts of the use of Sobolev embedding techniques, whereas the logarithmic dependence originates from the sharp estimate of Keeler (
2.30
) and cannot be dispensed with.

Example 2.4.17
.

By virtue of Theorem
2.4.1
, for generic
d\geq 2
and
k\geq 1
, in Theorem
2.4.15
one can choose

Q\big(\partial^{a}\tilde{\Psi}_{\lambda}^{x_{0}}(x):a\in\mathcal{J}(d;k)\big)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\|\nabla^{j}\tilde{\Psi}_{\lambda}^{x_{0}}(x)\|^{2},

where the coefficients
c_{j}
are such that
\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\neq 0
.

3.
Further preliminary notions

3.1.
Tempered distributions

3.1.1.
Schwartz spaces and distributions

We adopt standard conventions in distributional language. In particular, as in Section
2.1
above and
[
59
, Sec. 2.1]
, we denote by
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the real Schwartz space and by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the space of real tempered distributions. In the following, we will need to extend such notation to product spaces
M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
M
being a compact smooth Riemannian manifold. So, we define
\mathcal{S}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space of real-valued smooth functions on
M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose derivatives of
all orders exist and decay faster than any polynomial at infinity. Then, the space of real tempered distributions on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as the dual of
\mathcal{S}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
with respect to its natural topology. Instead of spelling out the intrinsic definition, we shall assume that
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
is isometrically embedded. Then,
\mathcal{S}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is canonically isomorphic to the space of all
\varphi|_{M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, where
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, endowed with the quotient topology; while
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the subset of tempered distributions
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\varphi|_{M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}=\psi|_{M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\implies T(\varphi)=T(\psi)
. More generally, if
E
is a vector bundle over
M
, as above, we define the spaces
\mathcal{S}(E)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(E)
in the analogous way, by embedding of
E
as a subbundle in a trivial vector bundle:
E\subset M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We denote as
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
the corresponding complexifications.

3.1.2.
Symbolic differential notation

We denote the canonical pairing of
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as

(3.1)

\langle f,\varphi\rangle=:\int_{M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential x)\varphi(x),

In particular, formally,
f(\differential x)=f(x)\differential x
when
f
is the distribution associated with a measurable function
f\colon M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
. Note that the pairing coincides with the
L^{2}
complex linear product with respect to the Riemannian density, for which we keep the same notation.

3.1.3.
Composition and adjoint

Let
E\colon\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a linear and continuous operator, then, for any
W\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, one can define
W\circ E\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, such that
\langle W\circ E,\varphi\rangle=\langle W,E(\varphi)\rangle
, for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. The definition extends naturally to
\mathcal{A}_{d}
and
\mathbb{A}_{d}
, as defined in Section
2.4.2
and, in more detail, in
5
. The
adjoint operator
of
E
is the operator
E^{*}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
defined as
E^{*}W=W\circ E
.

3.1.4.
Changes of variables

We say that
Y\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a
Schwartz diffeomorphism
if it is smooth and all of its derivatives decay faster than any polynomial at infinity. This property ensures that
|\det Y|^{-1},|\det Y|\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so that, for any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the function
|\det Y|^{-1}\varphi\circ Y^{-1}
is still in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Given
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define
f\circ Y\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as

(3.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(Y(\differential x))\varphi(x):=\langle f\circ Y,\varphi\rangle:=\langle f,|\det Y|^{-1}\varphi\circ Y^{-1}\rangle\!\!=\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential y)|\det Y|^{-1}\varphi(Y^{-1}(y)).

We also define the symbol
f(\differential Y(x)):=f(Y(\differential x))|\det Y|
, as the differential
g(\differential x)
associated with the distribution
g=(f\circ Y)|\det Y|
. As a consequence,

(3.3)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential Y(x))\varphi(x):=\langle f,\varphi\circ Y^{-1}\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential y)\varphi(Y^{-1}(y)).

One can easily check that this definition is coherent with standard changes of variables in the integral, when
f(\differential x)=f(x)\differential x
is also a smooth function. For instance, given
R>0
, the scaling
f(R\cdot)
of a distribution
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
reads as

(3.4)

\langle f(R\cdot),\varphi\rangle=\int f(R\differential x)\varphi(x)=\int f(\differential(Rx))R^{-d}\varphi(x)=\int f(\differential x)\varphi(R^{-1}x)R^{-d},

for any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

3.1.5.
Delta function

The delta distribution
\delta\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
admits more general changes of variables than just the Schwartz diffeomorphisms. Let
E\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be smooth and such that
0
is a regular value. Then,
\delta\circ E\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined as

(3.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\delta(E(\differential x))\varphi(x)=\langle\delta\circ E,\varphi\rangle:=\int_{E^{-1}(0)}\frac{\varphi(x)}{\mathrm{Jac}E(x)}\differential x.

This is a well-defined tempered distribution whenever all components of
E
are of Schwartz class. When working with the distribution
\delta\circ E
, it is more convenient to write the corresponding symbol as
\delta(E(x))\differential x:=\delta(E(\differential x))
, and manipulate directly the
delta function
symbol
\delta(E(x))
. When there is a natural splitting
\mathbb{R}^{d+d^{\prime}}=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
involved, the notation
\delta(E(x,y))\differential x\differential y
does not lead to ambiguities, since the following Fubini formula holds:

(3.6)

\int_{E^{-1}(0)}\varphi(x,y)\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d^{\prime}-k}(\differential(x,y))}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(E\right)(x,y)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential x\int_{E(x,\cdot)^{-1}(0)}\varphi(x,y)\frac{\mathcal{H}^{d-k}(\differential(x,y))}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(E(x,\cdot)\right)(y)};

this is due to the coarea formula
[
34
, Theorem 3.2.12]
, together with the fact that

\mathrm{Jac}(A|_{\ker B})\mathrm{Jac}(B)=\mathrm{Jac}(A,B)=\mathrm{Jac}(B|_{\ker(A)})\mathrm{Jac}(A),

for any two linear maps
A,B
with the same domain. In particular, for
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
E
as above, we have that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential x)\delta(E(x,y))
is a well defined distribution in the variable
y
, provided that
0
is a regular value for
E
.
7
7

7

Then, by Sard’s theorem,
0
is a regular value for
E(x,\cdot)
for almost every
x
.

In this context, it is useful to remember the following formula. Consider the case
E(x,y)=x-h(y)
, then one can easily check that
\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}\left(\differential x\delta(x-h(y))\right)\differential y=e^{-i\langle{\xi,h(y)}\rangle}\differential\xi\differential y
. Therefore, by the inversion formula

(3.7)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential\xi\frac{e^{i\langle{\xi,x-h(y)}\rangle}}{(2\pi)^{d}}:=\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to x}^{-1}\left(e^{-i\langle{\xi,h(y)}\rangle}\right)=\delta(x-h(y)),

as distributions in the variables
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
; where the left-most integral has to be interpreted as defined by the above identity.

The multiplication of two deltas
\delta(E_{1}(x))\delta(E_{2}(x))\differential x:=\delta(E_{1}(x),E_{2}(x))\differential x
is allowed if and only if all three are defined, i.e., if they all satisfy the regular value condition. Again, such a notation does not lead to ambiguities, thanks to the coarea formula, combined with
eq.

3.6
:

(3.8)

\int_{E_{1}^{-1}(0)\cap E_{2}^{-1}(0)}\varphi\frac{\differential\mathcal{H}^{d-k_{1}-k_{2}}}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(E_{1},E_{2}\right)}=\int_{E_{1}^{-1}(0)\cap E_{2}^{-1}(0)}\varphi\frac{\differential\mathcal{H}^{d-k_{1}-k_{2}}}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(E_{2}|_{E_{1}=0}\right)\mathrm{Jac}(E_{1})}.

In such context it is often useful to observe that
\delta(E_{1})\delta(E_{2})=\delta(E_{1})\delta(E_{2}+\alpha E_{1})
,
\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Note that, within this framework, the square
\delta(E(x))^{2}
is not well defined.

3.2.
Notations

For the rest of the paper, we will adopt the following conventions, analogous to those adopted in
[
38
, 1.3]
.

3.2.1.
Random elements

Given random elements,
A,B
, we write
A\sim B
to indicate that
A,B
have the same distribution. Given a topological space
\mathscr{X}
, we will write

(3.9)

Z\,\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\,\mathscr{X}

to denote the fact that
Z
is a measurable random element with values in
\mathscr{X}
.

3.2.2.
Notation for integrals, volumes and spheres

Given a Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
of dimension
d
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we
denote the associated
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure by
\mathcal{H}^{k}(\cdot)
. Moreover, given a Borel function
F\colon M\to\mathbb{R}
, we will use the following shorthand notations:

(3.10)

\displaystyle\int_{M}F(x)\differential x:=\int_{M}F(x)\mathcal{H}^{\dim(M)}(\differential x),

In particular, if
S\subset M
is a submanifold of dimension
k
, then
\int_{S}F(x)\differential x
stands for the integral with respect to the
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure associated with
g
(or, equivalently, to
g|_{S}
), unless otherwise specified.
Note that, although such notation is not standard, it will be adopted throughout the paper to make our formulas more readable while causing only minimal ambiguities.

3.2.3.
Spheres

Many formulas in the paper concern the case in which
M=S(V)
is the unit sphere in a metric vector space
V
, that is,
S(V):=\{x\in V:\|x\|=1\}
(see, e.g.,
lemma

5.6.7
). In particular,
S^{d}=S(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the standard round sphere. Finally, we define the constants

(3.11)

s_{d}:=\mathrm{Vol}(S^{d})=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)},

for any integer
d
.

3.3.
Random tempered distributions

In this subsection, we expand the discussion developed in
section

2.1
, including additional technical details.
Let
(\Omega,\mathscr{E},\mathbb{P})
be our reference probability space. A
random tempered distribution
on a space
E
(where
E=\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
E=M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
as above) is a measurable map
\Xi\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
, where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
is endowed with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E))
generated by the weak-
*
topology; we write
\Xi\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
, leaving
\Omega
as implicit.
These objects are also called
Generalized random fields
in
[
42
]
and
[
16
]
.
Note that the topological properties of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for arbitrary
d\in\mathbb{N}
, easily translates to their analogous version for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The extension to arbitrary
M
is done by choosing an arbitrary embedding
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
, as discussed in
section

3.1.1
. The extension to the complexified space is straightforward: a complex random tempered distribution
\Xi\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
is equivalent to a pair
(\real\Xi,\imaginary\Xi)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}(E)\times\mathcal{S}(E)\cong\mathcal{S}(E\sqcup E)
.
In particular, as proved in
[
16
, Proposition 3.7]
, it should be noted that
\mathscr{B}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(E))
coincides with the
\sigma
-algebra generated by a stronger topology: the one generated by the seminorms
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(E)\ni T\mapsto\sup_{\varphi\in B}|\langle T,\varphi\rangle|
, with
B
ranging over bounded subsets of
\mathcal{S}(E)
. Therefore, the two corresponding notions of convergence in law coincide.

The
characteristic functional
of
\Xi
is the map
\chi\colon\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)\to\mathbb{C}
defined by

(3.12)

\chi(\varphi):=\mathbb{E}\left\{e^{i\real\langle\Xi,\varphi\rangle}\right\},\quad\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E).

Note that
\chi
is defined on the complexified Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
: letting
\varphi
vary over all complex test functions is necessary to recover the full joint law of
(\real\Xi,\imaginary\Xi)
, since for
\varphi=\varphi_{1}+i\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
with
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathcal{S}(E)
, the real part
\real\langle\Xi,\varphi\rangle
depends on both components of
\Xi
.

The following classical result provides the existence and uniqueness of random tempered distributions through their characteristic functional.

Theorem 3.3.1

(Bochner–Minlos)
.

A functional
\chi\colon\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)\to\mathbb{C}
is the characteristic functional of a random tempered distribution
