\Xi\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
if and only if
\chi(0)=1
,
\chi
is positive definite, and
\chi
is continuous in the topology of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
. Moreover, the law of
\Xi
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
is uniquely determined by
\chi
.

Proof.

See
[
59
, Theorem 2.3]
and
[
16
, Theorem 2.1]
. The complex case follows from the real one via the identification
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(E\sqcup E)
.
∎

We say that
\Xi
is a
centered Gaussian
random tempered distribution if
\langle\Xi,\varphi\rangle
is a centered Gaussian random variable for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
. In this case, the characteristic functional takes the form
\chi(\varphi)=\exp\!\Big({-\tfrac{1}{4}C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi)-\tfrac{1}{4}\real\,C_{2}(\varphi,\varphi)}\Big)
, where

(3.13)

C(\varphi,\psi):=\left(\mathbb{E}\left\{\langle\Xi,\varphi\rangle\overline{\langle\Xi,\psi\rangle}\right\},\mathbb{E}\left\{\langle\Xi,\varphi\rangle\langle\Xi,\psi\rangle\right\}\right),\quad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}(E),

is a pair
C=(C_{1},C_{2})
of two forms on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
, called the
covariance
of
\Xi
.

Remark 3.3.2
.

By
theorem

3.3.1
, the law of a centered Gaussian random tempered distribution is uniquely determined by its covariance pair
C=(C_{1},C_{2})
, where
C_{1}
ranges over all continuous non-negative definite sesquilinear Hermitian forms on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
and
C_{2}
over all continuous symmetric bilinear forms on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
satisfying the compatibility condition
|C_{2}(\varphi,\psi)|^{2}\leq C_{1}(\varphi,\varphi)\,C_{1}(\psi,\psi)
for all
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
.

The following convergence criterion for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
-valued random variables is the natural counterpart of Lévy’s classical continuity theorem.

Theorem 3.3.3

(Fernique-Lévy continuity)
.

Let
\{\Xi_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
be random tempered distributions on
E
. Then
\Xi_{n}\xrightarrow{\;\rm law\;}\Xi
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
if and only if the characteristic functionals converge pointwise:

(3.14)

\chi_{n}(\varphi):=\mathbb{E}\left\{e^{i\real\langle\Xi_{n},\varphi\rangle}\right\}\;\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}\;\chi(\varphi),\qquad\forall\,\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E),

and
\chi
is continuous at
0
in the topology of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
.

Proof.

See
[
36
]
and

[
16
, Corollary 2.4]
.
The complex case follows from the real one via
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)\cong\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(E\sqcup E)
.
∎

Remark 3.3.4
.

For centered Gaussian random tempered distributions, the characteristic functional
\chi(\varphi)
is always continuous at
0
whenever
C
is on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)^{2}
. In particular, by
theorem

3.3.3
: if
\Xi_{n}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
are centered Gaussian with covariance
C_{n}
, and
C_{n}(\varphi,\varphi)\to C(\varphi,\varphi)
for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
with
C
continuous on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
, then
\Xi_{n}
converges in law to
\Xi
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(E)
, where
\Xi
is the centered Gaussian with covariance
C
(whose existence is guaranteed by
theorem

3.3.1
).

3.4.
White Noise and Fractional Gaussian Fields

In this section we will introduce the necessary definitions and notations to work with white noise and fractional Gaussian fields. We largely follow the surveys
[
59
,
90
]
.

3.4.1.
Fourier transform convention

Let
\langle x,\xi\rangle
denote the Euclidean scalar product of two vectors
x,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We define the Fourier transform of a function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, as the function

(3.15)

\hat{\varphi}(\xi):=\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}\left(\varphi(x)\right):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)e^{-i\langle{x,\xi}\rangle}\differential x.

We recall that
\varphi\mapsto\hat{\varphi}
extends to an isometry of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{C})
to itself, such that
\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to x}^{-1}=(2\pi)^{-d}\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to(-x)}.

Such a definition extends from Schwartz functions to tempered distributions, by the adjunction relation:

\langle\hat{f},\varphi\rangle=\langle f,\hat{\varphi}\rangle.

We refer to
[
45
, Section V.3]
for a list of the standard properties of the Fourier transform.

Note that if
v\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a linear isomorphism, then

(3.16)

\mathcal{F}_{x\to v^{-*}(\xi)}\left(\varphi(x)\right)=\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}\left(\varphi(v(x))\right)|\det v|.

Such a property implies that the Fourier transform is invariant under orthogonal (
v=v^{-*}
and
|\det v|=1
) changes of coordinates.
Given a metric finite-dimensional vector space
V
, and an isometry
v\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to V
. One defines through
v
the spaces
\mathcal{S}(V),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(V)
and the Fourier transform

(3.17)

\mathcal{F}_{v\to v(\xi)}^{V}\left(\varphi(v)\right):=\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}\left(\varphi(v(x))\right),\qquad\mathcal{F}_{v\to v(\xi)}^{-V}\left(\varphi(v)\right):=\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}^{-1}\left(\varphi(v(x))\right).

The definition so obtained does not depend on the chosen isometry
v
, by
eq.

3.16
.

3.4.2.
The fractional Laplacian

Let
\Delta=\sum_{i}\partial_{i}^{2}
be the usual (negative definite) Laplacian operator.
The fractional Laplacian
(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}
is the operator acting on
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by:

(3.18)

(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}\varphi(x)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to x}^{-1}\left(|\xi|^{s}\hat{\varphi}(\xi)\right).

This is a well defined function for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, whenever
s>-d
, while for arbitrary
s
the domain must be specified, see
[
59
, Section 2.1]
. We refer to
[
59
, Section 2.2]
for the extension of
(-\Delta)^{s}
to distributions, by self-adjointness.

Remark 3.4.1
.

The operator
(-\Delta)^{\frac{s}{2}}
does not depend on the choice of Fourier transform convention; its Fourier multiplier
|\xi|^{s}
does.

3.4.3.
White noise and FGF definition

Given
M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
as above, and a Borel measure
\omega
on it, we call
white noise on
M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with respect to
\omega
any Gaussian random tempered distribution
\mathbb{W}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(3.19)

\mathbb{E}\left\{\langle\mathbb{W},\varphi\rangle\langle\mathbb{W},\psi\rangle\right\}=\int\varphi\psi\,\differential\omega,\quad\forall\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Notably, in the following, we will always work in situations in which
\mathcal{S}(M\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\omega)
. When
\omega
is the Riemannian density or another canonical measure, we will just say
white noise on
M\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathbb{W}
be a white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we call
a Fractional Gaussian Field of order
s
any random distribution
h\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
that satisfies the distributional identity, almost surely:

(3.20)

h=(-\Delta)^{-\frac{s}{2}}\mathbb{W}

In such a case, we write
h\sim\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
.

Remark 3.4.2
.

In this paper we will be concerned only with the case
s<\frac{d}{2}
. In this regime, the operator
(-\Delta)^{-s/2}
is well-defined and injective on tempered distributions, and therefore
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
is a well-defined random element of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
In particular, the law of
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
is translation invariant: with obvious notation, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the translated field
x\mapsto\mathrm{FGF}_{s}(x+y)
has the same law as
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
.

Remark 3.4.3
.

In the case
s\geq\frac{d}{2}
, the field
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
is only defined modulo polynomials of degree at most
\lfloor H\rfloor
, where
H=s-d/2
(see
[
28
, Section 1.1]
).
Accordingly, the pairing
\langle\mathrm{FGF}_{s},\varphi\rangle
is intrinsically defined only for test functions
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}x^{\alpha}\varphi(x)\,\differential x=0\quad\text{for all }|\alpha|\leq\lfloor H\rfloor.

In the literature (see
[
59
]
),
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
is therefore defined as a random element of a suitable quotient
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})/\mathcal{T}_{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, in a way that is translation invariant in law (see
[
59
, Section 2,3]
).

In such case, all identities involving
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
, including the translation invariance, are understood modulo polynomials of degree at most
\lfloor H\rfloor
.

As discussed in
[
59
, Section 2.1]
, due to Gaussianity, the point of view of random distribution is equivalent to other common ones adopted in the literature, including those oan f isonormal process (see
[
74
]
) or Gaussian Hilbert spaces (see
[
53
]
). Such a point of view is very convenient for us, because it allows one to apply the Fourier transform techniques in an almost sure manner.

3.4.4.
Hurst parameter

The Hurst parameter of the
\mathrm{FGF}_{s}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the real number

(3.21)

H:=s-\frac{d}{2}.

We recall that such number characterizes the behavior of the field under rescaling: the following equivalence in law holds:

(3.22)

\mathrm{FGF}_{s}(R\cdot)\sim R^{H}\mathrm{FGF}_{s}(\cdot),

where
\langle h(R\cdot),\varphi\rangle:=\langle h(\cdot),R^{-d}\varphi(R^{-1}\cdot)\rangle
. Moreover, the Hurst parameter identifies the fractional Sobolev (a.s.) regularity of the paths of the field (see
[
59
, Proposition 6.2 and section 8]
):

(3.23)

\mathrm{FGF}_{s}\in\dot{H}^{H-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\quad\text{a.s.}\iff\varepsilon>0,

where, when
r\leq 0
,
\dot{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes the space of distributions defined as (see
[
59
, Section 2.1]
)

(3.24)

\dot{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=((-\Delta)^{\frac{r}{2}})^{-1}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\left\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:|\xi|^{r}\hat{f}(\xi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\right\}.

This space is endowed with the natural scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\dot{H}^{r}}
on
\dot{H}^{r}(X)
, that makes
(-\Delta)^{\frac{r}{2}}:H^{r}\to L^{2}
an isometry of Hilbert spaces.
8
8

8

In contrast with what happens for standard fractional Sobolev spaces, there is no inclusion relating
\dot{H}^{s_{1}}
and
\dot{H}^{s_{2}}
.
The standard fractional Sobolev spaces are defined, for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
, as

H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\left\{f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}:(1+|\xi|^{2})^{r/2}\hat{f}(\xi)\in L^{2}\right\}.

Then,
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\dot{H}^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for all
r\geq 0
. Instead, if
r>0
, then
\dot{H}^{-r}(X)\subsetneq H^{-r}(X)
.
Precisely, we have that

(3.25)

\displaystyle H^{-s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\{f\in\dot{H}^{-s}(X)\colon\partial^{\alpha}\hat{f}(0)=0\ \forall|\alpha|<s\}.

See
[
59
, Section 2.1]
for details.

3.5.
Poisson point process

The reader is referred e.g. to
[
58
,
80
,
51
]
for a detailed presentation of the material discussed below.

Consider a measurable space
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z})
, and write
\nu
to indicate a
\sigma
-finite measure on it. Denote by
\mathbf{N}_{\sigma}=\mathbf{N}_{\sigma}(\mathcal{Z})
the class of all
\sigma
-finite point measures
\chi
on
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z})
that satisfy
\chi(B)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cup\{+\infty\}
for all
B\in\mathscr{Z}
. The set
\mathbf{N}_{\sigma}=\mathbf{N}_{\sigma}(\mathcal{Z})
is equipped with the smallest

\sigma
-field
\mathscr{N}_{\sigma}:=\mathscr{N}_{\sigma}(\mathcal{Z})
enjoying the property that, for every
B\in\mathscr{Z}
, the mapping
\mathbf{N}_{\sigma}\ni\chi\mapsto\chi(B)\in[0,+\infty]
is measurable. A
Poisson point process
on
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z})
with
control measure

\nu
, written

\eta=\{\eta(B)\,:\,B\in{\mathscr{Z}}\},

is a random element
taking values in the measurable space
(\mathbf{N}_{\sigma},\mathscr{N}_{\sigma})
and satisfying the following two properties: (i) for any choice
B_{1},\dotsc,B_{m}\in\mathscr{Z}
of pairwise disjoint sets, the random variables
\eta(B_{1}),\dotsc,\eta(B_{m})
are stochastically independent, and (ii) for every
B\in\mathscr{Z}
, the random variable
\eta(B)
has a Poisson law with mean
\nu(B)
,
where we have implicitly extended the family of Poisson distributions to the completed half-line
[0,+\infty]
in the usual way. The existence of Poisson point processes associated with generic
\sigma
-finite measure spaces
(\mathcal{Z},\mathscr{Z},\nu)
is discussed e.g. in
[
58
, Section 3.2]
.

Remark 3.5.1
.

For
d\geq 1
, the homogeneous Poisson point process
\eta=\Pi_{t}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
t>0
, as introduced in Section
2.1
, corresponds to the following specifications:

–

Z=\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

–

\mathcal{Z}=\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

–

\nu=t\cdot\differential x
, where
\differential x
stands for the Lebesgue measure.

Remark 3.5.2
.

The Poisson line process
\eta=\eta_{t}\sim{\rm PLP}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(
d\geq 2
) defined in Section
2.3
corresponds to the following configuration:

–

Z=\mathbb{A}_{d}
, the Grassmannian of affine lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
;

–

\mathcal{Z}
is the
\sigma
-field generated by the standard topology on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
, as described e.g. in
[
51
, p. 4]
;
9
9

9

Fix an element
\ell
of the Grassmannian
G(d,1)
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; the standard topology of
\mathbb{A}_{d}
is the finest topology with respect to which the mapping
\ell^{\perp}\times SO(d):(x,\theta)\mapsto\theta(x+\ell)
is continuous. Such a topology is independent of the choice of
\ell
.

–

\nu=t\cdot\mu
, where
t>0
and
\mu
is the (
\sigma
-finite) invariant measure on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
, that has been fixed through relation (
2.8
).

Remark 3.5.3
.

Let
\Pi_{t}
be as in Remark
3.5.1
. By Campbell’s formula

[
58
, Proposition 2.7]
, one has that

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\|x\|)^{-N}\Pi_{t}(\differential x)\right]<\infty,

for every
N>d
. It follows that

\Pi_{t}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
almost surely. An analogous argument
shows that, if
\eta_{t}\sim{\rm PLP}_{t}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\eta_{t}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
almost surely, where
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
is
the class of tempered distributions introduced in Definition
5.1.3
.

4.
Observables of random fields: proofs of main results.

The aim of this section is to prove the main results stated in Section
2.4.1
.

Remark 4.0.1

(Convention on test functions)
.

From now on, we fix an integer
d\geq 2
and use the symbol
\varphi
to denote either an element of the Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, or a finite linear combination of indicators of bounded convex sets having a
C^{\infty}
boundary with non-vanishing Gauss curvature (as in the statement of Proposition
2.4.3
).

Given
\varphi
as above, we define the function
\varphi_{R}
according to (
2.2
); note that, for every
p\geq 1
, one has that
\|\varphi_{R}\|^{p}_{p}=R^{d}\|\varphi\|^{p}_{p}
, where
\|\cdot\|_{p}
stands for the usual norm in
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We write
\varphi^{-}(x):=\varphi(-x)
. Defining

(4.1)

\psi=\psi_{\varphi}:=\varphi*\varphi^{-},

one has therefore that

\varphi_{R}*\varphi^{-}_{R}=R^{d}\psi_{R}.

Note that, for every
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\psi_{R}(z)=\varphi*\varphi^{-}(0)=\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2},

and that our conventions allow one to rewrite (
2.23
) in convolutional form, namely:

(4.2)

\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\varphi(x)\varphi(y)}{\|x-y\|^{d-1}}\differential x\differential y=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\psi(x)}{\|x\|^{d-1}}\differential x.

4.1.
Three ancillary lemmas

In this subsection (consistently with (
1.2
)), we will denote by
\sigma
the uniform probability measure on the sphere with unit radius
S^{d-1}
. If
s
is a smooth complex function on
S^{d-1}
, we let
r_{s}
be the Fourier transform of
s\sigma
, that is:
r_{s}(x)=\int_{S^{d-1}}e^{i\langle x,\theta\rangle}s(\theta)\sigma(\differential\theta)
. We also recall the definition of the constant
K_{d}
given in (
2.20
).

Our first result provides an exact characterization of the behaviour of
r_{s}(x)
for large values of
\|x\|
.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let the above notation prevail and assume that
s
is a smooth symmetric function (that is
s(-\theta)=s(\theta)
). Then, as
\|x\|
goes to
+\infty
,

(4.3)

r_{s}(x)=\frac{K_{d}}{\|x\|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}s\left(\frac{x}{\|x\|}\right)\cos\left(\|x\|-\pi\frac{d-1}{4}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{\|x\|^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\right).

If
s
is antisymmetric (that is
s(-\theta)=-s(\theta)
) then (
4.3
) continues to hold, with
\cos
replaced by
i\sin
, with
i=\sqrt{-1}
.

Proof.

The proof is a consequence of the stationary phase method (see e.g.
[
50
]
) applied to the measure
s\,\differential\sigma
. Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The sphere
S^{d-1}
can be considered as the union of the graphs of the two functions
u\mapsto\pm\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}
, with
u\in x^{\perp}\cap B(0,1)
. Then,

\displaystyle r_{s}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|S^{d-1}|}\int_{x^{\perp}\cap B(0,1)}e^{i\|x\|\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}}s\left(\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}\frac{x}{\|x\|}+u\right)\frac{\differential u}{\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{|S^{d-1}|}\int_{x^{\perp}\cap B(0,1)}e^{-i\|x\|\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}}s\left(-\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}\frac{x}{\|x\|}+u\right)\frac{\differential u}{\sqrt{1-\|u\|^{2}}}.

As
\|x\|
goes to infinity, one can apply the stationary phase method to each of these two terms to estimate the asymptotics of
r_{s}(x)
. In both cases, the phase is stationary when
u=0
. At this point, the Hessian is
-\mathrm{Id}
for the first term, and
\mathrm{Id}
for the second. We deduce that as
\|x\|
goes to infinity, one the asymptotic relations

r_{s}(x)=\frac{1}{|S^{d-1}|}\left(\frac{2\pi}{\|x\|}\right)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\left[s\left(\frac{x}{\|x\|}\right)e^{i\|x\|-(d-1)\frac{i\pi}{4}}+s\left(-\frac{x}{\|x\|}\right)e^{-i\|x\|+(d-1)\frac{i\pi}{4}}\right]+O\left(\frac{1}{\|x\|^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\right)

The conclusion follows by gathering the complex exponentials in the case where
s
is even (resp. odd), and the definition of the constant
K_{d}
.
∎

Lemma 4.1.2
.

Let the test function
\varphi
be as in Remark
4.0.1
. Then, for some constant
C
depending on
\varphi
,

\lim_{R\rightarrow+\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{R}(x)r_{s}(x)\differential x=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\psi_{R}(x)r_{s}^{2}(x)\right|\differential x\leq CR,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\psi_{R}(x)r_{s}^{3}(x)\right|\differential x\leq C\sqrt{R}.

Proof.

For the first point, one has that, by Plancherel’s identity,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{R}(x)r_{s}(x)\differential x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widehat{\psi_{R}(\theta)}\differential\sigma(\theta).

The function
\widehat{\psi_{R}}
converges to a Dirac mass at zero. In particular, it converges to zero on the sphere, and the conclusion of the first point follows by dominated convergence. The second point follows from the bound

|r_{s}(x)|\leq\frac{K}{|x|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}},

given by the previous Lemma
4.1.1
, which in turn implies that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left|\psi_{R}(x)r_{s}^{2}(x)\right|\differential x\leq K^{2}R\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|\psi(x)|}{\|x\|^{d-1}}\differential x\leq CR.

The third point follows from the decay of
|r_{s}(x)|^{3}
: if
d\geq 4
the integral converges, if
d=3
then there is a factor
\log(R)
appearing, and if
d=2
there is a factor
\sqrt{R}
appearing. The given bound is thus valid in any dimension.
∎

Lemma 4.1.3
.

For
j\geq 0
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{R}(x)\|\nabla^{j}r(x)\|^{2}\differential x\underset{R\rightarrow+\infty}{\simeq}R\frac{K_{d}^{2}}{2}\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}.

Proof.

Note that

\nabla^{j}r(x)=\int_{S^{d-1}}(i\theta)^{\otimes j}e^{i\langle x,\theta\rangle}\sigma(d\theta),

By Lemma
4.1.1
applied with
s(\theta)=(i\theta)^{\otimes j}
, one has the asymptotic relation

\nabla^{j}r(x)=\frac{K_{d}}{\|x\|^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\left(\frac{x}{\|x\|}\right)^{\otimes j}\cos\left(\|x\|-\frac{\pi(d-1)}{4}+\frac{j\pi}{2}\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{\|x\|^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\right),

which implies that

\|\nabla^{j}r(x)\|^{2}=\frac{K_{d}^{2}}{\|x\|^{d-1}}\cos\left(\|x\|-\frac{\pi(d-1)}{4}+\frac{j\pi}{2}\right)^{2}+O\left(\frac{1}{\|x\|^{d+1}}\right),

yielding that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{R}(x)\|\nabla^{j}r(x)\|^{2}\differential x=R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)r^{2}(Rx)\differential x\simeq R\frac{K_{d}^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\psi(x)}{\|x\|^{d-1}}\differential x,

where the factor
1/2
comes from the mean of
\cos^{2}
and the Riemann–Lebesgue lemma.
∎

4.2.
Proof of Theorem
2.4.1

By standard Gaussian computations and stationarity, one has that

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{R}(x)\left(\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}-1\right)\differential x\right)=2R^{d}\sum_{i,j=0}^{k}c_{i}c_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi_{R}(x)\|\nabla^{i+j}r(x)\|^{2}\differential x.

Using Lemma
4.1.3
together with assumption (
2.19
), one eventually deduces the asymptotic relation

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X(\varphi_{R}))\underset{R\rightarrow+\infty}{\simeq}R^{d+1}{K_{d}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{k}c_{i}c_{j}\,\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L}=R^{d+1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\right)^{2}{K_{d}^{2}}\,\|\varphi\|^{2}_{L}.

Now define

Y_{R}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{R}(x)\left(\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}-1\right)\differential x,

so that
X(\varphi_{R})-\mathbb{E}[X(\varphi_{R})]=Y_{R}+T_{R}
. By virtue of (
2.19
) it is now sufficient to show that, as
R\to\infty
, the sequence

\widetilde{Y}_{R}:=\frac{Y_{R}}{R^{{(d+1)}/2}},\quad R>0,

converges in distribution to a Gaussian random variable. To prove such a result, it is convenient to represent Berry’s random field in terms of an adequate isonormal process over a space of Hermitian functions. To this end, consider the
real
Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
generated by the class

\left\{h\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(S^{d-1},\sigma):h(-\theta)=\overline{h(\theta)}\right\},

endowed with the scalar product
\langle h,g\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{S^{d-1}}h(\theta)\overline{g(\theta)}\,\sigma(d\theta)
(which is real-valued on
\mathfrak{H}
), and let
W=\{W(h):h\in\mathfrak{H}\}
be an
isonormal Gaussian process
over
\mathfrak{H}
, as defined in
[
74
, Section 2.1]
. For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we set

h_{x}(\theta)=e^{i\langle x,\theta\rangle};

then,
h_{x}
belongs trivially to
\mathfrak{H}
and — for the rest of the proof and without loss of generality — we may assume that
B
is realized as

B(x)=W(h_{x}),

where we use the fact that (consistently with (
1.2
))

\mathbb{E}[B(x)B(y)]=\mathbb{E}[W(h_{x})W(h_{y})]=\langle h_{x},h_{y}\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{S^{d-1}}e^{i\langle x-y,\theta\rangle}\,\sigma(d\theta).

Elementary considerations also show that, for an ordered multi-index
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{j})\in\{1,...,d\}^{j}
, one has

\partial^{\alpha}B(x)=\partial_{x_{\alpha_{1}}}\cdots\partial_{x_{\alpha_{j}}}B(x)=W(h_{x,\alpha}),\qquad h_{x,\alpha}(\theta)=i^{j}\theta^{\alpha}e^{i\langle x,\theta\rangle},

where
\theta^{\alpha}=\theta_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\theta_{\alpha_{j}}
, and
h_{x,\alpha}\in\mathfrak{H}
. We can now use the standard product formula stated in
[
74
, Theorem 2.7.10]
to infer that

\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}=I_{2}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,...,d\}^{j}}h_{x,\alpha}\otimes h_{x,\alpha}\right),

where
I_{2}
indicates a second order multiple Wiener-Itô integral with respect to
W
, the sum is over all ordered multiindices
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{j})\in\{1,...,d\}^{j}
, and one has the explicit representation

\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,...,d\}^{j}}(h_{x,\alpha}\otimes h_{x,\alpha})(\theta,\eta)=e^{i\langle x,\theta+\eta\rangle}\sum_{\alpha\in\{1,...,d\}^{j}}i^{j}\theta^{\alpha}\,i^{j}\eta^{\alpha}=e^{i\langle x,\theta+\eta\rangle}(-1)^{j}\langle\theta,\eta\rangle^{j}.

It follows that

Y_{R}=I_{2}(g_{R}),

where

g_{R}(\theta,\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{R}(x)e^{i\langle x,\theta+\eta\rangle}\,dx\,P(\theta,\eta),

with

P(\theta,\eta)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}(-1)^{j}\langle\theta,\eta\rangle^{j}.

Equivalently,

g_{R}(\theta,\eta)=R^{d}\widehat{\varphi}\big(R(\theta+\eta)\big)P(\theta,\eta).

We observe that
g_{R}
is symmetric and Hermitian (that is,
g_{R}(-\theta,-\eta)=\overline{g_{R}(\theta,\eta)}
), and also that
P
is bounded and such that
P(\theta,-\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j},
and the first part of the forthcoming Lemma
4.2.1
yields that

(4.4)

\|g_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}=\frac{1}{2}{\bf Var}(Y_{R})\asymp R^{d+1},\quad R\to\infty.

Set

M_{R}:=\sup_{\theta\in S^{d-1}}\int_{S^{d-1}}|g_{R}(\theta,a)|\,\sigma(\differential a).

According to the second part of Lemma
4.2.1
, it is now sufficient to show that
M_{R}=o(R^{(d+1)/2})
. Since
P
is bounded,

|g_{R}(\theta,a)|\leq CR^{d}\left|\widehat{\varphi}(R(\theta+a))\right|,

where
C
is constant depending on
d
,
k
and the coefficients
c_{j}
. We estimate
M_{R}
by using Lemma
4.2.2
, exploiting the explicit distribution of

t=\langle\theta,a\rangle,

where
a
is a random element with law
\sigma
, and yielding the estimate

(4.5)

\int_{S^{d-1}}F(\|\theta+a\|)\,\sigma(da)\leq C\int_{0}^{2}F(s)s^{d-2}\,ds,

for every
F
nonnegative and nonincreasing, and some absolute constant only depending on the dimension
d
. If
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then, for all integers
N
, one has that

|\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)|\leq C_{N}(1+\|\xi\|)^{-N}.

Choosing
N>d-1
and
F(r)=(1+Rr)^{-N}
in (
4.5
), one obtains

M_{R}\leq CR,\qquad\|T_{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(R).

If
\varphi=\mathbf{1}_{K}
with
K
bounded, convex, having a
C^{2}
boundary and with non-vanishing Gauss curvature, then

|\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{K}}(\xi)|\leq C(1+\|\xi\|)^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}

(see e.g.
[
22
]
and the references therein), and analogous computations yield

\|T_{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\begin{cases}CR,&d=2,\\
CR\log R,&d=3,\\
CR^{\frac{d-1}{2}},&d\geq 4.\end{cases}

The proof of Theorem
2.4.1
is concluded.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

Fix
d\geq 2
. Let
\mu
be a symmetric
10
10

10

That is,
\mu
is such that
\mu(A)=\mu(-A)
for all measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
Borel finite measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and consider the real Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
generated by the class

\left\{h\in L_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu):h(-y)=\overline{h(y)}\right\},

endowed with the real-valued scalar product

\langle h,g\rangle_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{R^{d}}h(y)\overline{g(y)}\,\mu(\differential y).

Let
W=\{W(h):h\in\mathfrak{H}\}
be an isonormal Gaussian process over
\mathfrak{H}
, as defined in
[
74
, Section 2.1]
. Let
\{g_{R}:R>0\}\subset\mathfrak{H}^{\odot 2}
and consider the corresponding sequence of double stochastic Wiener-Itô integrals (with respect to
W
)
\{I_{2}(g_{R}):R>0\}
. Then,

(4.6)

{\bf Var}(I_{2}(g_{R}))=2\|g_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}.

Moreover, if

(4.7)

M_{R}:=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,y\in{\rm supp}(\mu)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,\mu(\differential a)=o\big(\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}\big),\quad R\to\infty,

one has that
G_{R}:=(\sqrt{2}\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}})^{-1}\,I_{2}(g_{R})
converges in distribution to a standard Gaussian random variable.

Proof.

Relation (
4.6
) is a direct consequence of the usual isometry properties of multiple stochastic integrals (see
[
74
, Proposition 2.7.5]
).
Now introduce the (symmetric and Hermitian)
contraction kernel
(see
[
74
, Section B.4]
)

\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\ni(\theta,\eta)\mapsto g_{R}\otimes_{1}g_{R}(x,y):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{R}(x,a)g_{R}(y,-a)\mu(\differential a).

According to the Fourth Moment Theorem for multiple integrals stated in
[
74
, Theorem 5.2.7]
, one has that the asymptotic Gaussianity of
G_{R}
is equivalent to the relation

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\|g_{R}\otimes_{1}g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}}{\|g_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}}=0.

Now let
T_{R}
be the Hilbert-Schmidt operator on
\mathfrak{H}
defined by

(T_{R}h)(y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}g_{R}(y,a)h(-a)\,\mu(da),

and denote by
\{\lambda_{j}:j\geq 1\}
the sequence of its eigenvalues (note that
T_{R}
is self-adjoint on
\mathfrak{H}
, since
g_{R}
is symmetric). Then,
\|T_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|g_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}^{2}
, and

\|g_{R}\otimes_{1}g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}^{2}=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}^{4}\leq\|T_{R}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}^{2}

(see
[
74
, Section 2.7.4]
for details), that is,

\frac{\|g_{R}\otimes_{1}g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}}{\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}^{2}}\leq\frac{\|T_{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}}.

We claim that
\|T_{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq M_{R}
. To see this, we use a self-contained argument that corresponds to a version of the well-known
Schur’s test
for integral operators (see e.g.
[
43
]
). For every
h\in\mathfrak{H}
, one has

|(T_{R}h)(y)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,|h(-a)|\,\mu(\differential a).

By Cauchy–Schwarz, for every
y\in{\rm supp}(\mu)
,

\displaystyle|(T_{R}h)(y)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,\mu(\differential a)\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,|h(-a)|^{2}\,\mu(\differential a)\right)

\displaystyle\leq M_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,|h(-a)|^{2}\,\mu(\differential a).

Integrating with respect to
y
, and using Fubini, yields

\displaystyle\|T_{R}h\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,|h(-a)|^{2}\,\mu(\differential a)\mu(\differential y)

\displaystyle=M_{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|h(-a)|^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,\mu(\differential y)\right)\mu(\differential a).

Since
g_{R}
is symmetric in its two variables, the inner integral is also bounded by
M_{R}
. Hence,

\|T_{R}h\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq M_{R}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|h(-a)|^{2}\,\mu(\differential a)=M_{R}^{2}\|h\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

where we used the invariance of
\mu
under
a\mapsto-a
. Taking the supremum over
h\neq 0
, we obtain
\|T_{R}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq M_{R},

∎

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
d\geq 2
, and let
a
be a random element distributed according to the uniform probability measure
\sigma
on

S^{d-1}
, and fix
\theta\in S^{d-1}
. Then the law of

t=\langle\theta,a\rangle

does not depend on
\theta
, and has density

c_{d}(1-t^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\mathbf{1}_{(-1,1)}(t),

where
c_{d}
is a normalizing constant. Moreover, if

s=\|\theta+a\|,

then for every nonnegative nonincreasing function
F
,

\int_{S^{d-1}}F(\|\theta+a\|)\,\sigma(da)\leq C_{d}\int_{0}^{2}F(s)s^{d-2}\,ds,

for some constant
C_{d}>0
depending only on the dimension.

Proof.

The fact that the law of
t=\langle\theta,a\rangle
does not depend on
\theta

follows from the rotation invariance of
\sigma
. Hence we may assume without loss of generality that
\theta=e_{d}=(0,...,0,1)
, so that

t=a_{d}
. It is well known that
U=a_{d}^{2}
has a beta distribution with parameters

\frac{1}{2}
and
\frac{d-1}{2}
. Therefore
U
has density

\frac{1}{B\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}u^{-1/2}(1-u)^{\frac{d-3}{2}},\qquad 0<u<1.

Since
a_{d}
is symmetric and
U=t^{2}
, a standard change of variables shows
that
t=a_{d}
has density

c_{d}(1-t^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}},\qquad-1<t<1

(see
[
33
, formula (1.26)]
for more details). Now set
s=\|\theta+a\|
. Since

\|\theta+a\|^{2}=2(1+\langle\theta,a\rangle)=2(1+t),

it follows that the law of
s
has density proportional to

s^{d-2}\left(1-\frac{s^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{d-3}{2}},\qquad 0<s<2.

Let
F
be nonnegative and nonincreasing. Then

\int_{S^{d-1}}F(\|\theta+a\|)\,\sigma(da)=\int_{0}^{2}F(s)\,\rho_{d}(s)\,ds,

where
\rho_{d}(s)
is the density of
s
above. Since

\rho_{d}(s)=C_{d}s^{d-2}\left(1-\frac{s^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{d-3}{2}},

and the latter factor is bounded on
(0,2)
for
d\geq 3
, the desired estimate
follows immediately in that case. For
d=2
, one has

\rho_{2}(s)\asymp\left(1-\frac{s^{2}}{4}\right)^{-1/2},

which has an integrable singularity at
s=2
. Splitting the integral e.g. into

(0,1)
and
(1,2)
, and using that
F
is nonincreasing, one still obtains

\int_{0}^{2}F(s)\rho_{2}(s)\,ds\leq C\int_{0}^{2}F(s)\,ds,

which coincides with the desired bound.
∎

4.3.
General analysis of critical points

We will now move towards the proofs of Proposition
2.4.3
(given in Sections
4.5.2
), Proposition
2.4.5
(see Section
4.5.3
) and Theorem
2.4.7
(see Section
4.5.1
). Since the most technically demanding (and innovative) part of our proofs revolves around the asymptotic analysis of critical points of smooth Gaussian functions, we devote the present section and the subsequent Section
4.4
to the study of critical points of Gaussian fields satisfying the following set of generic assumptions.

Assumption A
.

Fix
d\geq 2
. In what follows we denote by

f=\{f(x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}

a real, centered stationary Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with covariance function

r(x):=\mathbb{E}[f(x)f(0)]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\langle\xi,x\rangle}\omega(\differential\xi),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(where
\omega
is the spectral measure of
f
), and satisfying the following assumptions:

(a)

f
is a.s. of class
\mathcal{C}^{3}
;

(b)

\lim_{\|x\|\to\infty}r(x)=0
;

(c)

the random element
(f(x),\sqrt{d}\nabla f(x))
is a standard Gaussian vector so that, in particular,

r(0)=1\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla^{2}r(0)=-\frac{1}{d}\mathrm{Id}.

(d)

the measure
\omega
either has a non-zero part that is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or it has a smooth density with respect to the surface measure of a centered sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

As in the statement of Proposition
2.4.3
, we write

{\bf CP}(u)={\bf CP}(u,f):=\left\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \nabla f(x)=0\;\text{and}\;f(x)\geq u\right\},\quad u\in\mathbb{R},

to indicate the set of critical points of
f
above
u
and we observe that, under

A
this set forms a.s. a locally finite simple point process (see
[
2
, Sec. 11.2]
). Given a nonnegative test function
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1
, we focus on the observable

X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)=\sum_{x\in{\bf CP}(u,f)}\varphi(x),

and observe that such a definition extends trivially to a generic
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}
whenever the above sum is a.s. absolutely converging (which is always the case under
A
). In this section, we are interested in formulas for the expectation
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)]
and for the second factorial moment

(4.8)

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)^{[2]}]=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi^{2})]

(which is always well-defined under
A
. To this end, we first recall the
co-area formula
(see e.g.
[
34
, Theorem 3.2.12]
, as well as
[
2
, Sec. 11.2]
), yielding that one has the formal identity

(4.9)

X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\delta_{0}(\nabla f(x))|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\differential x,

that one has to regard as an a.s. convergence result, obtained by replacing
\delta_{0}
by a sequence of appropriate mollificators.

4.3.1.
Kac–Rice formulae

Roughly speaking, Kac–Rice formulae (see
[
2
,
7
,
39
,
6
]
) are analytical devices allowing one to take expectations on both sides of relations such as (
4.9
), and then to apply a formal Fubini-type argument to the right-hand side, thereby representing expectations as integrals of suitable conditional kernels. The next statement consists in an application of Kac-Rice techniques to evaluate the quantities
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)]
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)^{[2]}]
introduced above (see, in particular, (
4.8
)). Given a test function
\varphi
, we recall the notation
\psi=\varphi*\varphi^{-}
introduced in (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
A
prevail and let
\varphi
be a test function as in Remark
4.0.1
. Then, one has that

(4.10)

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)]=\frac{d^{d/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}[|\det\nabla^{2}f(0)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(0))]\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\differential x,

and

(4.11)

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)^{[2]}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\rho_{u}(x)\differential x,

with

(4.12)

\rho_{u}(x)=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[|\det\nabla^{2}f(0)||\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(0))\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\,\middle|\,\nabla f(0)=\nabla f(x)=0\right]}{\sqrt{\det 2\pi{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(\nabla f(0),\nabla f(x))}}.

Proof.

When the spectral measure
\omega
has a non-zero part that is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the theorem is a direct consequence of
[
2
, Thm 11.2.1, Cor. 11.5.2]
, the non-degeneracy condition can be inferred for instance from
[
7
, Ex. 3.4]
. In the case where the spectral measure
\omega
has a smooth density with respect to the surface measure of a centered sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of radius
\lambda
, note that the vector
(f(x),\nabla^{2}f(x))
is degenerate, from the relation
\Delta f(x)+\lambda^{2}f(x)=0
. Nevertheless,
[
2
, Thm 11.2.1]
and
[
2
, Cor. 11.5.1]
can still be applied since at a fixed
x
, the vector
\nabla f(x)
is independent of
(f(x),\nabla^{2}f(x))
.
∎

The function
\rho_{u}
has been proved to be integrable near the origin (despite the obvious singularity at
x=0
) under
A
(see
[
39
, Thm. 1.1]
). Such a result will also follow as a corollary of the quantitative estimates derived in the forthcoming Section
4.4.2
.

4.3.2.
Chaotic expansion

Given a square-integrable random variable
F=F(f)
(that is,
F
is measurable with respect to the
\sigma
-field generated by the Gaussian field
f
), one always has that
F
admits a
Wiener-Itô chaos expansion
, meaning that
F
has a unique representation of the form

(4.13)

F=\mathbb{E}[F]+\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}F^{(q)},

where the series converges in square-mean, and
F^{(q)}:={\rm proj}(F\,|\,C_{q})
is the projection of
F
onto the
q
th
Wiener chaos

C_{q}
of
f
, as defined e.g. in
[
74
, Section 2.2]
.
11
11

11

Observe that, if
f
is represented as a subset of an isonormal Gaussian process
W
over some Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
(as we did for
f=B
in the proof of Theorem
2.4.1
), then
{\bf F}^{(q)}
admits a unique representation of the form
{\bf F}^{(q)}=I_{q}(f_{q})
, where
I_{q}
is a multiple Wiener-Itô integral of order
q
with respect to
W
, and
f_{q}\in\mathfrak{H}^{\odot q}
. See again
[
74
, Chapter 2]
.
A rigorous introduction to Wiener chaos expansions can be found in
[
74
, Chapter 2]
, as well as in
[
76
, Chapter 1]
or
[
53
, Chapter 2]
. Since the functionals of the Gaussian field
f
considered here are local functionals, the analysis of their chaotic decompositions can be made considerably simpler than in the general case. The following discussion roughly follows the approach developed in
[
41
]
. Before we derive the chaotic decomposition of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)
, we need to introduce some further notation.

It is easily checked that, under
A
and for fixed
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the Gaussian vectors
f(x)
,
\nabla f(x)
and
\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}
are mutually independent Gaussian vectors. The vector
\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}
is not necessarily a standard Gaussian element on the space of symmetric matrices (endowed with the Frobenius norm) on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Denoting by
T
the square root of the covariance matrix of the vector
\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}
(which is independent of
x
), one can define
D(x)
as the standard Gaussian vector taking values in the support of
\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}
, satisfying the relation

(4.14)

TD(x)=\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}

The stationary Gaussian field

(4.15)

W(x)=(f(x),\sqrt{d}\nabla f(x),D(x))

can then be identified, at fixed
x
, with a standard Gaussian vector of dimension
m
, with

(4.16)

m=1+d+\dim\left(\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}\right).

In the isotropic case, the transformation
T
can be made explicit, see for instance
[
41
, Lemma 4.10]
. In particular, for Berry’s random wave, one has the following lemma.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
\tau=\frac{1}{d(d+2)}
, and let
f=B
be defined as in (
1.1
)—(
1.2
). Then,

D(x):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\tau}}\left(\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}\right)

is a standard Gaussian element over the space of traceless symmetric matrices on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(equipped with the Frobenius scalar product).

Proof.

Note first that

\Tr(\nabla^2f(x) + \frac{1}{d}f(x)\Id)=\Delta f(x)+f(x)=0,

so that this random matrix lives in the space of traceless symmetric matrices. An elementary covariance computation yields that for a traceless symmetric matrix
A
,

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\nabla^{2}f(x)+\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id},A\right\rangle^{2}\right]=2\tau\|A\|^{2},

and the conclusion follows.
∎

Now let
f
be as in
A
. For
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
F_{u}
the generalized function defined by

\displaystyle F_{u}(W(x))

\displaystyle:=\delta_{0}(\nabla f(x))|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))

(4.17)

\displaystyle=\delta_{0}(\nabla f(x))\left|\det\left(TD(x)-\frac{1}{d}f(x)\mathrm{Id}\right)\right|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x)).

We also recall the following standard fact (see
[
74
, Chapter 2]
): given a square-integrable random variable
F=F(W(x))
, its Wiener-Itô chaos expansion (
4.13
) is given by

(4.18)

F(W(x))=\sum_{q=0}^{+\infty}F^{(q)}(W(x))=\sum_{q=0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{q!}\langle F^{(q)},H_{W(x)}^{(q)}\rangle,

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
stands for the Frobenius scalar product,
y\mapsto H_{y}^{(q)}\in(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{\otimes q}
is the (symmetric) tensor-valued mapping defined by

H_{y}^{(q)}:=(-1)^{q}e^{\frac{\|y\|^{2}}{2}}\nabla^{q}\left(y\mapsto e^{-\frac{\|y\|^{2}}{2}}\right),\quad\mbox{and}\quad F^{(q)}:=\mathbb{E}[F(W(x))H_{W(x)}^{(q)}].

The next statement exploits representation (
4.18
) to derive the Wiener chaos expansion of the random variable
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Let
A
prevail, and let
\varphi
be as in Remark
4.0.1
. Then, for every
u\in\mathbb{R}
, the quantity

(4.19)

F_{u}^{(q)}:=\mathbb{E}[F_{u}(W(x))H_{W(x)}^{(q)}],

where
F_{u}(W(x))
is defined in (
4.17
), is a well-defined symmetric element of
(\mathbb{R}^{m})^{\otimes q}
, where
m
is defined in (
4.16
) . Moreover, for
q\geq 1
, the projection of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)
onto the
q
-th Wiener chaos
C_{q}
is given by

(4.20)

X_{1}^{(q)}(f,u,\varphi)=\frac{1}{q!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\langle F_{u}^{(q)},H_{W(x)}^{(q)}\rangle\differential x=\frac{1}{q!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)F_{u}^{(q)}(W(x))\rangle\differential x,

where we used the notation

(4.21)

F_{u}^{(q)}(W(x)):=\frac{1}{q!}\langle F_{u}^{(q)},H_{W(x)}^{(q)}\rangle,\quad q\geq 1.

Proof.

It directly follows from the co-area formula (
4.9
) and the linearity of chaotic decomposition, as done in
[
41
, Sec. 2.3]
).
∎

The next two statements provide an explicit expression for the quantities
F^{(1)}(W(x))
and
F^{(2)}(W(x))
defined in (
4.21
). They are both valid under the assumptions of Lemma
4.3.3
.

Lemma 4.3.4
.

One has that

\displaystyle F_{u}^{(1)}(W(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{d/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[f(x)|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right]f(x)

\displaystyle+\frac{d^{d/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[D(x)|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right],D(x)\right\rangle.

If
f=B
, as defined in (
1.1
)—(
1.2
), the second term in the previous sum vanishes.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

One has that

\displaystyle F_{u}^{(2)}(W(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{d/2}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[(f(x)^{2}-1)|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right](f(x)^{2}-1)

\displaystyle-\frac{d^{d/2+1}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right](\|\nabla f(x)\|^{2}-1)

\displaystyle+\frac{d^{d/2}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[(D(x)^{\otimes 2}-\mathrm{Id})|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right],(D(x)^{\otimes 2}-\mathrm{Id})\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{d^{d/2}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\left\langle\mathbb{E}\left[f(x)D(x)|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right],f(x)D(x)\right\rangle.

If
f=B
, as defined in (
1.1
)—(
1.2
), then the fourth term in the previous sum vanishes, and the third term is equal to

\frac{d^{d/2}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{\|D(x)\|^{2}}{c}-1\right)|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right](\|D(x)\|^{2}-c),

with
D(x)
given by Lemma
4.3.2
and
c=\frac{(d-1)(d+2)}{2}
.

Unified proofs of Lemmas
4.3.4
and
4.3.5
.

The proof of formulas of the above two lemmas is straightforward from the definition, using for a continuous function
M
with polynomial growth the identity:

\mathbb{E}[M(W(x))\delta_{0}(\nabla f(x))]=\frac{d^{d/2}}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}[M(W(x))|\nabla f(x)=0].

For the isotropic random wave model, recall the expression of
D(x)
in
4.3.2
. The second chaotic projection must be invariant to isometric transformations of the Hessian. Up to a constant, the only linear form on symmetric matrices that is invariant by orthogonal transformation is the trace, which is zero for the hesHessian random waves. From this fact th,e cancellations above are deduced. Similarly, up to a constant, the only bilinear form in the space of traceless symmetric matrices invariant by isometry is the Frobenius norm, which yields the last formula in the Lemma
4.3.5
.
∎

4.3.3.
Variance bounds

Let
\Omega
be the covariance function of the
m
-dimensional Gaussian field
W
introduced in (
4.15
). The Kac density
\rho_{u}
(defined in formula (
4.12
) of Lemma
4.3.1
) evaluated at point
x
is actually a functional of the covariance function
\Omega(x)
so that one can write
\rho_{u}(x)=\tilde{\rho}_{u}(\Omega(x))
. It is straightforward to observe that the function
\tilde{\rho}_{u}
is analytic as soon as the Gaussian vector
(W(0),W(x))
is non-degenerate, which is satisfied for all
x\neq 0
, as a consequence of
A
. The Taylor expansion of
\tilde{\rho}_{u}
is given by the formula (see
[
41
, Sec. 2.2]
for details)

\tilde{\rho}_{u}(\Omega(x))=\mathbb{E}[F_{u}(W(0))F_{u}(W(x))]=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{q!}\Omega(x)^{\otimes q}(F_{u}^{(q)},F_{u}^{(q)}),

which implies, after integration and thanks to (
4.11
), the equality

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)^{[2]}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{q!}\Omega(x)^{\otimes q}(F_{u}^{(q)},F_{u}^{(q)})\differential x.

Remark 4.3.6
.

it is important to remark that one cannot exchange the sum and the integral in the above formula. Doing so will account to the usual second moment of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)
instead of the second factorial moment, since the second moment of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)
is the sum of the second moments of its chaotic projections, given by Lemma
4.3.3
.

The following useful result is a direct consequence of the bound of the remainder in the Taylor expansion near the origin of the function
\tilde{\rho}_{u}
.

Lemma 4.3.7
.

Let
x\neq 0
. Then

\left|\rho_{u}(x)-\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\frac{1}{k!}\Omega(x)^{\otimes k}(F_{u}^{(k)},F_{u}^{(k)})\right|\leq\frac{1}{q!}\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\nabla^{q}\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t\Omega(x))\|\times\|\Omega(x)\|^{q},

where the norms on the right-hand side of the previous expression are considered in appropriate spaces of tensors and matrices.

4.4.
Level dependence

In this section, we make the dependence on the level
u
of the various quantities introduced in the previous section more explicit. Our main objective is to investigate their asymptotic behavior in the regime
u\to\infty
.

4.4.1.
Chaotic decompositions

We next state a lemma describing the general mechanism by which large-
u
asymptotics can be obtained through the Laplace method, which is the object of the next Lemma. Variants of this statement will be used repeatedly throughout the remainder of the section.

Lemma 4.4.1
.

Let
A
, as well as the notation introduced in Section
4.3.2
, prevail. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
h
be a differentiable function on
\mathbb{R}
, such that, for large
u
, one has
h(u)\simeq Cu^{\alpha}
and
h^{\prime}(u)=o(h(u))
. Then

\int_{u}^{+\infty}h(v)e^{-\frac{v^{2}}{2}}\differential v=\frac{h(u)}{u}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right)

As a consequence, if
G
is a function with polynomial growth, one has that

\mathbb{E}[f(x)^{\alpha}G(D(x))|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))]=\mathbb{E}[G(D(x))]u^{\alpha+d-1}\frac{1}{d^{d}\sqrt{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right).

Proof.

Observe the equality

\displaystyle\int_{u}^{+\infty}h(v)e^{-\frac{v^{2}}{2}}\differential v

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{u^{2}}{2}}}{u}\int_{0}^{+\infty}h\left(u,+\frac{t}{u}\right)e^{-t}e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2u^{2}}}\differential t

the difference between
h(u)
and
h(u,+t/u)
is a
o(h(u)/u)
uniformly on compacts of
t
, and the conclusion follows from dominated convergence. For the second statement, observe that one has

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(x)^{\alpha}G(D(x))|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{u}^{+\infty}v^{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\left[G(D(x))\left|\det TD(x)-\frac{v}{d}\mathrm{Id}\right|\right]e^{-\frac{v^{2}}{2}}\differential v.

As
u
grows large,
\mathbb{E}[G(D(x))|\det TD(x)-\frac{u}{d}\mathrm{Id}|]\simeq\frac{u^{d}}{d^{d}}\mathbb{E}[G(D(x))]
and the conclusion follows from the previous statement.
∎

The following statement is a consequence of the previous lemma.

Lemma 4.4.2
.

Under
A
, and for
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1
, one has that, as
u\to\infty
,

(4.22)

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi)]=\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,\differential x\right)\frac{1}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{(d+1)/2}}u^{d-1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right),

where the constants involved in the
O(\cdot)
notation are independent of
\varphi
.

Proof.

The conclusion follows by combining (
4.10
) with Lemma
4.4.1
.
∎

We define the constant

\gamma_{u}:=\frac{u^{d+1}}{2d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}.

The above Lemma
4.4.2
implies the following lemma about large
u
asymptotics of the projection on the second chaos of critical points.

Lemma 4.4.3
.

Let
A
prevail and recall the notation introduced in (
4.21
). Then, there is a constant
C
such that, for
u>0
,

\left|\mathbb{E}[F_{u}^{(2)}(W(0))F_{u}^{(2)}(W(x))]-\gamma_{u}^{2}\mathbb{E}[(f(0)^{2}-1)(f(x)^{2}-1)]\right|\leq C\frac{\gamma_{u}^{2}}{u^{2}}\|\Omega(x)\|^{2},

where the covariance function
\Omega
was defined in Section
4.3.3
. In particular, for
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1
,

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(X_{1}^{(2)}(f,u,\varphi)-\gamma_{u}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)(f(x)^{2}-1)\differential x\right)\leq C\frac{\gamma_{u}^{2}}{u^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi(x)|\times\|\Omega(x)\|^{2}\differential x,

where the norm is Frobenius, and we have used (
4.1
).

Proof.

The first statement directly follows by observing that the mapping

(v,w)\mapsto\mathbb{E}[F_{u}^{(2)}(W(0))F_{v}^{(2)}(W(x))|f(0)=v,f(x)=w]

is analytic in
v,w
. From Lemma
4.4.1
, one deduces that, for the first coefficient in the expression of
F^{(2)}(W(x))
given in Lemma
4.3.5
, one has the asymptotic relations

\frac{d^{d/2}}{2(2\pi)^{d/2}}\mathbb{E}\left[(f(x)^{2}-1)|\det( TD(x) + f(x)\Id)|\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x))\right]=\gamma_{u}+O(u^{d}).

The remaining three coefficients appearing in the expression of
F^{(2)}(W(x))
given in Lemma
4.3.5
are all
O(u^{d})
, as one can deduce from a further application of Lemma
4.4.1
. The first part of the statement now follows from a direct covariance computation. To prove the second part of the statement, we use Lemma
4.3.3
and exploit the identity

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(X_{1}^{(2)}(f,u,\varphi)-\gamma_{u}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)(f(x)^{2}-1)\differential x\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\left(\mathbb{E}[F_{u}^{(2)}(W(0))F_{u}^{(2)}(W(x))]-\gamma_{u}^{2}\mathbb{E}[(f(0)^{2}-1)(f(x)^{2}-1)]\right)\differential x.

The conclusion follows from the first point.
∎

4.4.2.
Near-diagonal estimates in the Kac density

The goal of this subsection is to prove the integrability of the Kac density given in (
4.12
) near
0
, by explicitly tracking the dependence in
u
. We start with a useful lemma, and recall the general notation
\psi=\varphi*\varphi^{-}
introduced in (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.4.4
.

Let
A
prevail, and let
e
be a unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, the vector
\nabla^{2}f\cdot e
is non-degenerate.

Proof.

For a non-zero vector
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(\nabla^{2}f\cdot e)\cdot(v,v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\xi\cdot e)^{2}(\xi\cdot v)^{2}\omega(\differential\xi).

This quantity never cancels as soon as
\omega
is not supported on a union of two hyperplanes, which is not the case from our assumptions on the field
f
.
∎

Lemma 4.4.5
.

Under
A
and for
\eta
small enough, there is a constant
C
and a positive constant
\varepsilon
, such that
\|x\|\leq\eta
,

\rho_{u}(x)\leq\frac{C}{\|x\|^{d-2}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}.

In particular, for
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1

\int_{B(0,\eta)}\psi(x)\rho_{u}(x)\differential x\leq C\eta^{2}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}.

Proof.

We define the quantities

h(x)=\frac{f(0)+f(x)}{2},\quad g(x)=\frac{f(x)-f(0)}{\|x\|}

\quad H(x)=\frac{\nabla f(0)+\nabla f(x)}{2},\quad G(x)=\frac{\nabla f(x)-\nabla f(0)}{\|x\|}

P(x)=\left(\nabla^{2}f_{e_{x}^{\perp}}(0),\frac{\partial_{e_{x}}^{2}f(0)-G(x)}{\|x\|}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad Q(x)=\left(\nabla^{2}f_{e_{x}^{\perp}}(x),\frac{\partial_{e_{x}}^{2}f(x)-G(x)}{\|x\|}\right)

Notice that all these quantities have a.s. defined limits as
\|x\|
goes to zero and
x/\|x\|
converges to a limit vector
e
. One has the equality

\displaystyle\rho_{u}(x)=\frac{1}{\|x\|^{d-2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[|\det P(x)||\det Q(x)|\mathds{1}_{\{h(x)-u\geq|g(x)|/2\}}\,\middle|\,H(x)=0,\;G(x)=0\right]}{\sqrt{\det\left[2\pi{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(G(x),H(x))\right]}}.

Notice that the Gaussian vector
(h(x),G(x))
is independent from
(g(x),H(x))
. Their variances are given by

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(h(x),G(x))=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1+r(x)}{2}&\frac{\nabla r(x)^{T}}{\|x\|}\\
\frac{\nabla r(x)^{T}}{\|x\|}&-2\frac{\nabla^{2}r(0)-\nabla^{2}r(x)}{\|x\|^{2}}\end{pmatrix},\;{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(g(x),H(x))=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1-r(x)}{\|x\|^{2}}&-\frac{\nabla r(x)^{T}}{\|x\|}\\
-\frac{\nabla r(x)}{\|x\|}&-\frac{\nabla^{2}r(0)+\nabla^{2}r(x)}{2}\end{pmatrix}

Gaussian regression implies that

h(x)=\underset{\tilde{h}(x)}{\underbrace{h(x)-\langle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(G(x))^{-1}\nabla r(x),G(x)\rangle}}+\langle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(G(x))^{-1}\nabla r(x),G(x)\rangle,

where both terms are independent. In particular the distribution of
h(x)
conditionally to
G(x)=0
is
\tilde{h}(x)
. Moreover,

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(\tilde{h}(x))={\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(h(x))-\nabla r(x)^{T}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(G(x))^{-1}\nabla r(x).

As
\|x\|
goes to 0 and
x/\|x\|
converges to
e

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(\tilde{h}(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{1+r(x)}{2}-\nabla r(x)^{T}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(G(x))^{-1}\nabla r(x)

\displaystyle\simeq 1-e^{T}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}((\nabla^{2}f\cdot e)^{-1})e

\displaystyle<1,

where the last inequality follows from the previous Lemma
4.4.4
. One can then choose positive constant
\eta,\delta
such that

\forall\|x\|\leq\eta,\quad{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(\tilde{h}(x))\leq 1-\delta\quad\text{so that}\quad\mathbb{P}(\tilde{h}(x)\geq u)\leq e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2(1-\delta)}}.

One has

\displaystyle\rho_{u}(x)\leq\frac{e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2(1-\delta)}}}{\|x\|^{d-2}}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[|\det P(x)||\det Q(x)|\,\middle|\,H(x)=0,\;G(x)=0,\;\tilde{h}(x)\geq u\right]}{\det\left[2\pi\mathrm{Cov}(H(x),G(x))\right]}.

Notice that the covariance of
(P(x),Q(x))
conditionally to
H(x),G(x),\tilde{h}(x)
is uniformly bounded in
x
, since they all have a definite limit as
\|x\|
goes to
0
and
x/\|x\|
converges to a unit vector
e
. Applying Laplace method in Lemma
4.4.1
to this quantity, it follows that one can bound the expectation by a polynomial in
u
, yielding the bound

\rho_{u}(x)\leq P(u)\frac{e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2(1-\delta)}}}{\|x\|^{d-2}}.

The first part of the lemma follows by choosing
\varepsilon
accordingly. The second part of the lemma follows by integration of the first bound and using the inequality

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\leq\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2},

yielding the desired conclusion.
∎

4.4.3.
Off-diagonal estimates in the Kac density

The following result allows one to estimate the Kac density (
4.12
) for values of
x
that are far from the origin.

Lemma 4.4.6
.

Under
A
, let
\eta
be a positive constant. Then, there is a positive constant
\varepsilon
such that, for
\|x\|\geq\eta
, one has

\rho_{u}(x)\leq C_{\eta}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}.

In particular, for
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1
,

\int_{B(0,A)\setminus B(0,\eta)}\psi(x)\rho_{u}(x)\differential x\leq CA^{d}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}

Proof.

The idea of proof is very similar to the one of Lemma
4.4.5
. The non-degeneracy condition of the field
f
implies that one can find
\delta>0
such that for all
x
with
\|x\|\geq\eta
, one has
\mathrm{Cov}(f(0),f(x)|\nabla f(0)=\nabla f(x)=0)<1-\delta
. It follows that

\mathbb{P}(f(0)\geq u,f(x)\geq u|\nabla f(0)=\nabla f(x)=0)\leq\frac{C}{u^{2}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{1+\delta}}.

Applying Laplace method in Lemma
4.4.1
to
\rho_{u}
we deduce that for some polynomial
P
, the following bound holds

\rho_{u}(x)\leq P(u)e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{1+\delta}}.

The first part of the lemma follows from an adequate choice of
\varepsilon
, and the second part by integration on
B(0,A)\setminus B(0,\eta)
.
∎

The following statement uses once again the notation (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.4.7
.

Let the assumptions of Lemma
4.4.5
prevail. For all
\varepsilon
positive, there is a positive constant
\eta
and a constant
C
, such that if
\|\Omega\|\leq\eta
, then

\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\|\nabla^{q}\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t\Omega)\|\leq Ce^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}.

In particular, there is a constant
A
such that for
\|x\|\geq A
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)}\psi(x)\left|\rho_{u}(x)-\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\frac{1}{k!}\Omega(x)^{\otimes k}(F_{u}^{(k)},F_{u}^{(k)})\right|\differential x\leq Ce^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)}\!\!\psi(x)\|\Omega(x)\|^{q}\differential x

Proof.

We observe the quantity

\nabla^{q}\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t\Omega)=\nabla^{q}\left(\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{E}[F_{u}(V)F_{u}(W)]\right),

where
V,W
are standard Gaussian vectors with covariance
\Omega
. Recall that
V
can be decomposed as
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3}
, and similarly for
W
, which corresponds to the decomposition of
W(x)=(f(x),\sqrt{d}\nabla f(x),D(x))
. Taking derivatives with respect to
\Omega
we still obtain a Gaussian integral with respect to the density of
(V,W)
. The successive derivatives yield powers of
V
and
W
with coefficients that depend continuously on the covariance matrix of
(V,W)
in a neighborhood of the identity matrix, i.e., continuously on
\Omega
in a neighborhood of
0
, say a ball
B(0,\eta)
. It follows from the expression of the Kac density that there is a polynomial
P_{\eta}
such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{q}\tilde{\rho}_{u}(t\Omega)\|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[P_{\eta}(V,W)F_{u}(V)F_{u}(W)]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[P_{\eta}(V,W)F_{u}(V)F_{u}(W)|V_{1}\geq u,W_{1}\geq u]\mathbb{P}(V_{1}\geq u,W_{1}\geq u|V_{2}=W_{2}=0).

Now, by an application of the Laplace method in Lemma
4.4.1
, one finds that

\mathbb{P}(V_{1}\geq u,W_{1}\geq u|V_{2}=W_{2}=0)\leq\frac{C}{u^{2}}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{1+\delta}},

where
\delta=\mathrm{Cov}(V_{1},W_{1}|V_{2}=W_{2}=0)
. As for the conditional expectation, another application of the Laplace method yields that the expectation is bounded by a polynomial in
u
. Choosing eventually
\eta
small enough so that
\delta
is smaller than
\varepsilon
, one concludes the proof of the first part of the statement. The second part of the statement follows from Lemma
4.4.4
and the fact that
\Omega(x)
tends to zero as
\|x\|\rightarrow+\infty
, so that for some
A
big enough and
\|x\|\geq A
, one has
\|\Omega(x)\|\leq\eta
.

∎

We now collect all the previous estimates to state the following general bound on the second factorial moment of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})
. In the next statement,
A
is in force and
\varphi
is as in Remark (
4.0.1
).

Lemma 4.4.8
.

For all
\varepsilon
positive, there are constants
A,C
independent of
\varphi
such that

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(f,u,\varphi))-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi^{2})]-\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(k)}(f,u,\varphi))\right|

\displaystyle\leq CA^{d}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}+Ce^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)}\!\!\psi(x)\|\Omega(x)\|^{q}\differential x,

where the norm in the last summand is Frobenius, and we have used the notation (
4.1
).

Proof.

We use the equality

\displaystyle{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(f,u,\varphi))-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi^{2})]-\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(k)}(f,u,\varphi))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(x)\left(\rho_{u}(x)-\sum_{k=0}^{q-1}\frac{1}{k!}\Omega(x)^{\otimes k}(F_{u}^{(k)},F_{u}^{(k)})\right)\differential x

We split the domain of integration in three subdomains :
B(0,\eta)
,
B(0,A)\setminus B(0,\eta)
, and
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)
. The constants
\eta,A
are chosen accordingly to Lemmas
4.4.5
and
4.4.7
. On each of these domains, the bound follows from the second statements in Lemmas
4.4.5
,
4.4.6
and
4.4.7
, and the fact that Lemma
4.4.1
implies that
\|F_{u}^{(q)}\|
is bounded by
Cu^{d+q-1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}
.
∎

4.5.
End of the three remaining proofs

We will now focus on the proofs of Theorem
2.4.7
, Proposition
2.4.3
and Proposition
2.4.5
. These three results deal with the limit in distribution of
X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})
, where the level
u
is possibly dependent on
R
. Observe that, by virtue of Lemma
4.4.2
, one has that, for every
\varphi
as in Remark
4.0.1
.

\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})]=\frac{R^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\,\differential x}{d^{d/2}(2\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}u^{d-1}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right).

4.5.1.
Asymptotic results for fields with integrable covariance functions: Proof of Theorem
2.4.7

The proof is a consequence of the next two lemmas.

Lemma 4.5.1
.

If
\nabla^{j}r\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
j=0,\ldots,4
, then as
R
grow to infinity and
u
a fixed level, there exists a positive constant
C_{u}
such that

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R}))\simeq C_{u}R^{d},

and the observable
X_{1}(f,u,\cdot)
converges in the weak sense towards a Gaussian white noise.

Proof.

The proof is almost entirely contained in
[
41
]
, with the slight modification that we consider critical points above a level
u
(instead of all critical points). The argument for the finiteness and positivity of
C
through the analysis of the second chaos is in all points similar, as well as the Gaussian asymptotics via the fourth moment Theorem.
∎

Lemma 4.5.2
.

If
\nabla^{j}r\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
j=0\ldots 4
, then as
u,R
grow to infinity,

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R}))\simeq\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})]

Proof.

According to Lemma
4.4.8
applied with
q=1
,

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})^{[2]}]-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R})]^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq CA^{d}R^{d}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle+CR^{d}e^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)}\psi_{R}(x)\|\Omega(x)\|\differential x

Since
\psi_{R}
converges to
\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}
and
\Omega\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the last integral is bounded by
C\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}
. As
u,R
grow to infinity, the two terms are negligible compared to
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(f,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})]
and the conclusion follows.
∎

4.5.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.4.3

The proof is a consequence of Theorem
2.4.1
. We first prove the theorem for the observable
X_{1}(B,u,\cdot)
. Recall the expression of the chaotic decomposition of
X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})
given by Lemma
4.3.3
. According to Lemma
4.1.2
, the first chaotic projection satisfies

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(1)}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))=o(R^{d+1})

as
R
grows to infinity. Similarly, by Lemma
4.4.8
with
q=2
and the asymptotics given by Lemma
4.1.2

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left(\sum_{k=3}^{+\infty}X_{1}^{(k)}(B,u,\varphi_{R})\right)\leq CR^{d+\frac{1}{2}}=o(R^{d+1})

as
R
grows to infinity. Now Lemma
4.3.5
yields the existence of constant
c_{0}^{(u)},c_{1}^{(u)},c_{2}^{(u)}
such that

F_{u}^{(2)}(W(x))=c_{0}^{(u)}(f(x)^{2}-1)+c_{1}^{(u)}(\|\nabla f(x)\|^{2}-1)+c_{2}^{(u)}\left(\|\nabla^{2}f(x)\|^{2}-1\right),

which implies that

X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})=\sum_{j=0}^{2}c_{j}^{(u)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi_{R}(x)\left(\|\nabla^{j}B(x)\|^{2}-1\right)\differential x+T_{R},

where
T_{R}
is the sum of all chaotic components of
X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R})
except for the second, and whose variance is
o(R^{d+1})
as
R
grows to infinity. Note that the constants
c_{u}^{(i)}
are analytic in
u
, and according to the asymptotics established in Lemma
4.4.3
, one has as
u
goes to infinity the asymptotics

\sum_{j=0}^{2}c_{j}^{(u)}\sim\gamma_{u}.

It follows that this sum is not constant in
u
, and can then cancel only for a finite number of exceptional values of
u
. Outside of this exceptional subset of
\mathbb{R}
, one can directly apply Theorem
2.4.1
to establish that the critical points above
u
belong the universality class.

We quickly sketch the proofs for the three other observables
X_{\ell}(B,u,\cdot)
,
\ell=2,3,4
, which follow the same lines, up to minor modifications that we detail here. For the point processes of maxima above
u
given by
X_{2}(B,u,\cdot)
, Kac–Rice formula implies that the function
F_{u}
must be modified as

\widetilde{F}_{u}(W(x))=\delta_{0}(\nabla f(x))|\det\nabla^{2}f(x)|\mathds{1}_{\{-d\}}({\rm sign}\,\nabla^{2}f(x))\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x)),

where
\rm{sign}
denotes the signature of a symmetric matrix. The proof then follows the exact same lines for the point process of critical points above
u
. For the measure of the
u
-level given by the observable
X_{3}(B,u,\cdot)
, Kac–Rice formula implies that the function
F_{u}
must be modified as

\widetilde{F}_{u}(f(x),\nabla f(x))=\delta_{u}(f(x))\|\nabla f(x)\|.

The rest of the proof follows the same lines: one has the domination of the second chaotic projection, and Theorem
2.4.1
can be applied. For the volume of the excursion above
u
given by the observable
X_{4}(B,u,\cdot)
, the function
F_{u}
must be modified as

\widetilde{F}_{u}(f(x))=\mathds{1}_{[u,+\infty[}(f(x)),

and the conclusion again follows from the domination of the second chaotic projection and Theorem
2.4.1
.

4.5.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.4.5

Gathering Lemma
4.4.3
and
4.1.2
, the asymptotics of the variance of the second chaotic projection is given by

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(2)}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))=\gamma_{u}^{2}K_{d}^{2}R^{d+1}\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right)\simeq C_{d}R^{d+1}u^{2d+2}e^{-u^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{L}^{2}.

From Lemma
4.1.2
and the bound on
F_{u}^{(1)}
obtained by Lemma
4.4.1
, the following bound follows

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(1)}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))=u^{d}e^{-u^{2}}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{u}\right)\right)o(R^{d}).

Using Lemma
4.4.8
with
q=3
, and again the asymptotics of Lemma
4.1.2
we get

\displaystyle\left|{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))-\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})]-\sum_{k=0}^{2}{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(k)}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))\right|

\displaystyle\leq CR^{d}A^{d}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}+CR^{d}e^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus B(0,A)}\!\!\psi_{R}(x)\|\Omega(x)\|^{3}\differential x

\displaystyle\leq CR^{d}\|\varphi\|_{2}^{2}e^{-\frac{u^{2}}{2}(1+\varepsilon)}+CR^{d}e^{-u^{2}(1-\varepsilon)}\sqrt{R}.

It follows that, independently of the regime of
u,R
the first chaos and the tail are negligible with respect to
\mathbb{E}[X_{1}(B,u,\varphi_{R}^{2})]+{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}(X_{1}^{(2)}(B,u,\varphi_{R}))
, from which the conclusion follows.

5.
Fluctuations of Radon-Fourier coefficients

5.1.
The affine Grassmannian

Definition 5.1.1
.

We denote by
\mathbb{A}_{d}
the set of all affine lines in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and call it the
affine Grassmannian of lines
. Given a unit vector
u\in S^{d-1}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
\langle{r,u}\rangle=0
we identify the line

(5.1)

\ell:=\ell_{r,u}:=r+\mathbb{R}u\in\mathbb{A}_{d},\

We set the
origin
of such line
\ell
to be the point of minimal norm, which we denote as

(5.2)

\mathrm{o}_{\ell}:=\mathrm{arg}\min_{x\in\ell}|x|=r

We denote as
\mathcal{A}_{d}
the set of pairs
(\ell,v)\in\mathbb{A}_{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, such that
v\in\ell-\mathrm{o}_{\ell}
. Given
(r,u)
and
\ell
as above, for any
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

(5.3)

(\ell,v)_{r,u,\eta}:=\left(r+\mathbb{R}u,\eta u\right)\in\mathcal{A}_{d}

We denote by
TS^{d-1}
the space of pairs
(r,u)
defined as above: such that
u\in S^{d-1}
and
r\in u^{\perp}
.

12
12

12

\mathcal{A}_{d}
is the tautological bundle over
\mathbb{A}_{d}
.

The space
TS^{d-1}
is indeed the tangent bundle of the sphere, hence, it is a smooth manifold of dimension
2d-2
.

Note that
(r,u)
,
(r,-u)
represent the same element
\ell_{r,u}\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
. Moreover,
(r,u,\eta)
,
(r,-u,-\eta)
represent the same element in
\mathcal{A}_{d}
.

The next two lemmas gather the geometric description of the spaces
\mathbb{A}_{d}
and
\mathcal{A}_{d}
. Their proof is straightforward differential geometric book-keeping.

Lemma 5.1.2

(Differentiable structure)
.

\mathcal{A}_{d}
,
\mathbb{A}_{d}
are smooth manifolds. There is a commutative diagram of smooth mappings:

(5.4)

\begin{array}[]{ccc}TS^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}&\xrightarrow{(\ell,v)_{\cdot}}&\mathcal{A}_{d}\\
\downarrow\text{$\cancel{\eta}$}&&\downarrow\text{$\cancel{v}$}\\
TS^{d-1}&\xrightarrow{\ell_{\cdot}}&\mathbb{A}_{d}\end{array},

where the horizontal arrows are smooth double coverings, with respect to the relations

(5.5)

(r,u)\sim(r,-u),\quad(r,u,\eta)\sim(r,-u,-\eta).

In particular, the mapping
\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\colon(\ell)_{r,u}\mapsto\psi(r,u)
defines a one-to-one correspondence
\psi_{\mathbb{A}}\leftrightarrow\psi
(resp.
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}\leftrightarrow\psi
) between functions on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
(resp.
\mathcal{A}_{d}
) and functions on
TS^{d-1}
(resp.
TS^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}
) that are invariant under the symmetry imposed by
eq.

5.5
. Such a correspondence preserves measurability, smoothness and Sobolev regularity.
13
13

13

The lemma entails that
\mathbb{A}_{d}
is diffeomorphic to the tautological vector bundle
\ell\mapsto\ell^{\perp}
over the real projective space
\ell\in\mathbb{RP}^{d-1}
. It should be noted that this is not the tangent bundle of
\mathbb{RP}^{d-1}
.

Definition 5.1.3
.

The real Schwartz space
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
is the set of functions
\psi_{\mathbb{A}}
such that

(5.6)

\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{A}}(TS^{d-1}):=\left\{\psi\in\mathcal{S}(TS^{d-1}):\psi(r,-u)=\psi(r,u),\ \forall(r,u)\in TS^{d-1}\right\},

where
\mathcal{S}(TS^{d-1})
is defined as in
section

3.1.1
.
The tempered distributions on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
are the elements of the dual space
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
. We define analogously the spaces
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}}(TS^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
and denote all the complexifications with the subscript
\mathbb{C}
.

Notation:
In what follows, we will indiscriminately identify
\psi_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\psi
and write

(5.7)

\psi(r+u\mathbb{R},u\eta)=:\psi(r,u,\eta),\quad\forall(r,u,\eta)\in TS^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R},\ \forall\psi\colon\mathcal{A}_{d}\to\mathbb{C}.

Moreover, for
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
and
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, we will write:

(5.8)

\langle g,\psi\rangle=:\int_{\mathcal{A}_{d}}g(\differential(\ell,v))\psi(\ell,v)=:\int_{TS^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}}g(\differential(r,u,\eta))\psi(r,u,\eta).

Lemma 5.1.4

(Invariant Measure)
.

There exists a unique measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
such that for any
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
the next formula holds:

(5.9)

\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}{g}(\ell)\mu(\differential\ell)=\frac{1}{2\pi s_{d-2}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{u^{\perp}}g(r,u)\differential r\differential u.

With such a choice, for any isometry
\iota
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the map
\ell\mapsto\iota(\ell)
preserves
\mu
.
The analogous statement holds for any
g\in L^{1}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
.

Remark 5.1.5
.

The symbolic notation introduced in
eq.

5.8
, reduces to that of
eq.

5.9
in case the distributions
g
is represented by a function
g
, that is,
g(\differential(\ell,v))=g(\ell,v)\mu(\differential\ell)\differential v
and
g(\differential(r,u,\eta))=g(r,u,\eta)\differential r\differential u\differential\eta
. The symbol
\differential r\differential u
, in this context, denotes the integral in
r\in u^{\perp}
first and then in
u\in S^{d-1}
, as in the r.h.s. of
eq.

5.9
. It should be noted that this is different from the integral with respect to the Riemannian density induced by the inclusion
TS^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
(which we never use in this paper). The precise relation is shown in
lemma

5.1.7
below.

Remark 5.1.6
.

The normalizing constant of
\mu
,
\alpha_{d}:=\frac{1}{2\pi s_{d-2}}
is such that
eq.

2.8
and (equivalently)
eq.

5.26
hold without constants.

Lemma 5.1.7

(Grassmannian-Fubini)
.

For any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
, we have the following rule:

(5.10)

\begin{gathered}\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{u^{\perp}}\psi(r,u)\differential r\differential u=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{S(r^{\perp})}\psi(r,u)\frac{1}{|r|}\differential u\differential r\\
=\int_{\left\{(u,r)\in S^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\langle{u,r}\rangle=0\right\}}\psi(r,u)\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|r|^{2}}}\mathcal{H}^{2d-2}(\differential(u,r)).\end{gathered}

Proof.

The coarea formula
[
34
, Theorem 3.2.12]
(apply twice), gives

(5.11)

\begin{gathered}\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{u^{\perp}}\psi(r,u)\differential r\differential u=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{S(r^{\perp})}\psi(r,u)\frac{\mathrm{Jac}\left(\left((u,r)\mapsto u\right)|_{\langle{u,r}\rangle=0}\right)}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(\left((u,r)\mapsto r\right)|_{\langle{u,r}\rangle=0}\right)}\differential u\differential r\\
=\int_{\left\{(u,r)\in S^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\langle{u,r}\rangle=0\right\}}\psi(r,u)\mathrm{Jac}\left(\left((u,r)\mapsto u\right)|_{\langle{u,r}\rangle=0}\right)\mathcal{H}^{2d-2}(\differential(u,r)).\end{gathered}

Now, let us observe that for any two linear map
(A,B)\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{a+b}
, one has that

(5.12)

\mathrm{Jac}(A,B)=\mathrm{Jac}(A)\mathrm{Jac}(B|_{\ker(A)}).

In particular, one can interchange the role of
A
and
B
. Therefore, we can rewrite the above formula in terms of simpler Jacobians by applying
eq.

5.12
to
A,B,C
being the differentials of the maps sending
(u,r)\in S^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
u,r,\langle{u,r}\rangle
, respectively.

(5.13)

\begin{gathered}\int_{S^{d-1}}\int_{u^{\perp}}\psi(r,u)\differential r\differential u=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{S(r^{\perp})}\psi(r,u)\frac{\mathrm{Jac}\left(r\mapsto\langle{u,r}\rangle\right)\mathrm{Jac}\left((u,r)\mapsto u\right)}{\mathrm{Jac}\left(u\mapsto\langle{u,r}\rangle\right)\mathrm{Jac}\left((u,r)\mapsto r\right)}\differential u\differential r\\
=\int_{\left\{(u,r)\in S^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}:\langle{u,r}\rangle=0\right\}}\psi(r,u)\frac{\mathrm{Jac}\left(r\mapsto\langle{u,r}\rangle\right)\mathrm{Jac}\left((u,r)\mapsto u\right)}{\mathrm{Jac}\left((u,r)\mapsto\langle{u,r}\rangle\right)}\mathcal{H}^{2d-2}(\differential(u,r)).\end{gathered}

All Jacobians in this formula are now easy to compute and give the desired result.
∎

5.2.
X-ray-Fourier and Radon transforms

Definition 5.2.1
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define its
X-ray-Fourier transform

\mathcal{X}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})

as

(5.14)

\mathcal{X}\varphi(\ell,v):=\int_{\ell}\varphi(x)e^{-i\langle{v,x}\rangle}\differential x.

We extend such a definition to distributions as for the Fourier transform: given
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we define
\mathcal{X}f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
by setting

(5.15)

\langle\mathcal{X}f,\psi\rangle:=\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential u\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\differential x)\mathcal{F}_{t\to\langle{x,u}\rangle}\left(\psi(x+u\mathbb{R},ut)\right).

for any test function
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
.

Concretely, the
\mathcal{X}
transform is just the one-dimensional Fourier transform along the line. Precisely, the following identity holds, in a distributional sense, for all
f
as in the definition above.

(5.16)

\mathcal{X}f(r+\mathbb{R}u,\eta u)=\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}\left(f(r+ut)\right).

(see
section

3.4.1
) In particular, the above identities hold pointwise if
f,g
are of Schwartz class.

Remark 5.2.2
.

As a first rough heuristic, one can think that the concentration of
|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}
near a pair
(\ell,v)
corresponds to a strong oscillatory behavior of
f
along the line
\ell
.

Definition 5.2.3
.

We call
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}(\cdot,0)
the
X-ray (one-dimensional Radon) transform
, that is

(5.17)

\mathcal{R}\varphi(\ell)=\int_{\ell}\varphi(x)\differential x,

For all
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The adjoint Radon transform is thus defined as the operator
\mathcal{R}^{*}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{A}_{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}g,\varphi\rangle=\langle g,\mathcal{R}\varphi\rangle
. We extend such a definition and keep the same notation for the operator
