\mathcal{R}^{*}\colon\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
acting only in the space variable:
14
14

14

In other words, the operator
\mathcal{R}^{*}
is extended by identifying
\mathcal{R}^{*}\equiv(\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{R}})^{*}
.

(5.18)

\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}g,\Phi\rangle{:=}\int_{\mathcal{A}_{d}}g(d(r,u,\eta))\int_{r+u\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x,\eta)\differential x.

The above definition is well posed, since
\mathcal{R}\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see
[
45
]
. Additionally, the Radon transform
\mathcal{R}
extends to distributions
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with compact support as in
[
45
]
. For
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{A}_{d})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
, one has

(5.19)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\psi(x)=c_{d}\int_{x\in\ell}\psi(\ell)\differential\ell=c_{d}\int_{S^{d-1}}\psi(x-\langle{x,u}\rangle u,u)\sigma(\differential u),

for some dimensional constant
c_{d}>0
, where the integral is with respect to the canonical probability measure of the projective space
\mathbb{RP}^{d-1}\cong\{\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{d}:x\in\ell\}
, see
[
45
, Section 6]
, or, equivalently, as a uniform integral over the sphere, as above.

5.3.
The slice and inversion formulas for
\mathcal{X}f

The
Fourier slice theorem
, a cornerstone formula of the Radon transform theory (see
[
45
]
), takes the following form in our context.

Theorem 5.3.1

(Fourier slice for
\mathcal{X}
)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
,

(5.20)

\mathcal{X}f(r+u\mathbb{R},u\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{f}(\differential\xi)\frac{e^{i\langle{\xi,r}\rangle}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\delta(\eta-\langle{u,\xi}\rangle),

where the identity is meant in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
.

Proof.

Recall the formalism established in
section

3.1
, in particular the part regarding the delta function
section

3.1.5
.

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}\left(f(r+ut)\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential t\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{f}(\differential\xi)\,\frac{e^{i\langle{r+ut,\xi}\rangle}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,e^{-i{t\eta}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{f}(\differential\xi)\,\frac{e^{i\langle{\xi,r}\rangle}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{e^{i{t(\langle{u,\xi}\rangle-\eta)}}}{2\pi}\,\differential t}_{=\,\delta(\langle{u,\xi}\rangle-\eta)}.

The exchange of integrals is justified since the resulting expression is evaluated against a Schwartz test function.

∎

Corollary 5.3.2

(Fourier slice for test functions)
.

For
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\rho\in u^{\perp}
:

(5.22)

\mathcal{F}_{r\to\rho}^{u^{\perp}}(\mathcal{X}\varphi(r,u,\eta))=\hat{\varphi}(\rho+\eta u),\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}_{r\to\rho}^{u^{\perp}}\left(\mathcal{R}\varphi(r,u)\right)=\hat{\varphi}(\rho).

Proof.

The slice theorem
theorem

5.3.1
gives the first expression after developing the delta, and Fourier inversion.
By the same formula, applied at
\eta=0
:
\mathcal{R}\varphi=\mathcal{X}\varphi(\cdot,\cdot,0)
. At
\eta=0
:
\hat{\varphi}(\rho+0\cdot u)=\hat{\varphi}(\rho)
.
∎

Definition 5.3.3
.

We define
(-\Delta_{r})^{s}
as the
fractional Laplacian
operator in the variable
r\in u^{\perp}
, acting on tempered distributions on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
, (see
definition

5.1.3
). For
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
such that
(-\Delta_{r})^{s}\psi(r,u,\eta):=\mathcal{F}_{\rho\to r}^{-u^{\perp}}(|\rho|^{2s}\mathcal{F}_{s\to\rho}^{u^{\perp}}(\psi(s,u,\eta)))
. For general distributions, it is defined as the self-adjoint extension, exactly as for
(-\Delta)^{s}
, see
section

3.4.2
.

Theorem 5.3.4

(Intertwining relations)
.

(5.23)

\mathcal{R}(-\Delta)^{s}=(-\Delta_{r})^{s}\mathcal{R},\quad\mathcal{R}^{*}(-\Delta_{r})^{s}=(-\Delta)^{s}\mathcal{R}^{*},

For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

See
[
45
, Lemma 5.3]
.
∎

Theorem 5.3.5

(Inversion)
.

Let
f,\hat{f}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{C})
. The following identity holds:

(5.24)

\left(\mathcal{R}^{*}(-\Delta_{r})^{1/2}\mathcal{X}f\right)\left(x,\eta\right)=\mathcal{F}_{\xi\to x}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{s_{d-2}}\int_{S(\xi^{\perp})}\hat{f}(\xi+\eta u)\differential u\right),

where
S(\xi^{\perp}):=S^{d-2}\cap\xi^{\perp}
is the unit
(d-2)
-sphere perpendicular to
\xi
.

Proof.

Denote
\psi:=(-\Delta_{r})^{1/2}\mathcal{R}\Phi
. Then, by the slice formula
theorem

5.3.1
,

(5.25)

\begin{gathered}\langle\mathcal{X}f,(-\Delta_{r})^{1/2}\mathcal{R}\Phi\rangle=\frac{1}{2\pi s_{d-2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential u\ \hat{f}(r+\eta u)\mathcal{F}_{\rho\to r}^{-u^{\perp}}\left(\psi(u,\rho,\eta)\right)\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi s_{d-2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential u\int_{u^{\perp}}\differential r\ \hat{f}(r+\eta u)|r|\mathcal{F}_{\rho\to r}^{-u^{\perp}}\left(\mathcal{R}\Phi(u,\rho,\eta)\right)\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi s_{d-2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential r\int_{S(r^{\perp})}\differential u\ \hat{f}(r+\eta u)\frac{\hat{\Phi}(-r,\eta)}{(2\pi)^{d-1}}\\
=\frac{1}{s_{d-2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential r\int_{S(r^{\perp})}\differential u\ \hat{f}(r+\eta u)\mathcal{F}_{x\to r}^{-1}(\Phi(x,\eta));\end{gathered}

where in the last step, we exchanged the integrals with
lemma

5.1.7
and used
eq.

5.22
.
∎

Remark 5.3.6

(Recovery of
f
)
.

At
\eta=0
, (
5.24
) reduces to
f(x)
, consistent with
\mathcal{X}f(\cdot,\cdot,0)=\mathcal{R}f
and the X-ray inversion formula (
[
45
, Theorem 6.2]
):

(5.26)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\mathcal{R}f=f,\quad\text{or}\quad\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathcal{R}=(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Such a formula, however is valid only when
\mathcal{R}f
is defined and the evaluation at
\eta=0
makes sense: for
f
compactly supported distribution, or
f
a smooth function such that
f(x)=O(|x|^{-d-\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, see
[
45
, Theorem 6.2]
.

5.4.
The white noise on the line space

Let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{d})
be the white noise, defined as in
section

3.4.3
, on the space
\mathbb{A}_{d}=TS^{d-1}/\sim
, with respect to the measure
\mu
of
lemma

5.1.4
that is,

(5.27)

\mathbb{E}\left\{\langle\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}},\phi\rangle\langle\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}},\psi\rangle\right\}=\langle\phi,\psi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\phi(\ell)\psi(\ell)\mu(\differential\ell),\quad\forall\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{A}_{d}).

The inversion theorem
remark

5.3.6
, yields the following relation between
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}
and the fractional Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see
section

3.4
.

Proposition 5.4.1
.

The following have the same law:

(5.28)

\mathrm{FGF}_{\frac{1}{2}}\sim\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}

In particular, Blaschke–Petkantschin formulae (see
eq.

2.8
) follow.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then. By
eq.

5.26
,

(5.29)

{\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left[\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}},\varphi\rangle\right]={\mathbb{V}\mathrm{ar}}\left[\langle\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}},\mathcal{R}\varphi\rangle\right]=\langle\varphi,\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathcal{R}\varphi\rangle=\langle\varphi,(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\varphi\rangle.

This concludes the proof since
\mathrm{FGF}_{\frac{1}{2}}=(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{4}}\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is unique (up to law) Gaussian random tempered distribution, having the operator
(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
as covariance.
∎

5.5.
Stochastic integral

In this part of the paper, we consider smooth centered Gaussian random fields
f
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that are naturally described (see
eq.

1.2
) as the Fourier transform of a random tempered distribution
Z\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, through the expression:

(5.30)

f(x)=\hat{Z}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\langle{x,\xi}\rangle}Z(\differential\xi)

where the
spectral distribution

Z
(
=(2\pi)^{-d}\hat{f}(-\cdot)
by Fourier inversion) is a centered complex Gaussian
15
15

15

Following
[
53
]
, a complex random variable is a complex Gaussian if the real and imaginary part are jointly real Gaussian random variables; without any assumption on their covariance.
random tempered distribution, see
section

3.1.1
for the definition of random tempered distributions via Bochner-Minlos theorem. In general, the above identity has to be understood in a distributional sense:
for any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

(5.31)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)f(\differential x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hat{\varphi}(\xi)Z(\differential\xi)

In such a context, the field
f
is real if
Z
is Hermitian, that is:
\overline{Z}(\differential(-\xi))=Z(\differential\xi)
, and smooth if
Z
has a sufficiently strong decay at infinity, in the sense of the next
remark

5.5.1
.

Assumption B
.

From now on, in all statements of this section, we will make the following assumptions:
f
is a real stationary smooth Gaussian random field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with spectral measure
\omega
and spectral distribution
Z
, i.e.,
f=\hat{Z}
. We will additionally assume that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with probability one.

The next remark implies that the requirements of
B
are automatically satisfied under the assumptions of Theorem
2.4.9

Remark 5.5.1

(Pointwise evaluation of spectral integrals)
.

We clarify when the distributional identity (
5.31
) can be upgraded to the pointwise formula (
5.30
). For distribution spaces, we take up the classical notation of
[
89
]
.

(1)

(General criterion.)
A classical result of Schwartz
[
89
, Theorem XV, §VII]
asserts that the Fourier transform
\hat{Z}
of a tempered distribution
Z\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
belongs to the space
\mathscr{O}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of smooth functions with at most polynomial growth (together with all derivatives) if and only if
Z
belongs to the space
\mathscr{O}^{\prime}_{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of
rapidly decreasing distributions
, meaning that:
Z*\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for every
\rho\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
16
16

16

Equivalently, a tempered distribution
Z
is
rapidly decreasing
if
(1+|\xi|^{2})^{k/2}Z
is a bounded distribution for every
k\geq 0
; see
[
89
, Section VI.8]
. The equivalence is explained in
[
89
, “Remarque” after Theorem IX, §VII]
.

When
Z\in\mathscr{O}^{\prime}_{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the pairing
\langle Z,\varphi\rangle
extends from Schwartz test functions to a larger class, and in particular to
\xi\mapsto e^{-i\langle{x,\xi}\rangle}
. The pointwise formula
f(x)=\langle Z_{\xi},\,e^{-i\langle{x,\xi}\rangle}\rangle
then coincides with
\hat{Z}(x)\in\mathscr{O}_{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(2)

(Compact support case.)
If
Z\in\mathscr{E}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(compactly supported), then
Z\in\mathscr{O}^{\prime}_{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\hat{Z}
extends to an entire complex analytic function on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
with polynomial growth on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; this is the Paley-Wiener theorem, see
[
89
, Theorem XVI, §VII]
or
[
49
, Theorem 7.1.14]
. This covers the
Berry model
, where
Z(\omega)
is supported on
S^{d-1}
almost surely, as well as any model whose spectral measure
\omega
has compact support.

(3)

(Bargmann–Fock case.)
When
\omega(\differential\xi)=\rho(\xi)\,\differential\xi
with
\sqrt{\rho}
smooth and rapidly decreasing (e.g.,
\rho(\xi)=c\,e^{-|\xi|^{2}/2\sigma}
), one can write
Z=\sqrt{\rho}\cdot\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
, where
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the standard real white noise from
section

3.4.3
. For each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
\xi\mapsto\sqrt{\rho(\xi)}\,e^{-i\langle{x,\xi}\rangle}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so
f(x)=\langle\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},\,\sqrt{\rho}\,e^{-i\langle{x,\cdot}\rangle}\rangle
is a well-defined Gaussian random variable for every
x
, using only that
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\omega)\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a.s. (
theorem

3.3.1
).

Moreover,
f
is real and stationary if and only if there exists a positive definite function
K
such that
K(x):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(y)f(y+x)\right]
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. By Bochner’s theorem
[
19
]
(see also
[
2
, Theorem 5.4.1]
), this is equivalent to the existence of a positive even Borel measure
\omega
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
K=\hat{\omega}
.
\omega
is called the
spectral measure
of
f
.
In terms of the spectral distribution
Z
, the stationarity condition is equivalent to:
\mathbb{E}[Z(\differential\xi)\overline{Z(\differential\xi^{\prime})}]=\delta(\xi-\xi^{\prime})\omega(\differential\xi)
; in other words,
Z
is a
complex Gaussian white noise with intensity
\omega
: the family
\{\langle Z,\varphi\rangle:\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}
is a Hermitian complex Gaussian tempered distribution with covariance:

(5.32)

\mathbb{E}\left[\langle Z,\varphi\rangle\overline{\langle Z,\psi\rangle}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(\xi)\overline{\psi(\xi)}\omega(\differential\xi),\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\langle Z,\varphi\rangle\langle Z,\psi\rangle\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(\xi)\psi(-\xi)\omega(\differential\xi),

This can be easily verified via Fourier calculus rules, see
[
45
, Section V.3]
.
The covariance form is clearly continuous on
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
, hence by
theorem

3.3.1
, these two identities characterize
Z
as a Gaussian tempered distribution
Z\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The second of the above identities follows from the first and the afore-mentioned Hermitian property of
Z
, reflecting the fact that the field is real. In terms of test functions such a condition reads:

(5.33)

\overline{\langle Z,\varphi\rangle}=\langle Z,\overline{\varphi}(-\cdot)\rangle.

In addition,
f
is isotropic if and only if
\omega
is radial, meaning that there exists a measure
\rho
on
[0,+\infty)
, such that

(5.34)

\int\varphi(\xi)\omega(\differential\xi)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\rho(\differential t)\left(\int_{tS^{d-1}}\varphi(\xi)\differential\xi\right),

for every
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Our main cases of interest are, in order of importance: the
Berry
random field (see
example

5.5.2
below), for which
\rho(\differential t)=c\delta(\lambda-t)\differential t
; the
Bargmann-Fock
random field, for which
\rho(t)=ce^{-{t^{2}}/{2\sigma}}
(see Figure
1
); the
Annulus
random field, for which
\rho(t)=c1_{[a,b]}
(see Figure
8
); moreover, we will consider also the general non-isotropic case. The implied constants are specified elsewhere. In all these cases, the field
f
satisfies
B
.

Example 5.5.2
.

Berry’s field
B=\hat{Z}
is defined in
eq.

1.2
, as the stationary isotropic Gaussian random field with
\omega=\sigma
, the uniform probability measure on
S^{d-1}
. Since
\omega
is supported on
S^{d-1}
, the spectral distribution
Z\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is supported on
S^{d-1}
as well and is a complex Gaussian white noise with intensity
\sigma
.

Remark 5.5.3
.

For a continuous real stationary Gaussian field
f
, by standard extension arguments, based e.g. on (
5.32
), the random variable
\langle Z,\varphi\rangle
is defined for all complex-valued test functions
\varphi
in
L^{2}(\omega)
. Such a family restricts to a real
isonormal Gaussian process

I\colon\mathfrak{H}\to L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
over the real Hilbert subspace
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}_{\text{her}}(\omega)\subset L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{C})
of Hermitian complex valued functions:
\overline{\varphi}=\varphi(-\cdot)
, in the sense of
[
74
, Section 2.1]
. In this sense,
eq.

5.30
is equivalent to
f(x)=I(e^{-i\langle{x,\cdot}\rangle})
and
Z|_{\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=I_{\mathbb{C}}
is the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of
I
to
L^{2}(\omega;\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{H}_{\mathbb{C}}:=\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, restricted to Schwartz functions.

Remark 5.5.4
.

In the sense of
[
53
]
,
Z
can be equivalently described (up to the Fourier transform convention) in terms of the complex Gaussian stochastic measure
Z(A)=\langle Z,1_{A}\rangle
of
[
53
, Theorem 7.54]
. The corresponding stochastic integral, given by
[
53
, Theorem 7.52]
, is the operator
I_{\mathbb{C}}
described in
remark

5.5.3
above. This can be seen by comparing
eq.

5.30
and
eq.

5.31
with
[
53
, Eq. (7.45) and Remark 7.55]
.

5.6.
The Wick square
Z^{:2:}
and
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:

Let
f
be a real, smooth, stationary, centered Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with spectral measure
\omega
and spectral distribution
Z=\mathcal{F}^{-1}(f)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. By
remark

5.5.3
,
Z
can be seen as the
\mathbb{C}
-linear extension of an isonormal Gaussian process
I
over
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}_{\rm her}(\omega)
, in the sense of
[
75
, Section 2.1]
. For
n=1,2,...
, we write
I_{n}\colon L^{2}_{\text{sym}}(\omega^{n};\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\odot n}\to L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
for the associated
n
th
multiple Wiener–Itô integral
(this notion corresponds to
[
53
, Theorem 7.52]
, and to the complexification of
[
75
, Section 2.7]
).

Remark 5.6.1
.

We adopt the convention according to which the domain of
I_{q}
is the whole space
L^{2}(\omega^{q},\mathbb{C})
, by writing
I_{q}(h):=I_{q}(h_{\text{sym}})
, where
h_{\text{sym}}
is the symmetrization of
h
, that is, the orthogonal projection of
h
onto the subspace
L^{2}_{\text{sym}}(\omega^{q};\mathbb{C})=\mathfrak{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\odot q}\subset L^{2}(\omega^{q};\mathbb{C})
of a.e. symmetric complex-valued functions on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{q}
. The image of
I_{q}
coincides with the complexification of the
q
-th Wiener chaos associated to
f
(i.e., to
Z
) and the mapping
I_{q}
satisfies:

(5.35)

\mathbb{E}[|I_{q}(h)|^{2}]=q!\|h_{\text{sym}}\|_{L^{2}(\omega^{q};\mathbb{C})}^{2}

by the isometry properties of multiple integrals, see
[
53
, Theorem 7.52]
or
[
74
, Theorem 2.7.5]
.

Definition 5.6.2

(Wick square)
.

For
h\in L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
, the
Wick square pairing
is the second-chaos random variable

(5.36)

\int_{(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\ h(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\langle Z^{:2:},h\rangle:=I_{2}(h_{\text{sym}}),

where
h_{\rm sym}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\tfrac{1}{2}(h(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+h(\xi_{2},\xi_{1}))
denotes the symmetrization of
h
. In symbolic notation:
Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))=\ :\!{Z(\differential\xi_{1})\,Z(\differential\xi_{2})}\!:
.

Since (e.g. by Jensen)
\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})}\geq\|h_{\text{sym}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})}
, the random variable
\langle Z^{:2:},h\rangle
is well-defined. On product test functions
\varphi\otimes\psi
(with
\varphi,\psi\in L^{2}(\omega)
), the definition reduces to

(5.37)

\langle Z^{:2:},\varphi\otimes\psi\rangle=:\!{\langle Z,\varphi\rangle\,\langle Z,\psi\rangle}\!:=\langle Z,\varphi\rangle\,\langle Z,\psi\rangle-\mathbb{E}\left[\langle Z,\varphi\rangle\,\langle Z,\psi\rangle\right],

It should be noted that
Z^{:2:}
is uniquely characterized by the above formula, given in terms of the
Wick product
, see
[
53
, Theorem 3.9]
.

Lemma 5.6.3
.

The covariance of
Z^{:2:}
is:

(5.38)

\mathbb{E}\left\{\langle Z^{:2:},h_{1}\rangle\,\overline{\langle Z^{:2:},h_{2}\rangle}\right\}=2\langle(h_{1})_{\rm sym},(h_{2})_{\rm sym}\rangle_{L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})}.

Proof.

Follows from
eq.

5.35
.
∎

5.6.1.
A.s. well-posedness of the Wick square distribution

The Wick square
Z^{:2:}
is a priori defined as a continuous linear map
L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})\to L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
. However, we can promote it to a genuine
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
-valued random variable, after observing that the inclusion
\iota:\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
is continuous. Then, by the isometry property of
I_{2}
, the composition
Z^{:2:}=I_{2}\circ\iota

is a continuous linear map from

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
to
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
.

Proposition 5.6.4

(A.s. well-posedness of
Z^{:2:}
)
.

There exists a measurable
Z^{:2:}\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, unique up to a.s. equality, such that for every
h\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
:

(5.39)

\langle Z^{:2:}(\omega),h\rangle=I_{2}(h_{\rm sym})(\omega),\qquad\text{for a.e. }\omega\in\Omega.

Proof.

By the isometry property of
I_{2}
, the map
h\mapsto I_{2}(h_{\rm sym})
is a continuous linear map
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\to L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
. By
theorem

3.3.1
, there exists a unique probability measure
\omega
on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
whose finite dimensional marginals match those of
\{I_{2}(h_{\rm sym})\}_{h\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})}
.

Let
\{h_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
be a countable dense subset. Then,
\Omega\ni\omega\mapsto\Gamma(\omega):=(I_{2}(h_{j,\text{sym}}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a measurable map whose law is determined by a push-forward of
\omega
under the bijection:

(5.40)

e\colon\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\to E:=\left\{(\langle T,h_{j}\rangle)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}}\colon T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\right\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{N}},

Therefore
\mathbb{P}\left[\omega\in\Omega\colon\Gamma(\omega)\in E\right]=\omega(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d}))=1
. Since the finite-dimensional projections determine the
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
-topology,
e^{-1}
is measurable (and
e
too). It follows that setting
Z^{:2:}(\omega):=e^{-1}\Gamma(\omega)
, defines a measurable mapping from a full measure subset
\Omega_{0}\subset\Omega
to
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.

Now, for arbitrary
h\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
there is a converging subsequence
h_{j}\to h
, hence
I_{2}(h_{j,\text{sym}})\to I_{2}(h_{\text{sym}})
in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
, which implies almost sure convergence on a further subsequence. It follows that for
\omega
in the intersection of
\Omega_{0}
with such a.s. event one must have that
I_{2}(h_{\text{sym}})(\omega)=Z^{:2:}(\omega)
.
17
17

17

I_{2}(\cdot)(\omega)
does not need to be continuous, nor linear, in
h
for fixed
\omega
. Indeed, here, the a.s. event depends on
h
.

∎

Remark 5.6.5

(Nuclearity)
.

Z^{:2:}
is
not
Gaussian (it lives in the second chaos), so the Gaussian shortcut (
remark

3.3.4
) does not apply directly, via the covariance formula
eq.

5.38
.
theorem

3.3.1
requires nuclearity of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
. For instance, the same argument fails on
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\odot q}
, which is not nuclear (see
[
59
, Section 2.3]
) and indeed
I_{q}
cannot be seen as a random element of
\mathfrak{H}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\odot q}
.

5.6.2.
Mollification

For
\rho\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
\rho\geq 0
,
\int\rho=1
, set
\rho_{\varepsilon}:=\varepsilon^{-d}\rho(\cdot/\varepsilon)
and
Z_{\varepsilon}:=Z*\rho_{\varepsilon}
. Each
Z_{\varepsilon}
is a smooth Gaussian random function in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see
remark

5.5.1
(a)), so
Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\,\,:\!{Z_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{1})\,Z_{\varepsilon}(\xi_{2})}\!:
is a smooth random function on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. In particular,
Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:}
is a genuine smooth (in the Schwartz class) random tempered distribution, defined on
(\Omega,\mathbb{P})
.

Proposition 5.6.6

(Mollification convergence)
.

Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:}\to Z^{:2:}
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
in probability.

Proof.

By linearity and the fact that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
is metrizable, the convergence in probability can be reduced to the convergence in law. By
theorem

3.3.3
, we only need to check the convergence for any fixed
h\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
.
Setting
h_{\varepsilon}:=h*(\rho_{\varepsilon}\otimes\rho_{\varepsilon})
, Fubini and the bilinearity of the Wick product (see
[
53
, Theorem 3.9]
) give
\langle Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:},h\rangle=\langle Z^{:2:},h_{\varepsilon}\rangle
. Since
h_{\varepsilon}\to h
in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
, it follows:
\langle Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:},h\rangle\to\langle Z^{:2:},h\rangle
almost surely.
∎

5.6.3.
Well-posedness of
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:

Lemma 5.6.7

(Continuity of
\psi\mapsto h_{\psi}
)
.

For
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, define
h_{\psi}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
by

(5.41)

\displaystyle h_{\psi}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\alpha_{d}\int_{S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})}\mathcal{F}_{r\to\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}^{u^{\perp}}\ \left(\psi(r,u,\langle{\xi_{2},u}\rangle)\right)\,\differential u

Then the map
\psi\mapsto h_{\psi}
is continuous from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
to
L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
. Moreover,

(5.42)

h_{\mathcal{R}\Phi}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\alpha_{d}\int_{S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})}\hat{\Phi}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},\langle{\xi_{2},u}\rangle)\differential u

for any
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
. Here
\hat{\Phi}(\xi,\eta):=\mathcal{F}_{x\to\xi}(\Phi(x,\eta))
.

Proof.

Formula (
5.41
) integrates a bounded function (
|\Phi_{\psi}|\leq\|\Phi_{\psi}\|_{\infty}
) over the compact set
S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})
, where the mapping
\psi\mapsto\Phi_{\psi}
is continuous. Hence
\|h_{\psi}\|_{L^{2}(\omega^{2})}\leq\|\Phi_{\psi}\|_{\infty}\cdot\omega(\mathbb{R}^{d})k_{d}<\infty
, for a constant
k_{d}>0
.

The second formula is an immediate consequence of
theorem

5.3.1
.
∎

Remark 5.6.8
.

h_{\psi}\notin\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2d})
in general. In coordinates
s=\tfrac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}
,
z=\tfrac{\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}}{2}
, when
z\parallel s
one has
\langle{z,u}\rangle=0
for all
u\in S(s^{\perp})
, so
h_{\psi}
does not decay in
|z|
.

Theorem 5.6.9

(Well-posedness of
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
)
.

Let
f
be a real, smooth, stationary, centered Gaussian field with spectral measure
\omega
.
Then there exists
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, unique up to a.s. equality, such that for every
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
:

(5.43)

\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle=I_{2}(h_{\psi})\qquad\text{a.s.}

Moreover, given smooth approximations
Z_{\varepsilon}=Z*\rho_{\varepsilon}
,
Z_{\varepsilon}^{:2:}
, as in
section

5.6.2
, and
f_{\varepsilon}=\widehat{Z_{\varepsilon}}
, then
\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon},:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:
are random smooth functions on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
satisfying:

(5.44)

:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:(r,u,\eta)=:\!{\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}(r,u,\eta)\overline{\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}(r,u,\eta)}}\!:,\quad\forall(r,u,\eta)\in\mathcal{A}_{d}

and
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:\to\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
in probability in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

The argument is the same as for
proposition

5.6.4
. By
lemma

5.6.7
,
\psi\mapsto I_{2}(h_{\psi})
is a continuous linear map
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})\to L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C})
. The proof of
proposition

5.6.4
(based on Bochner-Minlos
theorem

3.3.1
and the Polish structure of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
) gives the version
(\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:)\colon\Omega\to\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
satisfying (
5.43
). For the convergence in probability: for each
\psi
, one has that
\langle(\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:)-(\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:),\psi\rangle\to 0
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
(since
(h_{\psi})_{\varepsilon}\to h_{\psi}
in
L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
I_{2}
is an isometry), hence in probability (convergence in law to a constant). Since
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
is metrizable, this gives convergence in probability in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
.

The only point that remains to show is the identity (
5.44
). A natural starting point is from the r.h.s. of the latter. Using the slice theorem and recalling that Fourier inversion gives
\hat{f}(\differential\xi)=(2\pi)^{d}Z(-\differential\xi)
(
theorem

5.3.1
), we deduce the identity (see
eq.

5.48
for a more detailed explanation):

(5.45)

\begin{gathered}:\!{\mathcal{X}f(r,u,\eta)\,\overline{\mathcal{X}f(r,u,\eta)}}\!:\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\,e^{-i\langle{r,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle}\,\delta(\eta_{1}+\langle{u,\xi_{1}}\rangle)\,\delta(\eta_{2}+\langle{u,\xi_{2}}\rangle)\Big|_{\eta_{1}=-\eta_{2}=\eta}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\,e^{-i\langle{r,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle}\,\delta(\langle{u,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle)\delta(\eta+\langle{\xi_{1},u}\rangle).\end{gathered}

In case both
f
and
Z
are smooth fields, then
\mathcal{X}f
is also smooth, by the slice theorem (
theorem

5.3.1
), thus the above formula defines a smooth random field on
\mathcal{A}_{d}
. By developing the deltas and using that the Jacobian of
\langle{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},u}\rangle
is
|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|
, we get

(5.46)

\begin{gathered}\langle:\!{\mathcal{X}f(r,u,\eta)\,\overline{\mathcal{X}f(r,u,\eta)}}\!:,\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\psi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\alpha_{d}\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential u\int_{u^{\perp}}\differential r\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta\ \\
\delta(\langle{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2},u}\rangle)\delta(\eta+\langle{\xi_{1},u}\rangle)e^{-i\langle{r,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle}(\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\psi)(r,u,\eta)=\\
=\alpha_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\!\!Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\int_{S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})}\!\!\!\differential u\int_{u^{\perp}}\differential r\ |\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|^{-1}e^{-i\langle{r,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle}(\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\psi)(r,u,-\langle{\xi_{1},u}\rangle)=\\
=\alpha_{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\int_{S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})}\differential u\ \mathcal{F}_{r\to\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}^{u^{\perp}}\ \left(\psi(r,u,-\langle{\xi_{1},u}\rangle)\right)\\
=\langle Z^{:2:},h_{\psi}\rangle=\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle.\end{gathered}

In the last step, we used the intertwining property of Fourier transform.

∎

Remark 5.6.10
.

One can think that
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
is a random element of the dual space of
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, of test functions which live in the range of the operator
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
.

5.6.4.
Interpretation: antidiagonal restriction

The object
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
admits a natural interpretation as a
restricton to the antidiagonal
in frequency space, which clarifies the necessity of
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
in
theorem

5.6.9
.

For fixed
\ell=r+\mathbb{R}u\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
, the X-ray–Fourier transform
\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\cdot)
is the one-dimensional Gaussian random distribution in
\eta
, obtained as the Fourier transform of the
f(r+(\cdot)u)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, a smooth real stationary Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}
. Note that this statement holds pointwise for each
\ell
, since
f
is assumed to be a smooth function (see
B
). By the slice theorem (
theorem

5.3.1
):

(5.47)

\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\eta)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-i\langle{r,\xi}\rangle}\,\delta(\eta+\langle{u,\xi}\rangle)\,Z(\differential\xi).

One can represent the collection of double integrals associated to such a family of one-dimensional Gaussian processes by writing:

(5.48)

:\!{\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\eta_{1})\,\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\eta_{2})}\!:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}Z^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\,e^{-i\langle{r,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}\rangle}\,\delta(\eta_{1}+\langle{u,\xi_{1}}\rangle)\,\delta(\eta_{2}+\langle{u,\xi_{2}}\rangle),

which is a well-defined random tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2}}
(at fixed
\ell
). The squared modulus
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\eta)|^{2}}\!:
corresponds to the
restriction to the anti-diagonal

\eta_{1}=-\eta_{2}
(since
\overline{\mathcal{X}f(\ell,\eta)}=\mathcal{X}f(\ell,-\eta)
by Hermitian symmetry). However, restricting a distribution
T\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to a submanifold
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is not automatically well-defined; it requires additional regularity assumptions on
T
.

Remark 5.6.11

(On the role of
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
in
theorem

5.6.9
)
.

The filter
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
is a
sufficient

regularization that makes the antidiagonal restriction well-defined as a random tempered distribution. This is the reason why
theorem

5.6.9
defines
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
rather than
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
itself.

Remark 5.6.12

(Mollification validates the construction)
.

theorem

5.6.9
confirms that the formal construction agrees with the “honest” pointwise Wick square: for the mollified
Z_{\varepsilon}=Z*\rho_{\varepsilon}
(a smooth Gaussian function), the object
:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}(\ell,\eta)|^{2}}\!:\,\,=|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}(\ell,\eta)|^{2}-\mathbb{E}[|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}(\ell,\eta)|^{2}]
is defined pointwise, and
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:\to\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}f|^{2}}\!:
in probability in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

5.7.
Scaling limit

5.7.1.
Scaling

Let us consider the rescaled field
f_{R}(\cdot):=f(R(\cdot))
. Then, we shall consider the following rescaling of
\mathcal{X}f
:

(5.49)

(\mathcal{X}_{R}f)(r,u,\eta):=\mathcal{X}f_{R}(r,u,R\eta)R=\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}\left(f(Rr+ut)\right),

where the latter identity is to be interpreted in a distributional sense, as elements of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
. Then, for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have that

(5.50)

\langle\mathcal{X}_{R}f,\psi\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X}f_{R},\psi(\cdot,\cdot,R^{-1}\cdot)\rangle,\quad\langle:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle=\langle:\!{|\mathcal{X}f_{R}|^{2}}\!:,\psi(\cdot,\cdot,R^{-1}\cdot)\rangle.

We apply such a rescaling to capture the fact that
\mathcal{X}f_{R}
concentrates around
\eta\sim R
, hence
\mathcal{X}_{R}f
concentrates near
\eta\sim 1
.

5.7.2.
Limit

In order to understand the limit as
R\to+\infty
, one should study the behavior of the measure

\omega^{2}\left(d\left({z+R^{-1}s},{-z+R^{-1}s}\right)\right).

This can be seen from
lemma

6.1.3
(in the appendix), a key step in the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
below.
In other words, the problem reduces to analyzing the (Euclidean) blow-up of the measure
\omega^{2}
along the anti-diagonal
\{(z,-z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.
18
18

18

In fact, since
\omega
is symmetric in our setting of interest (
f
is real), this is equivalent as studying
\omega^{2}
around the diagonal.
This is where we need to distinguish between the case where
\omega
admits a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure, from the case where it is supported on lower-dimensional manifold. Indeed, the blow-up behaviour is fundamentally different in the two situations. We observe that Point (1) in the theorem below recovers the case of Berry’s random field, as defined in
eq.

1.2
, by taking the spectral measure to be
\omega=\sigma
, i.e.,
\omega
is the probability measure with constant density
\rho=s_{d-1}^{-1}
with respect to the volume measure of the sphere
S^{d-1}
. As already observed, the forthcoming statement is a more general version of Theorem
2.4.9
.

Theorem 5.7.1
.

Let
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
. Then, under
B
,

(1)

If
\omega(\differential\xi)=\rho(\xi)s_{d-1}\sigma(\differential\xi)
is supported on
S^{d-1}
and is absolutely continuous with respect to the uniform spherical probability measure
\sigma
, and assume
\rho
is bounded and almost everywhere continuous. Then,

(5.51)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R^{d-1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\Phi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}\left(\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\circ E_{d}\right),\Phi\rangle|^{2}\right]\\
=\frac{2\alpha_{d}\pi^{d-1}}{2^{d-1}}\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\differential(u,r)\left(\rho(u)\int_{S^{d-2}}(\mathcal{R}\Phi)(r,u,\theta_{1})\differential\theta\right)^{2}.\end{gathered}

The Gaussian process
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\circ E_{d}
is the composition of: a White noise
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}
on
\mathbb{A}_{d}
with respect to the measure
\rho(u)^{2}\mu(\differential(u,r))
, and the deterministic operator:

E_{d}(\psi)(r,u):=\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha_{d}\pi^{d-1}}{2^{d-1}}}\int_{S^{d-2}}\psi(r,u,\theta_{1})\differential\theta

.

(2)

If
\omega(\differential\xi)=\rho(\xi)\differential\xi
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure, and assume
\rho
is bounded and almost everywhere continuous. Then,

(5.52)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\Phi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\circ F_{d},\Phi\rangle|^{2}\right]\\
=\frac{2\alpha_{d}^{2}}{2^{2d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential z\ \left|\rho(z)\int_{S^{d-2}}\mathcal{F}_{r\to s}\left(\Phi(r,{|\Pi_{s^{\perp}}(z)|\theta_{1}})\right)\ \differential\theta\right|^{2}.\end{gathered}

The Gaussian process
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\circ F_{d}
is the composition of: a White noise
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, with respect to the measure
\rho(z)^{2}\differential s\differential z
,
and the deterministic operator
F_{d}(\Phi)(s,z):=\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha_{d}^{2}}{2^{2d}}}\int_{S^{d-2}}\mathcal{F}_{r\to s}\left(\Phi(r,{|\Pi_{s^{\perp}}(z)|\theta_{1}})\right)\ \differential\theta
.

(3)

Both limits at Points
(1)
and
(2)
correspond to limits in law in the sense of random tempered distributions in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
:

(5.53)

\begin{gathered}R^{\frac{d_{i}}{2}}\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\xrightarrow[R\to\infty]{\ \mathrm{law}-{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})}\ }\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}^{*}\left(\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\circ E_{d}\right),\quad&\text{in case (1)},\\
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\circ F_{d},\quad&\text{in case (2)},\end{cases}\end{gathered}

where
d_{i}
depends on the case (i) (i=1,2) under consideration:
d_{1}=d-1
and
d_{2}=d
. In particular, in both cases the limit exists and is Gaussian, with covariance given as above.

The proof of
theorem

5.7.1
above is deferred to
section

6
.

5.7.3.
White scars: recovering
theorem

2.4.1
and
theorem

2.4.7
(ii)

Let
A\in\left\{\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{A}_{d}\right\}
. For a distribution
g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(A\times\mathbb{R})
, we denote as
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta g\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(A)
, the restriction of
g
to test functions
\Phi
of the form
\Phi(a,\eta)=\varphi(a)
. Note that since the operators
\mathcal{R}^{*}
and
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
act only on the first variable
a
, they both commute with
\int\differential\eta
, that is,
\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\int\differential\eta=\int\differential\eta\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}
.

The operation
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta
is well-defined for
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:
, although
\varphi(r,u)\cdot 1\notin\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
. The reason is that the kernel
h_{\varphi\cdot 1}
defined by (
5.41
) is uniformly bounded (since the Fourier transform in
r
is Schwartz and the integration is over a compact sphere), hence it belongs to
L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
. The pairing
\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\varphi\cdot 1\rangle:=I_{2}(h_{\varphi\cdot 1})
extends the definition in
theorem

5.6.9
beyond Schwartz test functions.

Proposition 5.7.2
.

The following identity holds almost surely in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

(5.54)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\int\differential\eta:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:=:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:,

Where
f
is any real smooth and stationary Gaussian random field on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathcal{X}f
is the Fourier transform of
f
in the variable
\eta
, therefore, by Plancherel
\eta
,
\int\differential\eta:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:=\mathcal{R}:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:
. The conclusion now follows from the Radon inversion formula
\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\mathcal{R}=\mathrm{Id}
, see
remark

5.3.6
. This argument, however, requires a clarification, since
\mathcal{R}f^{2}
is not defined unless
f
has sufficient decay (see
[
45
]
). We can bypass this issue with a mollifier.
For the mollified field
f_{\varepsilon}=\widehat{Z_{\varepsilon}}

of
section

5.6.2
, both
f_{\varepsilon}
and
:\!f_{\varepsilon}^{2}\!:

are smooth functions of rapid decay (since
Z_{\varepsilon}
has
compactly supported spectral density), so the identity

(5.55)

\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}\int\differential\eta\,:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f_{\varepsilon}|^{2}}\!:=:\!{f_{\varepsilon}(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:

holds pointwise, by Plancherel in
\eta
followed by
the Radon inversion formula
eq.

5.26

applied to the Schwartz function
:\!f_{\varepsilon}(R\cdot)^{2}\!:
.
The conclusion follows by passing to the limit

\varepsilon\to 0
: the left-hand side converges in

\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
theorem

5.6.9
(mollification
convergence), and the right-hand side converges because

:\!f_{\varepsilon}^{2}\!:\to:\!f^{2}\!:
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

by
proposition

5.6.6
.
∎

Corollary 5.7.3
.

Let the setting of
theorem

5.7.1
prevail. Assume in addition that
\rho(\xi)=\rho(|\xi|)
in both cases. The following limits hold in law in the sense of random tempered distributions in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

(5.56)

\begin{gathered}R^{\frac{d_{i}}{2}}:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:\xrightarrow[R\to\infty]{\ \mathrm{law}-{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\ }\sqrt{\omega(\rho)}c_{i,d}\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}=\mathrm{FGF}_{\frac{1}{2}},\quad&\text{in case (1)},\\
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},\quad&\text{in case (2)},\end{cases}\end{gathered}

where
d_{i}
depends on the case (i) under consideration:
d_{1}=d-1
and
d_{2}=d
, referring to
theorem

5.7.1
. The constants
c_{1,d}
and
c_{2,d}
can be deduced explicitly from
theorem

5.7.1
and from the constant in
theorem

5.3.5
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to compute the covariance in (1) and (2) in the case when
\Phi
does not depend on
\eta
.
∎

Remark 5.7.4
.

From
theorem

5.7.1
one can immediately deduce other more general variants of
corollary

5.7.3
, including: the case of non-isotropic fields, i.e., more general
\rho
; as well as the analogous theorem for derivatives: Let
P
denote a real polynomial
P(x)=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}c_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}
(the sum being finite) and define
f^{\prime}:=P(\nabla)f:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}c_{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}f
. Then,
f^{\prime}
satisfies the hypotheses of
theorem

5.7.1
with
\rho^{\prime}(\xi)=P(-i\xi)\rho(\xi)
. Consider now a finite family
(P_{k})_{k}
of polynomials such that
\sum_{k}P_{k}(-i\xi)^{2}\rho(\xi)=q(|\xi|)^{2}
and assume
\rho(\xi)=\rho(|\xi|)
; then, by summing over
k
, one can show that

(5.57)

\begin{gathered}R^{\frac{d_{i}}{2}}:\!{\sum_{k}\left((P_{k}(\nabla)f)(R\cdot)\right)^{2}}\!:\xrightarrow[R\to\infty]{\ \mathrm{law}-{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\ }\sqrt{\omega\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}\right)}c_{i,d}\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}=\mathrm{FGF}_{\frac{1}{2}},\quad&\text{in case (1)},\\
\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}},\quad&\text{in case (2)},\end{cases}\end{gathered}

Such a description includes any finite linear combination of the random fields appearing in
theorem

2.4.1
: indeed, there exist polynomials
P_{k,j}
(homogeneous, of degree
j
) such that

(5.58)

\|\nabla^{j}f\|^{2}-\mathbb{E}\left[\|\nabla^{j}f\|^{2}\right]=:\!{\sum_{i}\left(P_{k,j}(\nabla)f\right)^{2}}\!:,

for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. The condition
\sum_{k}c_{k}\neq 0
in
theorem

2.4.1
corresponds to
q\neq 0
. The conclusion follows from the same proof as that of
theorem

5.7.1
applied with spectral density
\tilde{\rho}(\xi)=\sum_{k}|P_{k}(i\xi)|^{2}\cdot\rho(\xi)=q(|\xi|)
, after an additional covariance computation.

5.7.4.
Line space convergence

Remark 5.7.5
.

The operator
E_{d}(\psi)
evoked in the statement of
theorem

5.7.1
shows how the frequency concentrates in the limit. It admits the following alternative formula:

(5.59)

E_{d}(\psi)(r,u)=e_{d}\rho(u)\begin{cases}\int_{-1}^{1}\differential\eta\ \psi(r,u,\eta)\left(1-\eta^{2}\right)^{\frac{d-4}{2}},\quad&\text{if $d\geq 3$,}\\
\psi(r,u,1)+\psi(r,u,-1),\quad&\text{if $d=2$.}\end{cases}

Here
e_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
is a dimensional constant. We can observe that if
d=2,3
, the limit exhibits a singularity at
|\eta|=1
; for
d=4
, the frequency is uniformly distributed in the interval
[-1,1]
; for
d\geq 5
the function
\eta\mapsto(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta)
is continuous and reach its maximum value at
\eta=0
.

Remark 5.7.6
.

One can see the operator
E_{d}
appear naturally, when computing the X-ray-Fourier transform of an eigenfunction of
\Delta
of the form
h=\widehat{s\sigma}_{d}
, where
\sigma_{d}
denotes the uniform measure on
S^{d-1}
and
s
a smooth symmetric function on
S^{d-1}
, as in the setting of
lemma

4.1.1
. Let
\ell=r+\mathbb{R}u\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
be a line, where
r\in u^{\perp}
,
r\neq 0
and
u\in S^{d-1}
. The slice theorem
theorem

5.3.1
gives:

(5.60)

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{X}_{R}h(r,u,\eta)=\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}\left(h(Rr+tu)\right)=c_{d}\int_{S^{d-1}}\sigma_{d}(\differential\xi)s(\xi)e^{i\langle{\xi,rR}\rangle}\delta(\eta-\langle{u,\xi}\rangle)\\
=c_{d}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta)(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\int_{S(u^{\perp})}\sigma_{d-1}(\differential v)s(v)e^{i\langle{\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}v,rR}\rangle}\\
=c_{d}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta)(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\widehat{s\sigma_{d-1}}(R\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}r)\\
\sim c_{d}(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{4}}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta)(\|r\|R)^{-\frac{d-2}{2}}\cos\left(R\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}\|r\|-\pi\frac{d-2}{4}\right)s\left(\frac{r}{\|r\|}\right)\end{gathered}

In the first step, we used that
\mathrm{Jac}\left(\xi\mapsto\eta-\langle{u,\xi}\rangle\right)=\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}
; in the approximation step, as
R\to+\infty
, we used
lemma

4.1.1
, in the space
u^{\perp}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
. Indeed, the formula depends on
u
, through the constraint
r\in u^{\perp}
. The constant
c_{d}
changes from line to line. Therefore,

(5.61)

|\mathcal{X}_{R}h(r,u,\eta)|^{2}\sim\begin{cases}c_{d}(\|r\|R)^{-d+2}s\left(\frac{r}{\|r\|}\right)\cdot(1-\eta^{2})^{\frac{d-4}{2}}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta),&\text{ if $d=2$;}\\
c_{2}\log(R\|r\|)s\left(\frac{r}{\|r\|}\right)\cdot\delta(|\eta|-1),&\text{ if $d\geq 3$;}\end{cases}

as
R\to+\infty
, in the sense of
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, as a distribution in
\eta
, for any fixed line
r,u
. Here, we used that, if
d\geq 3
then
\cos^{2}(R\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}\|r\|)\to 1/2
in a distributional sense, by Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma. In dimension
d=2
, such a limit does not hold anymore because the function
(1-\eta^{2})^{-1}
is not integrable near
\eta=\pm 1
(since
(d-4)/2>-1
if and only if
d>2
). Such a non-integrable singularity at
\pm 1
is responsible for the localization, happening in the two-dimensional case. The delta limit in
eq.

5.61
, easily follows from a direct analysis of the integral
\int_{-1}^{1}{\cos^{2}(R\|r\|\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}})}{(1-\eta^{2})}^{-1}\tau(\eta)\differential\eta
, for an arbitrary Schwartz test function
\tau
, via the change of variable
t=R\|r\|\sqrt{1-\eta^{2}}
. We notice that the product form of the quantity appearing on the right-hand side of
eq.

5.61
, separating the roles of the variables
r
and
\eta
, is consistent with the factorized structure of the noise emerging in
eq.

5.53
.

Remark 5.7.7
.

The convergence in law at
eq.

5.53
descends from a convergence in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
:

(5.62)

R^{\frac{d_{i}}{2}}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\;\xrightarrow{\;\rm law\;}\;\Xi\qquad\text{in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d}),

where, in case i=(1),
\Xi\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
is the centered Gaussian with covariance

(5.63)

\mathbb{E}[|\Xi(\psi)|^{2}]=\frac{2}{2^{d-1}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential v\int_{v^{\perp}}\differential s\;\rho(v)^{2}\left|h_{\psi}(s+v,s-v)\right|^{2}.

The Gaussianity and tightness follow from the same Schur test argument as in the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
(3), which works for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
(not only
\psi=\mathcal{R}\Phi
), see
remark

6.4.1
. On the subclass
\psi=\mathcal{R}\Phi
, the covariance simplifies via
lemma

6.3.1
to the expression in
theorem

5.7.1
.

The range of
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
is
not
dense in
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
(it is constrained by the Helgason–John range conditions, see
[
45
, Ch. I, §6]
). In particular,
\mathcal{R}^{*}
is not injective on
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, so the lift from
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
does not follow formally from the
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
result. This issue is present in all dimension
d\geq 2
.

We stress that indeed the covariance of
\Xi
has a more involved formula than that of
theorem

5.7.1
, for general test functions
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, for all
d\geq 3
. However, in the two-dimensional case it is no longer the case and we have the following strengthening of
theorem

5.7.1
.

Theorem 5.7.8

(Two-dimensional case)
.

Let
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
. Assume that
\omega(\differential\xi)=\rho(\xi)\sigma(\differential\xi)
is supported on
S^{1}
and is absolutely continuous with respect to the uniform circular measure
\sigma
, and assume
\rho
is bounded and almost everywhere continuous. Then,

(5.64)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}\delta(|\eta|-1),\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]\\
=\alpha_{d}\pi\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\differential(u,r)\left(\rho(u)(\psi(r,u,1)+\psi(r,u,-1)\right)^{2}.\end{gathered}

and the limit holds in law as random tempered distributions in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
:

(5.65)

\begin{gathered}R^{\frac{1}{2}}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\xrightarrow[R\to\infty]{\ \mathrm{law}-{\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}\ }(\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}})\delta(|\eta|-1),\end{gathered}

where
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{A}_{2}}
is the white noise with respect to the measure
\rho(u)^{2}\mu(\differential(r,u))
on
\mathbb{A}_{2}
.

The proof of
theorem

5.7.1
above, is deferred to the end of
section

6
, after
remark

6.4.1
.

5.7.5.
Other types of spectral measures

Following the steps of the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
, in particular from
lemma

6.1.3
, it is not hard to show that a distributional limit, as the one stated in
theorem

5.7.1
, exists for a variety of more general spectral measures. The following two examples show how the full distributional limit of
\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:
retains both the oscillatory content of
f
, and the spatial part, that is, the limit of
:\!{f(R\cdot)}\!:
.

Example 5.7.9
.

Consider a Gaussian field on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, of the form
f(x,y)=\cos(\lambda x)\gamma_{1}(y)+\sin(\lambda x)\gamma_{2}(y)
, where
\lambda>0
and
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are two independent copies of a stationary Gaussian field with spectral measure
\omega(\differential y)=\rho(|y|)dy
, e.g. the Annulus field, or Bargmann-Fock field. Starting from
lemma

6.1.3
and proceeding analogously as in the rest of the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
, we find that the limit is of the form

(5.66)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R^{d-1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d-2}}\circ F_{\lambda},\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]\\
=c\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\differential s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}\differential z\left|\rho(|z|)\int_{S^{d-2}}\psi\left((0,s),(1,0),\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+z_{1}^{2}}\theta_{1}\right)\differential\theta\right|^{2},\end{gathered}

for an operator
\psi\mapsto F_{\lambda}(\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
, similar to that of
theorem

5.7.1
(2):
F_{\lambda}(\psi)(s,z)=\sqrt{c}\int_{S^{d-2}}\psi\left((0,s),(1,0),\sqrt{\lambda^{2}+z_{1}^{2}}\theta_{1}\right)\differential\theta
; and where
\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d-2}}
is a white noise on
\mathbb{R}^{2d-2}
, with respect to the measure
\rho(|z|)^{2}\differential s\differential z
, Note that, from
F_{\lambda}
we can recover the fixed frequency
\lambda
in the
x
direction. Moreover,
\left(\int\differential\eta\rho\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{2d-2}}\circ F_{\lambda}\right)(x,y)
has the same law as a white noise
c\mathbb{W}_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}(y)
in the variable
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}
, constant in the
x
direction.

Example 5.7.10
.

A second noteworthy example is the case of a discrete spectral measure
\omega(\differential\xi)=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\delta(\xi-\lambda)\differential\xi
, with
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
being a finite symmetric subset. Such spectral measure corresponds to a field of the form
f(x)=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\gamma_{\lambda}\prod_{i=1}^{d}\cos(\lambda_ix_i)
, where
(\gamma_{\lambda})_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
are independent real Gaussian variables of same
\sigma
. Such a field enjoys clear oscillatory patterns.
From
lemma

6.1.3
, we can easily compute the full limit covariance as

(5.67)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\gamma_{\lambda}\delta(\eta+\langle{\lambda,u}\rangle),\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]\\
=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\left|\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\differential(u,r)\psi_{\text{sym}}(r,u,\langle{\lambda,u}\rangle)\right|^{2}\end{gathered}

The limit field is thus the Gaussian distribution
G=\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\gamma_{\lambda}\delta(\eta+\langle{\lambda,u}\rangle)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, with spatial projection:
\int\differential\eta G=\gamma_{\Lambda}
for some single Gaussian variable
\gamma_{\Lambda}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\mathrm{Card}(\Lambda))
.

5.8.
Heuristic interpretation of
theorem

5.7.1

Let
c_{R}(t)
be a function with the property that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta|\hat{c_{R}}(\cdot)|^{2}\tau(\eta)\to E_{d}(\tau)
for any
\tau\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R})
. The choice of
c_{R}
is not unique. When
d\geq 3
, one can take
c_{R}=c
, where

(5.68)

\hat{c}(\eta)=\left(1-\eta^{2}\right)^{\frac{d-4}{4}}1_{[-1,1]}(\eta),

while if
d=2
, since
|\hat{c}(\eta)|^{2}=\delta(|\eta|-1)
we need to approximate with a mollifier
\rho(\cdot R)R\to\delta
:

(5.69)

c_{R}(t)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sqrt{\rho((|\eta|-1)R)R}e^{it\eta}\differential\eta\approx\frac{\cos(t)}{\sqrt{R}}.

On each line on which
f
concentrates, the oscillation profile
f|_{\ell}
should be a function
c_{R}
of such form.
Such a claim is based on the next heuristic discussion.

We can observe the following. Take
\Phi(r,u,\eta)=\varphi(r,u)\tau(\eta)
in
theorem

5.7.1
, and
\varphi
in
corollary

5.7.3
. We obtain that the next two objects have the same (non-zero) limit in law:

(5.70)

\langle R^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\varphi\tau\rangle\approx\langle R^{\frac{d-1}{2}}:\!{f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:,\varphi\rangle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\differential\eta|\hat{c_{R}}(\eta)|^{2}\tau(\eta)

Recall that
\mathcal{X}_{R}f(r,u,\eta)=\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}(f(Rr+tu)))
, therefore, since the above identity holds for all test functions
\varphi,\tau
, we can
heuristically
deduce that

(5.71)

:\!{\left|\mathcal{F}_{t\to\eta}(f(Rr+tu)))\right|^{2}}\!:=o\left(R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}\right)+\left(:\!{\mathcal{R}f(R\cdot)^{2}}\!:\right)(r,u)|\hat{c_{R}}(\eta)|^{2},\quad\forall r,u,\eta

where we used
eq.

5.26
to
heuristically
infer that
\mathcal{R}^{*}\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}=\mathcal{R}^{-1}
(such an identity would require a thorough justification). At this point we can observe that for any fixed
(r,u)
the above says that two functions
a,c
of
\eta
satisfy
|\hat{a}(\eta)|^{2}=|\hat{c}(\eta)|^{2}
for all
\eta
.

When
d=2
, one has
|\hat{c}(\eta)|^{2}=\delta(|\eta|-1)
,
so that any real function
a
satisfying
|\hat{a}|^{2}=|\hat{c}|^{2}

must be of the form
a(t)=\cos(t - t_0)
for some
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus, for any fixed
(r,u)
, corresponding to a line
\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{d}
, we deduce that there exist a
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, and an amplitude
A>0
, both depending on
(f,R,r,u)
such that
f(Rr+tu)=A\cos(t-t_0)
. In other words, for all
\ell\in\mathbb{A}_{2}
, we have

(5.72)

f|_{R\ell}\approx\cos,\quad\text{up to a translation and a scalar factor, depending on $R,\ell,f$.}

This seems to suggest that there exist random fields
T_{R}(x)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}\mathbb{R}
and
U_{R}(x)\mathchoice{\raisebox{-1.50696pt}{$\displaystyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.5mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\displaystyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\mkern 1.0mu\raisebox{-1.1625pt}{$\textstyle{{}^{\subset}}$}\mkern-11.7mu\raisebox{1.93747pt}{$\textstyle{{}_{\subset}}$}}{\raisebox{1.50696pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-14.0mu\raisebox{-0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle\subset$}}{\raisebox{1.29167pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}\mkern-13.5mu\raisebox{-0.43057pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\subset$}}S^{d-1}
such that

(5.73)

:\!{f\left(R\left(x+(t-T_{R}(x))U_{R}(x))\right)\right)f(Rx)}\!:=c_{2}\cos(Rt)\mathrm{FGF}_{1/2}(x)+o\left(R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}}\right),

in the sense of convergence in law in the space
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for some
c_{2}>0
. Notice that indeed
2\cos(Rt)^{2}=1-\cos(2Rt)\to 1
in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
, by the Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma.

When
d\geq 3
, there is not a unique solution
a
to the
phase retrieval

problem:
|\hat{a}(\eta)|^{2}=|\hat{c}(\eta)|^{2}
, since
|\hat{c}|^{2}
is not a delta. However, what we can observe in this case is that the oscillation profile of
f|_{R\ell}
, along the enhanced lines, is not a pure cosine, and it is instead a mix of frequencies.

6.
Proof of
theorem

5.7.1

6.1.
Covariance structures at fixed
R

Definition 6.1.1
.

Let us define for any
s,z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
(recall
eq.

5.41
)

(6.1)

\begin{gathered}\langle M(s,z),\psi\rangle:=h_{\psi}(s+z,s-z)=\alpha_{d}\int_{S(s^{\perp})}\mathcal{F}_{r\to 2s}^{u^{\perp}}\ \left(\psi(r,u,-\langle{z,u}\rangle)\right)\differential u.\end{gathered}

Remark 6.1.2
.

Note that since
\psi\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
respects the symmetry at
eq.

5.5
, it follows that
\langle M(-s,z),\psi\rangle=\langle M(s,z),\psi\rangle
.

Lemma 6.1.3
.

The covariance functional of
\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:
is given by the following expression: for any
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, let
2\psi_{\text{sym}}(r,u,\eta):=\psi(r,u,\eta)+\psi(r,u,-\eta)
.

(6.2)

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega^{2}\left(d\left({z+R^{-1}s},{-z+R^{-1}s}\right)\right)\ \left|\langle M(s,z),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}\\
=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega^{2}\left(\differential\left(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\right)\right)\ \left|\langle M\left(R\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2}\right),\frac{\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}}{2}\right),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}.\end{gathered}

(The first formula is meant as a change of variables, in the sense of
section

3.1.4
.)

Proof.

Let

(6.3)

h_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}):=\langle M\left(\frac{\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}}{2},\frac{\xi_{1}-\xi_{2}}{2}\right),\psi_{\text{sym}}(\cdot,\cdot,R^{-1}\cdot)\rangle,

where
M
is as in
eq.

6.1
.
Then, by
theorem

5.6.9
, considering
eq.

5.50
,

(6.4)

\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}Z_{R}^{:2:}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))h_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),

where
Z_{R}=\widehat{f(R\cdot)}
.
We now compute the variance of the right-hand side of such an expression using
lemma

5.6.3
, which is given by

(6.5)

\begin{gathered}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega_{R}^{2}(\differential(\xi_1,\xi_2))\ h_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\overline{h_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}2.\end{gathered}

Recall that the spectral measure is
\omega_{R}=\widehat{K(R\cdot)}
, where
K(x)=\mathbb{E}\left\{f(x)f(0)\right\}
. Therefore, it scales according to the rule:

(6.6)

\omega_{R}(\differential\xi)=\omega(R^{-1}\differential\xi)R^{-d}=\omega(\differential(R^{-1}\xi)).

Applying the Schwartz change of variables,
(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\Xi_{R}(r,s)=(s+Rz,s-Rz)
(see
section

3.1.4
), one infers that the expression in (
6.5
) equals

(6.7)

\begin{gathered}2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega^{2}\left(\differential R^{-1}\left({s+Rz},{s-Rz}\right)\right)\ \left|\langle M(s,Rz),\psi_{\text{sym}}(\cdot,\cdot,R^{-1}\cdot)\rangle\right|^{2}\\
=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega^{2}\left(\differential\left({z+R^{-1}s},{-z+R^{-1}s}\right)\right)\ \left|\langle M(s,z),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2},\end{gathered}

which coincides with the formula in the statement.
∎

6.2.
The limit covariance exists

Proof of
theorem

5.7.1
– Point
(1)
.

Let
N,N^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be
k
dimensional submanifolds and let
Y\colon N\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be smooth and injective and such that
Y(N)=N^{\prime}\cup D
, where
D
has zero
k
-dimensional Hausdorff measure. Let
\delta_{N}
denote the measure of integration over
N
. The coarea formula implies that

(6.8)

\delta_{N^{\prime}}(Y(\differential x))=\delta_{N}(\differential x){\mathrm{Jac}(Y)}.

Consider the map
Y_{R}\colon B_{R}(TS^{d-1})\to S^{d-1}\times S^{d-1}
such that

(6.9)

\displaystyle Y_{R}(v,s):

\displaystyle=\left(v\sqrt{1-|s/R|^{2}}+s/R,-v\sqrt{1-|s/R|^{2}}+s/R\right),\quad\forall v\in S^{d-1},\ s\in v^{\perp},|s|<R

\displaystyle=\left(\exp_{v}\left(\frac{s}{R}\frac{\arcsin{\frac{|s|}R}}{|s|}\right),\exp_{-v}\left(\frac{s}{R}\frac{\arcsin{\frac{|s|}R}}{|s|}\right)\right).

Y_{R}
is smooth and injective, since
s\mapsto s|s|^{-1}\arcsin{|s|}
and the exponential map
\exp_{v}
of
S^{d-1}
are.
Observe that
Y_{R}(v,s)=\Xi_{R}(s,v\sqrt{1-|s/R|^{2}})
, where
\Xi_{R}(s,z)=(z+R^{-1}s,-z+R^{-1}s)
.
The above formula applies to the map
Y_{R}
from
N=B_{R}(TS^{d-1})
(the set of all tangent vectors or length less than
R
) to
N^{\prime}=S^{d-1}\times S^{d-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2d}
, with
D
being the diagonal. Let
\rho^{\otimes 2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\rho(\xi_{1})\rho(\xi_{2})
. We obtain

(6.10)

\begin{gathered}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=2\int\delta_{N}(\differential(\Xi_R(s,z)))\rho^{\otimes 2}(\Xi_{R}(s,z))\ \left|\langle M(s,z),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}\\
2\int\delta_{N}(\differential(Y_R(s,z)))\rho^{\otimes 2}(Y_{R}(s,z))\ \left|\langle M(s,v\sqrt{1-|s/R|^{2}}),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}\\
2\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential v\int_{B_{R}(v^{\perp})}\differential s\ {\mathrm{Jac}(Y_{R})(v,s)}\ \rho^{\otimes 2}(Y_{R}(s,z))\ \left|\langle M(s,v\sqrt{1-|s/R|^{2}}),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}.\end{gathered}

The Jacobian of
Y_{R}
is computed in
lemma

6.2.2
below, and is
\mathrm{Jac}Y_{R}(v,s)=\left(\frac{R}{2}\right)^{1-d}\left(1-\frac{|s|^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
. Therefore, we conclude that passing to the limit
R\to+\infty
, we get:

(6.11)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R^{d-1}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\frac{2}{2^{d-1}}\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential v\int_{v^{\perp}}\differential s\ \rho(v)^{2}\left|\langle M(s,v),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}\end{gathered}

We conclude by
lemma

6.3.1
.
∎

Proof of
theorem

5.7.1
, Point
(2)
.

In this case, we can use directly that
\omega^{2}(\differential(\Xi_R(s,z)))=\rho^{\otimes 2}\circ\Xi_{R}\mathrm{Jac}(\Xi_{R})\differential s\differential z
, and easily compute
\mathrm{Jac}(\Xi_{R})=R^{-d}2^{d}
, to conclude:

(6.12)

\begin{gathered}\lim_{R\to+\infty}R^{d}\mathbb{E}\left[|\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:\ ,\psi\rangle|^{2}\right]=\frac{2}{2^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\differential s\differential z\ \rho(z)^{2}\ \left|\langle M(s,z),\psi_{\text{sym}}\rangle\right|^{2}.\end{gathered}

We conclude by
lemma

6.3.1
.
∎

Remark 6.2.1
.

In the previous proofs, we used the assumption that
\rho
is almost everywhere continuous to infer the a.s. limits:
\rho^{\otimes 2}\circ Y_{R}(v,s)\to\rho(v)^{2}
and
\rho^{\otimes 2}\circ\Xi_{R}(z,s)\to\rho(z)^{2}
.

Lemma 6.2.2
.

One has that

\mathrm{Jac}\,Y_{R}(v,s)=\left(\frac{R}{2}\right)^{1-d}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{|s|^{2}}{R^{2}}}\right)^{d-3}
.

Proof.

To compute the Jacobian in the statement, we evaluate the volume of the image of an orthonormal basis. Choose orthonormal bases adapted so that

(6.13)

\begin{gathered}v=e_{1},\qquad s=s_{2}e_{2},\quad\text{then}\\
T:=T_{(v,s)}TS^{d-1}=\mathrm{span}\left\{(e_{2},-|s|e_{1}),(e_{3},0),\dots,(e_{d},0),(0,e_{2}),\dots,(0,e_{d})\right\}.\end{gathered}

The matrix representing
DY_{R}\colon T\to\mathbb{R}^{2d}
in such a frame is

(6.14)

DY_{R}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{s_{2}}{R}&0^{T}&-\frac{s_{2}}{R^{2}\alpha}&0^{T}\\[6.0pt]
\alpha\,e_{2}^{T}&\alpha\,\mathbbm{1}_{d-2}&\frac{1}{R}\,e_{2}^{T}&\frac{1}{R}\,\mathbbm{1}_{d-2}\\[6.0pt]
-\frac{s_{2}}{R}&0^{T}&\frac{s_{2}}{R^{2}\alpha}&0^{T}\\[6.0pt]
-\alpha\,e_{2}^{T}&-\alpha\,\mathbbm{1}_{d-2}&\frac{1}{R}\,e_{2}^{T}&\frac{1}{R}\,\mathbbm{1}_{d-2}\end{pmatrix}

Computing the Jacobian of this matrix gives the result.
∎

6.3.
Identifying the limit covariance

Lemma 6.3.1

(Plancherel-like formula)
.

For all
\Phi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
such that
\Phi(x,t)=\Phi(x,-t)
, we have:

(6.15)

\begin{gathered}\int_{S^{d-1}}\differential v\int_{v^{\perp}}\differential s\ \rho(v)^{2}\left|\langle M(s,v),\mathcal{R}\Phi\rangle\right|^{2}=\alpha_{d}\pi^{d-1}\int_{\mathbb{A}_{d}}\differential(u,r)\left(\rho(u)\int_{S^{d-2}}\mathcal{R}\Phi(r,u,\theta_{1})\differential\theta\right)^{2};\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\differential s\differential z\ \rho(z)^{2}\ \left|\langle M(s,z),\mathcal{R}\Phi\rangle\right|^{2}=\frac{\alpha_{d}^{2}}{2^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential s\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\differential z\ \left|\rho(z)\int_{S^{d-2}}\mathcal{F}_{r\to s}\left(\Phi(r,{|\Pi_{s^{\perp}}(z)|\theta_{1}})\right)\ \differential\theta\right|^{2}.\end{gathered}

Proof.

The key observation is that the next quantity depends only on
(s,\langle{s,z}\rangle)
. For all
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(6.16)

\begin{gathered}\langle M(s,z),\mathcal{R}\Phi\rangle=h_{\mathcal{R}\Phi}(s+z,s-z)=\alpha_{d}\int_{S(s^{\perp})}\hat{\Phi}(2s,-\langle{z,u}\rangle)\differential u\\
=\alpha_{d}\int_{S^{d-2}}\hat{\Phi}(2s,|\Pi_{s^{\perp}}(z)|\theta_{1})\differential\theta=\alpha_{d}\mathcal{F}_{r\to 2s}^{u^{\perp}}\ \left(\int_{S^{d-2}}\mathcal{R}\Phi(r,u,|\Pi_{s^{\perp}}(z)|\theta_{1})\differential\theta\right),\end{gathered}

the latter identity being due to
eq.

5.22
, valid for any
u\in s^{\perp}
. In the first formula in the statement, since
v\in s^{\perp}
, the spherical integral on the left-hand side is independent of both
v
and
s
. Thus, we can apply Plancherel identity in the variable
s\in v^{\perp}
and obtain the desired formula in the r.h.s. The second formula follows directly from the third identity in
eq.

6.16
.
∎

6.4.
Gaussianity and tightness

Conclusion of the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
: Point
(3)
.

By
[
16
, Corollary 2.4]
(Lévy continuity theorem for random tempered distributions): convergence in law in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
holds if and only if, for every
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, the real-valued random variable
\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle
converges in law. Moreover, the limit characteristic functional is automatically continuous at
0
(since the limit is Gaussian with a continuous covariance form). We will prove that, for every fixed
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
:

(6.17)

F_{R}:=R^{d_{i}/2}\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle\;\xrightarrow{\;\rm law\;}\;\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}(\psi)),

where
\sigma^{2}(\psi)
is the limit covariance computed in
theorem

5.7.1
, points (1) and (2). By
theorem

5.6.9
,
\langle\sqrt{-\Delta_{r}}:\!{|\mathcal{X}_{R}f|^{2}}\!:,\psi\rangle=\alpha_{d}I_{2}(g_{R})
, where
I_{2}
denotes the second Wiener–Itô integral with respect to the isonormal process associated with the spectral measure
\omega
, and
g_{R}\in L^{2}(\omega^{2};\mathbb{C})
is the symmetric kernel

(6.18)

g_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=\int_{S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})}\mathcal{F}_{r\to R(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})}^{u^{\perp}}\left(\psi_{\text{sym}}\left(r,u,\langle{\xi_{2},u}\rangle\right)\right)\,\differential u,\qquad\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

obtained from
eq.

5.41
by evaluating
h_{\psi_{\text{sym}}(\cdot,\cdot,R^{-1}\cdot)}
at
(R\xi_{1},R\xi_{2})
. The isometry property of
I_{2}
gives
\mathbb{E}[F_{R}^{2}]=2\alpha_{d}^{2}R^{d_{i}}\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}^{2}
, and
\mathbb{E}[F_{R}^{2}]\to\sigma^{2}(\psi)
was established above. We now identify a uniform decay estimate for the kernel
g_{R}
. Since
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
, for each
u\in S^{d-1}
the mapping
r\mapsto\psi_{\text{sym}}(r,u,\eta)
is a Schwartz function of
r\in u^{\perp}
, uniformly in
u\in S^{d-1}
and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}
. Standard estimates on the Fourier transform yield: for every
N\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C_{N}>0
(depending on
\psi
and
N
) such that

(6.19)

\left|\mathcal{F}_{r\to\rho}^{u^{\perp}}\left(\psi_{\text{sym}}(r,u,\eta)\right)\right|\leq\frac{C_{N}}{(1+|\rho|)^{N}},\qquad\forall\,u\in S^{d-1},\quad\forall\,\eta\in\mathbb{R}.

Indeed, by integration by parts,
(1+|\rho|)^{N}|\hat{\psi}_{\text{sym}}(\rho,u,\eta)|
is bounded by a sum of
u^{\perp}
-integrals of derivatives
\partial_{r}^{\alpha}\psi_{\text{sym}}(\cdot,u,\eta)
, each of which is finite and bounded uniformly in
(u,\eta)
by the Schwartz decay of
\psi
. Since the sphere
S((\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})^{\perp})
has uniformly bounded volume for all
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}
(including
\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}=0
), we deduce from (
6.18
) that, for every
N>d
,

(6.20)

|g_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|\leq\frac{C^{\prime}_{N}}{(1+R|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|)^{N}},\qquad\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We now verify the Schur-like condition (
4.7
) of
lemma

4.2.1
, which yields the desired asymptotic Gaussianity.

Case
(1)
:

\omega=\rho\,\sigma
on
S^{d-1}
, with
\rho
bounded. Applying the change of coordinates
a_{R}(z,\varepsilon)=\frac{z}{R}+\varepsilon y\sqrt{1-\frac{|z|^{2}}{R^{2}}}
, with
z\in y^{\perp}
,
|z|<R
,
\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}
. We get:

(6.21)

\displaystyle M_{R}

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in S^{d-1}:\ y\in\supp(\omega)}\int_{S^{d-1}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,\rho(a)\sigma(\differential a)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{y\in S^{d-1}}\int_{B_{R}(y^{\perp})}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}}\frac{C^{\prime}_{N}}{(1+|R(y+a_{R}(z,\varepsilon))|)^{N}}\,\rho(a_{R}(z,\varepsilon))\mathrm{Jac}(a_{R})(z,\varepsilon)\,\differential z,

where we have used the bound (
6.20
). The Jacobian is
\mathrm{Jac}(a_{R})(z,\varepsilon)=(1-\frac{|z|^{2}}{R^{2}})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{|z|}{R^{d}}
. As
R\to\infty
, we have that
R(y+a_{R}(z,-1))\to z
and
R(y+a_{R}(z,+1))\approx z+2Ry
. Expanding separately the two terms in
\varepsilon
,

(6.22)

\displaystyle M_{R}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\infty}\sup_{y\in S^{d-1}}\int_{y^{\perp}}\frac{C^{\prime}_{N}}{(1+|z|)^{N}}\frac{|z|}{R^{d}}\,\differential z+\|\rho\|_{\infty}\sup_{y\in S^{d-1}}\int_{y^{\perp}}\frac{C^{\prime}_{N}}{(1+|z+2Ry|)^{N}}\frac{|z|}{R^{d}}\,\differential z

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{d}}+\frac{o(R^{-k})}{R^{d}}=O(R^{-d})=o(R^{-\frac{d-1}{2}})=o\big(\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}\big),

since
d\geq 2
implies
d>(d-1)/2
, and we used the rapid decay (
6.20
) with
N>d
.

Case
(2)
:

\omega=\rho\,\differential\xi
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
\rho
bounded. Using (
6.20
) directly and substituting
b=a+y
:

(6.23)

\displaystyle M_{R}

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,y\in{\rm supp}(\omega)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|g_{R}(y,a)|\,\rho(a)\,\differential a

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{C^{\prime}_{N}}{(1+R|b|)^{N}}\,\differential b=O(R^{-d})=o(R^{-\frac{d}{2}})=o\big(\|g_{R}\|_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}\big),

for
N>d
, completing the verification of (
4.7
) in both cases.
∎

Remark 6.4.1
.

The proof above works for arbitrary
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
: not only for test functions of the form
\psi=\mathcal{R}\Phi
. The key estimate (
6.20
) uses only the rapid decay of the Fourier transform of
\psi
in the variable
r\in u^{\perp}
, which is guaranteed by the Schwartz regularity of
\psi
. For test functions of the form
\psi=\mathcal{R}\Phi
, the kernel
g_{R}
enjoys the stronger decay
|g_{R}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})|\leq C_{N}(1+R|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}|+|\langle\xi_{1},u\rangle|)^{-N}
(since
\hat{\Phi}
is Schwartz in both variables), but this additional decay is not needed for the Schur test.
In particular, the argument justifies the claim in
remark

5.7.7
: the convergence in
eq.

5.53
can be lifted from
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{d})
.

Proof of
theorem

5.7.8
.

In the proof of
theorem

5.7.1
(1), at
eq.

6.11
, we obtain the convergence of the variance in the l.h.s. of
eq.

5.64
. In the two-dimensional case, this formula simplifies easily, for all test functions
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{C}}(\mathcal{A}_{2})
.

(6.24)
